## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide supplementary information in the proposed cross-referenced annexes such as project studies, generic assumptions, term sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (including annexes) should not exceed 30 pages. Proposals exceeding this prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
* Text should be presented in Arial, size 11.
* Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to any confidentiality requirements. Further information pertaining to the IDP: Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

Climate change threatens the lives and livelihoods of over millions in extreme poverty, especially in the sub-Saharan region of Africa. Togo, Senegal and Guinea are all located in the major climate "hotspots" in the region.

They are suffering from increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts and very wet periods which have allowed increased frequency and intensity of extreme events. The frequent and severe droughts resulted in harmful consequences on the economy due to the large dependence on the climate-sensitive agriculture sector, while the heavy rainfall has led to flooding and landslides. These conditions also affect natural resources, difficult access to energy, water resources and quality health care.

The country recorded temperatures increasing by 0.5°C per decade over the last century with a range of temperature variations from -12.8°C to +37.4°C. The mean annual temperature has increased at an average rate of 0.18°C per year, with maximum and minimum daily temperature ranges increasing from 15 to 98 mm.

These climate conditions translate into physical risks manifested by the increase in frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts and very wet periods which have led to flooding and landslides. These conditions also affect natural resources, difficult access to energy, water resources and quality health care.


The Staple Crops Processing Zone (SCPZ) program is specifically designed to help rationalize interventions focused on the staple crops value chains in four regions from the three highly indebted poor countries in Africa.

The project will focus on strengthening the agricultural products supply chain infrastructure; building the resilience of SCPZs agricultural products Supply Value Chain, and programme management and coordination. The programme will invest along the agricultural value chains by improving the productivity of smallholder farmers through the use of improved seeds, fertilizers, and other inputs such as rice, maize, cassava, banana, sesame, horticulture, mango, cashew, coffee, wool, vegetables among others.

The project will also focus on strengthening the infrastructure such as drip irrigation and gravity irrigation and equipped boreholes. At the post-harvest and processing stages of the value chain, the project will invest in the construction of cold storage facilities, energy technologies such as biogas/producer and solar PV for processing, lighting and storage while at the marketing and distribution stage, the project will focus on improving access to markets through Agricultural Transformation Centres (ATCs), offers adequate infrastructure, logistics and specialized facilities and services for improved marketing and competitiveness.

The GCF funds, along with the African Development Bank (AfDB) funds, other program financiers and the respective governments of Togo, Senegal and Guinea will provide the resources needed to address institutional barriers that constrain the effective implementation of an integrated approach to climate adaptation and mitigation. The project will be implemented in four regions: Kara, Kolda, Dogon in Senegal; and Boké and Boke in Guinea.

This will help improve the productivity of smallholder farmers through the use of improved seeds, fertilizers, and other inputs such as rice, maize, cassava, banana, sesame, horticulture, mango, cashew, coffee, wool, vegetables among others. The project will also focus on strengthening the infrastructure such as drip irrigation and gravity irrigation and equipped boreholes.

At the post-harvest and processing stages of the value chain, the project will invest in the construction of cold storage facilities, energy technologies such as biogas/producer and solar PV for processing, lighting and storage while at the marketing and distribution stage, the project will focus on improving access to markets through Agricultural Transformation Centres (ATCs), offers adequate infrastructure, logistics and specialized facilities and services for improved marketing and competitiveness.

These countries have a long history of confronting serious crises. Key human-influenced drivers of climate change include rising temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (floods, droughts), reduced rainfall in some areas, deforestation, agriculture, extended periods of drought, shifting cultivation, mono-cropping, over exploitation of the already vulnerable land and forests resources, and poor water management. These factors have led to soil erosion, desertification, loss of biodiversity, increased vulnerability to contributions to alterations in rainfall patterns and increase the frequency of extreme weather events (floods including marine floods, droughts, heat waves, landslides, etc.), reduced agricultural productivity, increased food insecurity, crop losses, lower productivity levels and even famine and desertification. All these climatic together with non-climatic factors contribute to greater food insecurity and poverty experienced in the countries.

The average rainfall patterns with average annual rainfall ranging from about 600 mm/year to above 2500 mm/year (Figure B.1). Both observations and models agree in terms of reproducing the south drying conditions further north of the Karim region in Togo. On the average, Senegal receives the lowest amount of annual rainfall compared with other countries in the region. The observed rainfall patterns are consistent with the modelled rainfall patterns. The mean pattern of the observed rainfall in conserved highlight the usefulness of the RCMs for investigating the possible climate situation in the future.


---
**Page 13**
---

The major feature of the observations is a wide band of high rainfall, which spans along the Sudanese AEZ in the southern parts of Senegal and across the Guinean coast. The highest precipitation occurs during the heart of the rainy season from July to September. This model is able to simulate most of the precipitation patterns observed over the two AEZs. Also, it captures the rainfall over the coastal areas of Guinea and the inland areas of Guinea, which mainly occurs during JJAS (and JSON), as well represented by the model. However, in general, some discrepancies are noted between the observed and modeled precipitation. The main displacement of the precipitation band northwards from July to September is associated with both the El Niño and Nino remanent modes. SSTs over the tropical Atlantic Ocean are well reproduced by the model (Figure B.1.2). For instance, increasing rainfall was observed at Nantoumbo in Togo. In Kankan, the observed and modeled trends in precipitation diverge slightly, where the observed trends show increase.

Both observed and modeled 2-metre temperature agree with terms of spatial patterns for all seasons in the two AEZs (see Figure B.1.3). The model captures the seasonal cycle, the regional characteristics, and the interannual variability well reproduced by the ENS model. The ENS simulation captures the wet area of cold temperatures, in the coastal areas of Guinea, while the dry area is captured as warm temperature. However, the model overestimates temperatures almost everywhere in the coastal domain. The warm bias of temperature may be contributing to the underestimation of precipitation. The spatial pattern of mean annual surface air temperature (Figure B.1.3) is influenced by the criterion in the model depends on the near-surface air temperatures. The spatial pattern of mean annual surface air temperature over the two AEZs shows a clear gradient from north to south, with minimum and maximum temperature ranges from 14°C in the south of Guinea to about 28°C in the northern region of Senegal.

[2] The data used in this document were retrieved from the archives of the respective meteorological services for selected stations within the SCP26

## Local trends in temperature show a general increase for minimum and maximum temperatures in both station observed and model simulated data (Figure B.1.4). The warming is significant, especially with maximum temperature where the mean annual deviation from the climatological mean is about to 2°C. The observed warming partly contributes to soil degradation through increased evaporation and reduced infiltration. Increased evaporation also increases droughts in the region, affecting livelihoods especially in rural settings of the host SCPZs.

Annual cycle: Figure B.1.5 shows the annual cycle of the observed and simulated precipitation for the SCPZ programme regions. This includes, the Kara Region of Togo; the Casamance Natural region of Senegal; and the Bole and Kankan regions of Guinea.

The annual cycle of 2-metre temperature for Kara Region in Togo, the Casamance Natural region of Senegal; and the Bole and Kankan Regions of Guinea is shown in Figure B.1.6. In all sessions, the RCMS and ENSM simulations approach the CRU observations for all the regions.

The annual cycle of precipitation for most of the regions shows a single cycle of precipitation for most of the regions. The onset of the rainy season as observed by CRU is also well simulated by 2-m temperature in all the regions except Bole, which is better captured by the ENS model. The ENS, in general, captures better the timing and peaks of rainfall in most of the points in the region.

### Seasonal Drought (SD): With rising weather as evident from the analysis of mean annual cycle of 2-metre temperature at the project specific levels, drought occurrence are bound to be more frequent with far reaching implications for the agricultural sector. This is in line with the findings of the IPCC AR5 report on climate change and its impacts, adaptation and mitigation.

The WMO recommends the use of the Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI) and the Standardized Temperature Index (STI). The SPI takes into account both precipitation and temperature while the STI only considers temperature into account, but the SPI does not. The ability to calculate the SPI at various timescales allows for multiple applications in drought monitoring and assessment.

### In Figure 8.1.7, the SPI and SPEI calculated at 3, 6 and 12 months time intervals for Kara region in Togo (1975 - 2005) is presented. As observed, the 6 months and 12 months intervals, indicate that the drought frequency and intensity in Kara region were more pronounced in the early parts of the 80s and 90s than the 21st century. However, more very dry (with SPI and SPEI) drought events with values ≤ -1 where mostly recorded in the later parts of the 21st century, notably years 2000 and 2003.

*World Meteorological Organization (WMO) and Global Water Partnership (GWP), 2016: Handbook of Drought Indices and Monitoring. Geneva: World Meteorology Management Programme (WMP), Integrated Drought Management Tools and Guidelines Series 2, Geneva.*

Also, the SPI and SPEI calculated at 2, 6 and 12 months time intervals for the South Agropole of the Casamance Natural region of Senegal and the Boké and Kankan regions of Guinea (1973 - 2005), are shown in Figure B.1.8. The SPEI captures more drought events than the SPI, especially during the period from 1984 to 2005. Both graphs show similar shape but with different duration and intensity. While SPEI measure shows larger duration and intensity by 1987 to 2000, it was smaller in comparison with the SPI during this period.

The general takeaway from the SPI and SPEI time series for present analysis is that drought is a recurrent drought events in both regions. In all data, that the agricultural and water sectors are the two sectors most affected by drought impacts.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## Projections:
To understand the potential physical risk associated with future climate, projections from downscaled global climate model projections are used to provide a more refined regional and country specific information especially for extremes (e.g., temperature, precipitation) and other variables of interest.

### Change in mean climate: Figure B.1.9 shows projected changes in annual total precipitation averaged for the climate periods 2041-2070 and 2071-2100 under the Representative Concentration Pathways (RCP4.5) emission scenario.
The figure indicates that significant drying and wet conditions are expected in the three countries. For instance, drying conditions are projected to dominate most parts of Burkina Faso, Mali, Niger, and Chad. In Senegal and Togo, a pattern of high and low precipitation is expected in the southern and northern parts, respectively, of the region.

### Climate temperature projections:
Current climate projects a general increase in mean annual temperature for all three countries during near-term periods (current-near, mid- and far future) under RCP4.5 scenario (Figure B.1.10). The projected warming is estimated to be about 2°C in the current-near period, while it is expected to increase further in the mid-and far future.

### Figure B.1.9:
Projected change in mean annual total precipitation (mm) for Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger under RCP4.5 emission scenario: 2041-2070 (mid-future) and 2071-2100 (far future) under the RCP4.5 emission scenario.

### Figure B.1.10:
Projected change in mean annual total precipitation (mm) for Senegal, Togo and Côte d'Ivoire under RCP4.5 emission scenario: 2041-2070 (mid-future) and 2071-2100 (far future).

### Climate temperature projections:
The current and future climate conditions are compared using a two-sided student t-test considering unequal variances at the 0.05 significance level to determine if the mean values are significantly different between the two climates.

Drought projections: In Figure B.1.17 the SPI and SPII calculated at 3-, 6- and 12-months time intervals for Kans, Casamance, and Gambia are presented. As expected, the results are not quite different from the baseline results obtained. The frequency and duration of drought vary between the 6- and 12-months intervals, and between the SPI and the SPII. In general, the SPIII shows a higher frequency than the SPI for all regions. The SPIII also shows an increase in relation to the baseline, the calculated 6- and 12-months drought duration, increases for all the region. This is consistent with the rising temperatures observed over the last decades. These results suggest that investing in agriculture must be accompanied by plans to support water efficient use in the regions. This offers opportunities for the development of more climate resilient agricultural systems through the introduction of heat tolerant varieties for seasonal crops as well as adopting climate-smart agricultural practices for precision farming to ensure food security.


The current and future climate conditions are compared using a two-sided student t-test considering unequal variances at 0.05 significance level to determine if the mean values are significantly different between the two climates.

Consecutive Dry Days (CDD): Another useful indicator for assessing climate change impacts on agriculture beside changes in temperature and precipitation, is the maximum number of consecutive dry days (CDD). This is defined as a day without any rainfall. CDD has an important impact on rain-fed agriculture as it directly impacts soil moisture, and thus crop growth. CDD in the three host countries is expected to increase over time due to climate change. The increase is significant in the north-eastern parts of Burkina Faso. An eastward pattern of significant increase in CDD is possible in the north-west region of Senegal. A significant increase in CDD (at least 11 days) is likely around the eastern part of Togo.

<https://climatelegendsportal.worldbank.org/>


---
**Page 21**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 14

**Zingaomoh:** While southern parts of Togo are expected to experience a decrease in CDD, the northern parts including the Kara region will possibly have to cope with increasing CDD. Higher number of CDD with little or no precipitation can lead to water stress and drought conditions thereby, adding more stress to limited water resources, with far-reaching implications for irrigation and other water uses.

Figure B.1.13 shows the historic and projected change in consecutive dry days (CDD) across the Casamance Region of Senegal, and the northern parts of Togo from 2011-2040 to the period 1981 to 2056. The black line indicates the average CDD for each year during the period 1981 to 2056. In addition, average trends derived from the emission scenarios RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5. In addition, an average of more than 30 consecutive dry days is projected to occur in the northern parts of Togo by 2071-2100. However, the effects are more pronounced in the Casamance Region where a CDD of over 60 is projected that the CDD could reach up to 200 days per year by 2041-2070. This means more drought conditions with implications for food security.

Figure B.1.13: Projected change in consecutive dry days (CDD) (days) relative to a baseline (1976–2005) over the periods 2011–2040 (current near future), 2041-2070 (mid-future) and 2071-2100 (far future) under the RCP4.5 emission scenario. The color scale indicates areas where the change is statistically significant at 0.05 significance level.

### Figure 6.13: Historical and projected maximum number of consecutive dry days (CDD) in the Kara Region of Togo, Casamance Region of Senegal, and the Boké and Kankan Regions of Guinea for the period 1981 to 2056 The black line indicates historical while the blue and red lines represent trends derived from RCP45 and RCP85.

#### Length of Growing Period (LGP): The length of growing period (LGP) is defined as the period during the year when average temperatures are greater than or equal to 9°C (Tmean≥9°C) and precipitation plus moisture stored in the soil exceed half the potential evapotranspiration (PET). LGP is a critical parameter for crop growth, development, and yield. It is also used by many agricultural programmes in the midst of changing climatic conditions. A normal growing period is defined as one when there is an excess of precipitation over PET during most parts of the year. An intermediate growing period is defined as one which precipitation does not normally exceed PET but does for part of the year. No growing period is when temperatures are not conducive to crop growth or if never accessible.

*CLIMPAg: Climate Impact on Agriculture / ADVICE AND WARNINGS! Agrometeorological Crop Forecasting (upi.org)*

## Crop Water Requirements (CWR): Implications on crop water requirement of crops in what for irrigation water planning in the future, in particular when considering climate change and its variability

The need of a crop is usually expressed in mm/day, mm/month or mm/season. Based on the different climatic variables of the reference period, the CWR can be calculated as a function of the reference evapotranspiration (ET0) for each crop. The standardization recommended by FAO27 can be used to calculate the reference crop evapotranspiration ET0 (daily water needs of the reference crop). The reference crop is usually a grass with a height of 10 cm and a leaf area index of 0.85, which grows under climatic conditions. For instance, a certain maize variety grown in a cool climate will need less water per day than the same maize variety grown in a hot climate.

With the standardized reference crop evapotranspiration ET0 computed, calculations of the crop water requirement can be made using the standardization recommended by FAO27 (1965) for the calculation of the reference crop evapotranspiration. ETC. For more details on this approach, see pages 103-114 of the programme feasibility study report (PS).

In Figures B.1.15a to B.1.15c, we present the compound differences between the approximate value (in mm per total season) of crop water requirement for each CWR scenario and the reference value (i.e., the average value in the reference period). The results are conditioned: Semidry for Togo, Humid and Semi-Arid for Senegal, and Humid/Sub-humid for Guinea. Two categories of crops were considered: drought-tolerant crops with longer growing days, denoted by SDCG; and drought-sensitive crops with shorter growing days, denoted by SDSC. For each crop, the approximate crop water need (CWR) in mm per total season is calculated using the reference crop evapotranspiration ET0 computed for each scenario (RCP2.6, RCP4.5, RCP6.0, RCP8.5), using the RCP 4.5 scenario. The figures presented below show the approximate crop water requirement in mm per total season.

The major feature of the observations is a wide disparity in the approximate crop water requirement between the CWR crops and the reference crops for each country. This difference can be very large, especially when the CWR crops are more drought-tolerant than the reference crops. The differences are more intense for Senegal and Togo than the other countries. Another interesting feature of the observations is that, in these countries, the approximate crop water requirement (CWR) is higher than the reference crop evapotranspiration ET0, suggesting that the deployment of crops with LCG in these countries would not be profitable in terms of water needs.

[http://www.iiasa.ac.at/Research/GCF/Papers/2014/gcf14004.html](http://www.iiasa.ac.at/Research/GCF/Papers/2014/gcf14004.html)

*Note: The CWR is the "crop water coefficient"; varies according to the growth stage of a given crop, seasonal average has been widely used.

### Key Sectors at Risk to Climate Change

#### Senegal
- **Agriculture**: Highly vulnerable to environmental shocks given the recurring incidences of natural disasters, such as floods and droughts that threaten the national stability maintained over time. Such type of destabilizing events will increase in magnitude and frequency under climate change.
- **Water resources**: The country is highly dependent on rainfall for its water supply. For instance, the prolonged droughts recorded between 1970 and 2000 caused the rural urban migration experienced in the country. This trend has continued to date with a significant increase in the number of people migrating from rural areas during October and December. This led to a corresponding increase in hot nights (27 days per year) since 1960. Although rains have increased over time, they are becoming more erratic and less predictable.
- **Energy**: The country is highly dependent on rainfall for its water supply. For instance, the prolonged droughts recorded between 1970 and 2000 caused the rural urban migration experienced in the country. This trend has continued to date with a significant increase in the number of people migrating from rural areas during October and December. This led to a corresponding increase in hot nights (27 days per year) since 1960. Although rains have increased over time, they are becoming more erratic and less predictable.
- **Socioeconomic impacts**: The host country. This gives a useful insight into the most vulnerable areas through the spatial distribution of these indicators.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 24

## Guinea

The climate of Guinea is characterized by highly variable rainfall. Decrease in rainfall over Guinea has offset income flow, influencing agricultural calendar and altered river regimes. The country also reports droughts, which is expected to be the highest frequency in West Africa. Droughts are projected to increase in intensity and duration, leading to reduced water availability for surface runoff. Also, droughts are projected to contribute to loss of biodiversity, degraded headwaters, increased the proliferation of invasive species, and desertification. These events have significant implications on the main economic activity of 80% of Guineans as well as agricultural production and food security. Flood events also occur frequently in Guinea and negatively impact the already poor sewage and water systems as well as sanitation infrastructure. The frequency and intensity of these events are projected to increase, leading to increased flooding, fever, malaria, and/or polio. Sea level rise has been observed along Guinea's coast and will cause increased salinization and erosion of coastal areas, which in turn will negatively impact the livelihoods of communities living close to the coast, ecosystems, and proliferation of diseases.

The country is committed to invest in improved water management, early warning systems, and salt-tolerant crop varieties to reduce risks in Guinea which are associated with sea level rise. This will further help to alleviate water stress and improve food security for Guineans. The country has also committed to increase the use of agroforestry practices in agriculture, introduction of new crop species, and other likely interventions to decrease the country's populations and vulnerability to climate change.

## Togo

Togo being a low income country with high poverty will see further exposure unaided handling from climate risk and associated impacts. The precipitation regime in this region significantly vary on inter-annual and inter-decadal time scales, partly due to variations in the movements and intensity of the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), and variations in timing and intensity of the West African Monsoon. Over the past 50 years, there has been a significant increase in temperature with an average increase rate of 2.4°C per decade. Hot days have also increased to have increased by 13.5% while cold nights decrease since 1960. This has significantly impacted on livelihoods, human and animal health and natural resources. Variable precipitation patterns have led to a significant reduction in the reliability of rainfall leading to reduced crop yields and food insecurity for communities to the intensity of extreme events, coastal storms, and natural hazards such as heat waves, droughts, floods, and landslides.

Climate models project a rising temperatures, changing seasonal rainfall pattern, increased duration of dry spells, and increased frequency of extreme weather events. These changes will have significant impacts on agriculture, water resources, human health, and ecosystems in Togo.

As highlighted in Togo's adaptation action for the Agriculture Forests and Other Land Use (AFOLU) sector, there is the need to increase the resilience of livelihoods through improved access to climate information services, strengthening the resilience of crops, livestock and forests through breeding of crops and livestock for resistance to pests and diseases, increasing the use of drought tolerant crop varieties, and improving the availability of water resources and spaces for wood energy; and) and sustainable land management including the development of a national monitoring program for soil erosion and degradation. The country has committed to invest in improved water management, early warning systems, and salt-tolerant crop varieties to reduce risks in Togo which are associated with sea level rise. This will further help to alleviate water stress and improve food security for Guineans.

The country is also committed to the construction and/or improvement of reservoirs for multi-irrigation and livestock watering in rural areas throughout all regions, as well as the development of a national program on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. The country has committed to the Climate-Smart Agriculture process outlined in the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

## Summary of the implementation of the agricultural policy laid out by ECONAS and in the National Policy for the Agricultural Development of Togo 2013-2022

Based on these literature review and national publications, the proposed SCPZ programme interventions align with the adaptation strategies outlined.

### Baseline Situation:
Today, more frequent and prolonged droughts, shriveling of the crop growing season due to late onset and early cessation, shift in crops calendar, increasing demand for more water in agriculture, and poor yields from traditional crops are some of the challenges facing smallholder farmers in Togo. In addition to this, pandemic and disease outbreaks, such as the COVID-19 or the previous Ebola crisis not only pose threats to human health but also have severe impacts on the already vulnerable economies in the region and undermine efforts to strengthen the resilience of these communities.

This makes smallholder agriculture more vulnerable than ever. Without adequate measures to strengthen and improve the entire value chain, this vulnerability will continue to increase, pushing smallholders into poverty further, particularly women farmers, who are often the most affected by climate change. The unanticipated shock of COVID-19 underscores the need for a shift from “business as usual” practices to a more resilient approach in agriculture.

### Integrated Approach:
The integrated approach is paramount as agriculture has been identified as the number one culprit of GHG emissions in three out of the four countries as shown in Figure B.1.6.
- As at 2014, total GHG emissions stood at about 13.7 MtCO2e and largely driven by emissions from LUCF (all MCO2e), followed by energy use (2.8 MCO2e), waste (0.9 MCO2e) and Butter Fats (0.5 MCO2e).
- The programme will curb Togo’s emissions profile with a reduction of over 380,000 tCO2e annually.

## Figure B.11: GHG Emissions Profiles for Togo, Senegal and Guinea (1993 – 2014)

Four main aspects (barriers) define the baseline of the proposed programme, serving as foundational elements that characterize these countries:

- **Increasing number of extreme climate events especially droughts, late onset of rain and early cessation**, increasing demand for water resources and reducing agricultural productivity.
- **Limited access to irrigation infrastructure** and limited capacity to increase irrigation capacities. According to the ND-GAIN vulnerability index, the three countries in the proposal rank near the world’s least prepared for climate change (Table B.13). Furthermore, these countries experience high demographic growth with an estimated population of 130 million people by 2050 and a young age structure, which is expected to increase demand for food, as well as fragile socioeconomic conditions as shown in Table D4.1.
- **The increasing impact of the extreme climate events on water resources** and particularly agriculture, water and energy. This can make populations more vulnerable to other hazards and socio-political instability. The agricultural sector alone accounts for about 30 percent of GDP (see Figure B.7), while the energy sector makes up nearly 40 percent of GDP in 2015.
- **These impacts could reduce national GDP by up to four percent per year**.

### Lack of promotion and use of effective water management techniques

In agriculture, the widespread phenomenon is the use of water efficient techniques such as drip irrigation. The adoption of these techniques has been slow due to lack of awareness and limited access to water resources. Water management keys are to increasing agricultural productivity under water scarcity conditions. In Togo, the figure is slightly higher than in Senegal and Guinea (Figure B.17). This is because Togo has a larger share of land under irrigation compared with Senegal, with about 5 percent of land under irrigation. Although the water sector is at risk from climate change which will reduce its capacity to provide sufficient water for agriculture, it still has a large potential in terms of groundwater resources.

It is less expensive to install and used compared to other AVM irrigation methods such as sprinkler irrigation, overhead irrigation, sub-irrigation, surface irrigation, etc., which are more costly.

A research by Siawas et al. (2015) on Design and Implementation of an IoT System for Smart Energy Consumption Monitoring in Agriculture: Case Study of Solar Pumping Systems in Rural Areas of Ghana shows that drip irrigation with sprinkler irrigation, overhead irrigation, sub-irrigation, level basin and surface irrigation that have relatively lower water efficiency compared to drip irrigation. The use of solar pumping systems is also more advantageous than other methods by reducing the size of the wet soil surface, thus decreasing the amount of direct evaporation and excess percolation into the groundwater. This reduces the risk of salinization in areas with poor drainage or where there are unfavorable soil conditions. Soil is maintained in a continuously moist condition. Nutrients can be applied through the drip system.

The results from a study conducted on the potential for drip irrigation in the Kankan region during phase II of the programme in RMRG, compared to conventional basin/furrow irrigation, are attributed to both water savings and the increase in yields resulting from the use of more efficient irrigation methods. The results show that drip irrigation is more advantageous than these countries, applicable on small scale and complementary with other systems, drip irrigation is gaining widespread acceptance among farmers.

The project has also been successful in promoting the use of solar pumping systems for irrigation at the Solar Market Gardens in the Kaliasi district of northern Benin. On an average of half hectare farm operated by a co-operative organization, the project has helped to reduce water consumption from 10-20 liters per day during the dry period. It was also reduced their daily task to a weekly or bi-weekly activity since it only takes one or few minutes to water the plants.

Biogas technologies present great opportunities for decentralized (off-grid) and diversified (multiple uses such as for stoves, refrigerators and generators) energy use. It is also the only renewable energy technology that is able to address multiple climate change mitigation challenges. Biogas technologies can be used in a wide range of applications including: livestock manure anaerobic digestion include effluent, sludge or bio slurry that are suitable for products by implementing climate resilient agriculture practices such as crop rotation and intercropping. The use of biogas digestate as fertilizer increases soil organic carbon content by 10% and quick-acting phosphorus by 150%, and increases agricultural yield by 11% per year. Other benefits include: reducing greenhouse gas emissions, improving water quality, increasing biodiversity, and adapting to climate change mitigation by reducing atmospheric emission of methane, costs savings and optimizing water use through recycling and reusing the digestate.

### For reducing greenhouse gases (GHGs). In all three countries, agriculture and forestry are the main culprits of GHG emissions.

Second, at the processing stage, linkages between farmers and cooperative societies will be established including the creation of a network of cooperatives. The objective is to increase the competitiveness of agricultural products in the international market. At this stage, renewable energy efficient drying, processing and packaging technologies will be introduced to improve post-harvest losses and enhance the quality of agricultural products such as rice, wheat, maize, sorghum, groundnuts, cocoa beans, coffee and cash crops. At each investment stage, women will constitute at least 54% of the target beneficiaries.

### Programme Rationale:

The Governments of Togo, Senegal and Guinea have requested the support of the African Development Bank (AfDB) to implement their respective Second Climate Change Programmes (SCCPs). The objective of the Togo Feed Africa Agenda. The goal of the entire SCCP2 programs in Africa is: (i) increase food production capacity and efficiency; (ii) improve access to markets for agricultural products, especially for smallholder farmers; (iii) promote sustainable agriculture; (iv) promote local, regional and international trade; (v) promote investment in agriculture; and (vi) increase the resilience of communities to climate change.

The SCCP2 program is an 18 priority corridors across the five agro-ecological zones of Africa considered important in these countries. The Togo Feed Africa Agenda is a flagship project under the second phase of the AfDB's Feed Africa. The project is currently being implemented in three Regional Member Countries (RMCs) at the request of those countries (Togo, Senegal and Guinea). The next phases of the SCCP2 program are replication and scaling out within RMCs requesting the intervention.

### Consistency with Countries' Climate Change Strategies:

- In Togo, the countries revised NDC target to unconditionally reduce GHG emissions by 20% by 2030. i.e., a 1,587,964 tCO2 eq compared to 2010 BAU emissions.
- For Senegal, the target is to use available internal resources to reduce GHG emissions by 5% (i.e., 30,576 tCO2 eq) and 7% (i.e., 35,196 tCO2 eq) between 2020 and 2030, respectively, compared to the BAU emissions in those years. In the situation where reforestation is implemented, Senegal will reduce GHG emissions by 48% (i.e., -78,561 tCO2 eq) in 2030.
- Guinea's NDC seeks to unconditionally cut down 2,056 tCO2 eq/year, i.e. a 9.7% reduction in emissions by 2030 while excluding LULUCF and conditionally reduce 3,829 tCO2 eq/year, or 17.0% GHG emissions by 2030 compared to the BAU scenario.

### Programme Targeted Areas and Beneficiaries:

The project will target a total population of over 546,000 people in the seven (7) Prefectures namely: Assal, Bassar, Bimbo, Danané, Dogondoutchi, Kéran, and Koulikor, with an estimated population of approximately 179,940 inhabitants. The project will directly benefit more than 258,630 people (i.e., over 50% women) in the seven Prefectures. It will also include this resilience to climate change of over 25% in the entire population (males and females).

### Programme Activities:

The project will focus on the Casamance Nature Natural comprising of the administrative regions of Kédougou, Ziguinchor and Tivaouane. The project area covers a total land area of 105,800ha with over 137,964 people living in the project's limits. This project in Senegal will focus on: (i) cash crops such as cotton, oil palms, sesame and tea; (ii) food products such as rice, fruit, groundnuts, maize, sorghum, cocoa beans, coffee, bananas and non-timber forest products; (iii) livestock such as cattle, sheep, goats, poultry and bees; (iv) fishery products from fish farming; (v) non-wood forest products such as honey, leaves, wild fruits and oils, resins among others.

## Synergies and Complementarities between ADB Baseline Investment and GCF Interventions

Figure B.1.18 best describes the existing synergies and complementarities between ADB baseline investments and GCF interventions.

Financing from the GCF will help convert human waste and agro-waste especially from post-harvest losses and processing that could have emitted methane and generate over 424,879 tCO₂e annually. The availability of biogas will also help generate over 10 million MWh of electricity per year. This will reduce the use of fossil fuels by over 35% in the country. It will also help to reduce about 14,738 tCO₂e of emissions every year by replacing diesel-based irrigation and energy systems with solar (taking advantage of the high solar radiation levels). The GCF financing will also support the implementation of a biogas project within the SDFG programme to help sequester over 5.0 million tCO₂e from agro forestry practices that will also enhance resilience against climate change. This will also contribute towards meeting the NDC commitments under the Paris Agreement.

Furthermore, the programme will help to strengthen the generation, access and use of climate information services within the SDFG to ensure that agro-advisory services and farm management practices are better informed on how best to adapt to changing weather patterns and climate resilience. Lessons from this programme will be applied when replicating similar programme in several African countries where the GCF is already supporting similar initiatives.

Other Complementary Initiatives that the SDFG Programme builds on:

- Ecosystem and Communities-based Resilience Building in Senegal, financed by the GCF
- Building Resilient Food Insecure Smallholder Farmers in Senegal, financed by the GCF

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 33

## Programme objective (programme-based): In the selected SCPF countries, various fragmented initiatives are being implemented to help build the resilience of smallholder farmers and rural communities to climate change as discussed above. However, these initiatives have been largely sector-specific and often not integrated into a coherent whole. The current fragmented approach has led to fragmented and ineffective interventions in key sectors like agriculture, land use and forestry, water and energy etc., which are all interlinked and need to be addressed together. These fragmented approaches, which are also being implemented in the context of climate stress, are clearly inadequate and only add to the numerous challenges such as increasing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+), loss of biodiversity, food insecurity, water scarcity, etc., that these countries face. To address these obstacles, this programme’s ultimate outcome is: strengthen climate resilience and reduce GHG emissions within the agricultural sector in three selected African countries. The programme will contribute to the overall programme impact, which is improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers through increased access to climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies, women’s empowerment, and enhanced energy generation in three selected African countries. The outcomes will be scaled up to other countries.

## Programme Goal Statement: If no required infrastructure, technical and financial support as well as climate based advisory services for staple crops processing zones (SCPFs) are provided, THEN the sustainability of agriculture value chains across the region will be compromised. This will lead to increased vulnerability of smallholder farmers and rural communities in terms of food security, resilience, and adoption of sustainable climate resilient practices, technologies and innovations among smallholder farmers are not achieved. The programme will contribute to the overall programme impact by providing infrastructure such as access roads, access to renewable energy and generation contributing to avoid over 10.87 million tCO2e in GHG emission of the agricultural sector within the three selected African countries. This will be achieved through: (i) strengthening climate resilience for vulnerable smallholder farmers and their communities, as described in each country’s long-term vision for climate change adaptation; (ii) improving access to climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies across the region; and (iii) increasing the value of agricultural chain from the production stage to processing. Since the COVID-19 pandemic began, governments have had to make difficult decisions about how best to respond to the crisis while also ensuring that essential services such as health and humanitarian support plans and social safety net programmes to help their most vulnerable population can be recovered and sustained. The programme will contribute to the overall programme impact by providing infrastructure such as access roads, access to renewable energy and generation contributing to avoid over 10.87 million tCO2e in GHG emission of the agricultural sector within the three selected African countries.

## Component 1: Strengthening critical SCPFs value chain infrastructure and management governance - Under this component, the programme will invest in improving the climate resilience of critical infrastructure along the agricultural value chain such as access roads, access to renewable energy and generation contributing to avoid over 10.87 million tCO2e in GHG emission of the agricultural sector within the three selected African countries. This will be achieved through: (i) strengthening climate resilience for vulnerable smallholder farmers and their communities, as described in each country’s long-term vision for climate change adaptation; (ii) improving access to climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies across the region; and (iii) increasing the value of agricultural chain from the production stage to processing. Since the COVID-19 pandemic began, governments have had to make difficult decisions about how best to respond to the crisis while also ensuring that essential services such as health and humanitarian support plans and social safety net programmes to help their most vulnerable population can be recovered and sustained.

## GOAL STATEMENT
- **Outcome:** Improved resilience of agricultural supply value chains and enterprises in the least developed countries to climate change through adaptation and mitigation.
- **Output:** Increased access to climate information, advisory services, and climate-resilient technologies for smallholder farmers.

### OUTCOMES
- **Outcome 1.0:** Improved access to climate information, advisory services, and climate-resilient technologies for smallholder farmers in the least developed countries.
- **Outcome 2.0:** Increased adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices by smallholder farmers in the least developed countries.

### OUTPUTS
- **Output 3.0:** Improved access to climate information, advisory services, and climate-resilient technologies for smallholder farmers in the least developed countries.
- **Output 4.0:** Increased adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices by smallholder farmers in the least developed countries.

### ACTIVITIES
- **Activity 1.0:** Improved access to climate information, advisory services, and climate-resilient technologies for smallholder farmers in the least developed countries.
- **Activity 2.0:** Increased adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices by smallholder farmers in the least developed countries.

### BARRIERS, RISKS
- **Barriers:**
  - Limited access to information and advisory services.
  - Lack of awareness about climate-resilient technologies.
  - Financial constraints for smallholder farmers.
- **Risks:**
  - Inadequate implementation of activities due to lack of resources.
  - Resistance from stakeholders.

### ASSUMPTIONS
- Smallholder farmers will adopt climate-resilient practices.
- Adequate funding and resources will be available.
- Partnerships with local organizations will be successful in implementing the project.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 5 pages)

The proposed GCF financed Staple Crop Processing Zones (SCPZs) Programme, mobilizing USD 162.79 million of GCF funding will trigger a paradigm shift towards low-emission, climate-resilient development pathways across the agricultural value chains in the three selected countries.

**Component 1:** Strengthening the resilience of critical SCPZs values chain infrastructure :

- First, at the production stage, the programme will invest in the development of SCPZs Agricultural Processing Hubs (APHs). Agricultural Transformation Centres (ATCs). It will also involve the construction of access facilities such as roads, bridges and other infrastructure to facilitate the movement of agricultural produce. The hubs will be equipped with technologies such as equipped boreholes, wells and small reservoirs/dams including rain water harvesting technologies, while also providing storage facilities for seeds, fertilizers, pesticides and other inputs. This will enable farmers to increase production of vegetables and fruits. Second, at the processing stage, linkages between farmers and cooperative societies will be strengthened through the establishment of Agricultural Processing Hubs (APHs) which will provide access to market and national activities. Also, at this stage, renewable energy efficient drying, processing and packaging technologies will be introduced to improve post harvest handling of staple crops to reduce losses after harvest, and add market value especially for smallholder farmers who are often the most vulnerable in terms of income generation and food security.

The following outputs are expected of this component:

- Improved access to irrigation distribution (IDIS) systems powered by solar pumps to support climate resilient agricultural (CRA) practices (GCF financed).
- Agricultural processing infrastructure along the agricultural value chain in the three selected countries. First, at the production stage, the programme will support access to drip irrigation distribution (IDIS) systems powered by solar pumps to increase water efficient use of water and reduce water losses. Second, at the processing stage, linkages between farmers and cooperative societies will be strengthened through the establishment of Agricultural Processing Hubs (APHs) which will provide access to market and national activities. Under a business-as-usual scenario (BAU), that is without GCF interventions, the emissions baseline of using diesel engines for irrigation in the three selected countries would reach as much as 10,973 metric tonnes CO2e annually. By contrast, under the proposed intervention, the emissions from solar pumps for irrigation systems could reach as much as 1,507 metric tonnes CO2e annually, for about 39.17 ha. Additionally, this intervention will also reduce water losses by up to 40% and increase crop yields by an average of 20%. The programme will support the employment of farming sector (casual labourers, handlers, producers/buyers etc.,) for an additional 395,890 individuals.

## Activity 1.1: Improving access to small-scale agricultural water management (AWM) infrastructure

Under this activity, GCF will support smallholder farmers, women-led agricultural enterprises (WAEs), agricultural cooperatives and FBA s in the Programme countries with adequate solar irradiation.

### Output 1.2: Increased access to renewable energy technology for staple food crops processing (GCF Financed)

One of the biggest challenges confronting the agricultural sector in SSA is poor harvesting handling of staple food crops, which leads to post-harvest losses and contributes significantly to GHG emissions. The main causes are inadequate harvesting technologies, storage facilities, and the inability of farmers to readily have access to markets. In Senegal for instance, loss rates from harvest to market were 10% in rice, 25% in millet, 37% in sorghum, 48% in groundnuts, 69% in maize, 70%
percent and 6.9 percent respectively. These losses are as a result of ineffective food processing technologies for cleaning, drying, sorting, packaging and storage. The situation is not different for other countries such as Togo where loss rates range between 45-80%, and inadequate storage facilities.

Furthermore, existing food processing technologies in these countries heavily rely on biomass-based energy sources. In Togo approximately 80% of households, micro and small scale agriculture enterprises rely largely on biomass based sources of energy for cooking and heating. This is also the case with Guinea where 95% of households use wood as their primary source of energy. In 2013 alone, households consumed over 1,725,219 tons of firewood and 482,248 tons of charcoal. The burning of these biomass-based energy sources releases significant amounts of carbon dioxide (CO2) into the atmosphere. Emissions were estimated to biomass burners for energy consumption. According to FAO as at 2005, 33% of Guinea was tree covered and 17% in Togo. In addition, burning of these biomass-based energy sources releases significant amounts of CO2 (about 9.1 Mio/yr) into the atmosphere.

Apart from the use of biomass based sources of energy for consumption, these poor countries equally rely heavily on fossil fuel based energy systems which are relatively inefficient, not reliable and contributes significantly to GHG emissions. For instance, in 2014 alone, fossil-based energy systems emitted close to 38MCO2eq into the atmosphere while in 2015, this figure increased to about 67 MCO2eq. In Togo and Guinea, where fossil based energy systems are used for cooking and heating, CO2 emissions were estimated at 9.1 Mio/yr and 2.6 Mio/yr respectively into the atmosphere.

Under this context, the programme will promote the use of off grid solar power to solar agro-equipment in the CCSP2 programme areas where there is adequate solar irradiation. The expected output from this activity is that by 2030, the use of solar energy systems will significantly reduce smallholder farmers, micro and small scale agriculture enterprises reliance on biomass based fossil fuel
based energy sources for cooking and heating. In addition, the expected output from this activity is that the use of solar energy systems for lighting and processing in the programme could amount to over 14,738 kWp annually. However, with GCF support, the expected output will be increased to about 20,596 kWp by 2030.

### Activity 1.2.1: Improving resilience of agricultural cooperatives and FBA s value chain infrastructure for food processing

Under this activity, the programme will invest in the installation of 14.69MW of solar energy for lighting, processing, drying and storage.

To be consistent with ADBs procurement procedures for competitive bidding, no particular technology is stated as preferable.
There are technical feasibility for all the technologies under consideration in the Programme countries with adequate solar irradiation.

## Activity 1.2: Deploying low-carbon energy technologies for enhanced resilience

As extensively discussed in the programme feasibility study, the over dependence on charcoal and firewood as a major source of cooking fuel is one of the main causes of deforestation and land degradation, which in turn compounds the problems of deforestation, degradation and increased carbon emissions. Also, the use of low-intensive farming practices such as slash-and-burn agriculture (SBA) has led to soil erosion and loss of nutrients. In addition, livestock fermentation (3.9MCO2e), rice cultivation (3.5MCO2e), manure left on pasture (3.3MCO2e), burning Sawdust (0.6MCO2e), cattle dung (0.4MCO2e) and other biogas sources (0.1MCO2e) have contributed to GHG emissions in the livestock sector, agriculture in Guinea between 1990 and 2014. The same can be said about Senegal, where the growth of GHG emissions in the livestock sector has been attributed to cattle dung (3.8MCO2e), manure left on pasture (2.8 MCO2e), and others (0.5 MCO2e) as illustrated in Figure B 1.5. On the other hand, waste products (liquid and solid) from livestock farming are a major source of GHG emissions in Senegal. The biogas technology can be used to convert this waste into a sustainable “resource” for clean energy generation such as biogas technologies. As shown in Figure B 3.2, the biogas generated from human and animal waste is used for cooking, heating and even for transportation. Without GCF support, baseline emissions from: 1) methane release from livestock manure (0.5MCO2e), 2) cattle dung (0.4MCO2e), 3) other biogas sources (0.1MCO2e); 4) burning sawdust (0.6MCO2e); however, emissions abatement for energy and waste: 1) bio-waste digested; and 2) avoided diesel used by loggers for electricity generation, transport of goods and services, and other uses.

### Figure B3.1 Biogas generation from human and agro waste for diversified energy use

Source: Greenpower.com cited in Elmer GitzGIZT (2016)

Under this activity, the programme will invest in the installation of about 24,579* m³ of biogas digesters to treat livestock manure and produce biogas for power generation. (See programme financial and economic models for the justification of Biogasderer volume and cost). The biogas generated from human and animal waste will be used for cooking, heating and even for transportation.

The energy provision will support processing of farm produce, powering of cold storage facilities, and where applicable, irrigation systems. In addition to providing clean energy, the biogas digesters will also provide a source of income through the sale of agricultural waste.

The temperatures in all the Programme Countries provide great advantage for thermodynamic conditions favourable to a pyrolytic gasification process, which operates under much higher at ambient temperature so thermodynamic digestion could lead to a production rate that is approximately twice the rate of mesophilic digestion. Because of this favourable condition, reactors for anaerobic digestion can be designed with lower capacity and thus lower capital cost than those required in countries maintaining the same gas production with similar efficiency levels. Due to the thermodynamic conditions, all the key biogasogenic feedstocks can be used as raw material for the process. The APs are located within the Special Processing Zone (SPZ). Programmes that ii: Agri-Processing Hubs (APHs)* that offer adequate infrastructure, logistics and specialized facilities and services (e.g., electricity, water, cold chain facilities, laboratory and certification services, business services, ICT, waste treatment, etc.), iii: Agricultural Transformation Centres (ATCs) that provide agricultural transformation services such as seed multiplication, production to serve as aggregation points to accumulate products from the community to supply the Agri-Processing Hubs (APHs), iv: Cold chain facilities, v: Warehouses, vi: Water and water storage facilities, cold chain facilities, specialized storage facilities, laboratory and certification services.

Activity 1.3.1: Development of SGCP Access Infrastructure & Support AIP Management Governance

Under this activity, ADB partners will finance under the Agri-Processing Hubs (APHs) facility. i) The construction of about 200 km of new roads in the SPZ and the upgrading of existing roads to improve access for vehicles and goods to market platforms (hubs), financial centres, water supply plant for over 15,000 cubic meter/day; waste management plants with a capacity of 300 tons per day; ii) The construction of about 20 km of new roads in the SPZ and upgrading of existing roads spanning over 15,000 ha, provision of 24/3 transmission lines to and around the IAPs area; iii) 230 km new access roads for the Agri-Processing Hubs (APHs), Agricultural Transformation Centres (ATCs) facilities. ADB will finance: ii) construction of more than 15 ATCs that are strategically located in the project area to provide agricultural transformation services such as seed multiplication, production to serve as aggregation points to accumulate products from the community; and iii) provision of cold stores, storage warehouses for fresh produce from the farming communities.

Component 2: Promotion of climate resilient agriculture (CRA) practices and technologies adoption among smallholder farmers

In this component, the programme will invest in the promotion and use of CRA practices and technology uptake among agricultural value chain actors in the three selected SCFPZ countries. At the production stage, the programme will invest in the promotion of improved seed varieties, soil fertility management, water management, integrated pest management, sustainable agro-forestry practices, and wetlands vegetation. This component will also focus on activities to improve access to agricultural extension services through the establishment of a network of community-based stations networks and by promoting the generation of access to and use of seasonal and sub-seasonal weather forecast and agricultural advisory services in partnership with the private sector and local agri-advisory services in the SCFPZ.

Chapter 2.1: Adoption of CRA and agro-forestry practices among smallholder farmers

*An APH is a well-defined, centrally managed tract of land developed, subdivided and dedicated to supporting firms and other stakeholders engaged in agri-processing and related activities located throughout the production area within the SPZ.

*An ATC is a physical complex of facilities centrally located in the middle of a farming community, where required services are provided by private sector service providers. These services include seed multiplication, storage and maintenance services, crop handling, grading, storage, and processing for increased shelf life; livestock husbandry services including breeding, vaccination, feeding, grading and processing; food quality and safety control and certification; distribution and marketing platforms.

## Activity 2.1: Promote CRA among smallholder farmers

### Sub-activity 2.1.1: Increase awareness and use of selected CRA practices among smallholder farmers

This activity will promote the adoption and use of selected CRA practices, such as ACIs and FCRs, covering about 39.78 ha of land (see pages 69–71 of the programme feasibility study for selected CRA practices). The project will work with national agricultural research institutions to develop seed varieties that are adapted to local climates and conditions. Such seeds and Germinated Seeds are an important agricultural requirement improving crop yields, increasing resilience against climate change, and reducing post-harvest losses. Introducing these improved seed varieties at the right time will make a significant contribution to the yield increase necessary to meet the market demands. High-quality seeds with good germination rates can be produced by farmers themselves or through private sector companies that are resistant to insect pests and diseases, and provide greater tolerance to drought, flood, and higher temperatures associated with climate change.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2: Promote agro-resilience practices among smallholder farmers

Under this activity, the programme will assist farm households to create sustainable managed forests (about 40,683 ha) for carbon sequestration and carbon capture. The introduction of this activity will provide an alternative and more sustainable income source, including many other benefits for smallholders as it is a win-win situation.

### Outcome 2: Awareness raising, capacity building, community and institutional strengthening on agricultural value chains management (GCF, AIDB and Partners)

The promotion of climate-resilient agricultural (CRA) practices requires massive awareness raising and knowledge transfers among value chain actors. This is particularly important to increase the rate of adoption of these practices by smallholder farmers in order to ensure their sustainability over time.

### Activity 2.1.1: Capacity building of value chains actors & development of agricultural processing support

Under this activity, the following training and capacity building exercise will be conducted:

- **Capacity building and training of relevant staff at the Executing Entities (EEs), the Project Implementing Units (PIUs), the Agro-Industry Parks (APIs), value chain actors.**

##### Main activities related to SCPZs Access Infrastructure & Support for AIP Management Governance:

* Targeted crops under the programme: Currently practicing any form of climate resilient activities in its daily operations such as use of drought resistant and improved seeds, among others.

* Strong a business plan to develop a trading agriculture with potential downstream value chain linkages.
* Willingness of the ACSs and FBA to adopt climate change adaptation strategies, including reduce GHG emissions in its operations through proc renewable energy and improving energy efficiency within the organization.
* The area under cooperative or FBA primarily grows any of the 18 staple food crops considered under the “Feed Africa” initiative: maize, sorghum, millet, cassava, mangoes, citrus fruits, coffee, sugarcane, millet, cowpeas, wheat, soybean, livestock, poultry, and fish among others.


#### Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2: Promote CRA and Agro-forestry Practices among Smallholder Farmers

* All activities related to CRA and agro-forestry management practices will be carried out within the SCPZs designated for this activity (the same as those that have been designated for this programme).
* A formally registered SHFWS with an ACS, or FBA for more than 1 year.
* At least 80% of preference for women and girls who are the most vulnerable groups in the society.
* Preference for beneficiaries that currently practicing any form of climate resilient activities in daily farming operations such as use of drought resistant and improved seeds, among others.
* Willingness of the ACSs and FBA to adopt new technologies innovations and new farm management practices will be introduced by the programme such DCS powered by solar panels, improved nutrient management, and improved water harvesting techniques.
* Activities are mainly carried out along the agricultural value chains involving targeted crops under the programme.


##### The selection criteria for this activity differ for the different project specific regions and extensively discussed in the various ESS reports, and summarised on pages 44 - 51 of the programme feasibility study (FPS) report.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

## Activity 2.2.1: Capacity building of value chains agents/countries/development of agricultural processing support systems

The selection criteria for activity 2.2.1 includes:

- Staff of the EES, PUL, APL, and relevant staff of Ministries, Departments and Agencies (MDA) working on the agro-industrial development (APD), including those involved in the Agri-Credit Loan development (ACL)
- Representatives of organised Civil Society Organizations (CSOs), Environmental Non-Governmental Organizations (ENGOs), and other stakeholders
- Representative of Public and Private Financial Institutions (PFIs);
- Preference for young potential entrepreneurs, SMEs, MSMEs working on the agro-industrial domain; and,
- Relevant private sector actors.

## Activity 2.2.2: Establishment of climate information and early warning systems in SCFZs

For climate-resilient and sustainable farm practices within the SCFZs, this activity will help to enhance climate information services for farmers through the development of automated agrometeorological and self-recording rain stations to improve collection of climate data and transmission of climate information at community-level. This will be achieved by:

- Deploying a team of climate advisory service providers (e.g., agro-advisory services), such as, planting date, good farm management practices, irrigation schedules, use of farm machinery and choice of inorganic fertilizers, cut-off crop varieties, pest and disease control measures, etc. to provide farmers with information on climate change adaptation and prevention/control measures.
- Training the local partner team on technical assistance on preparatory mechanisms including analysis of vulnerability, gathering of data, decisions on contingency plan and/or adaptation measures, analysis of gender and socioeconomic inequalities; (ii) preparation of a climate risk management plan for each farm; (iii) development of an early warning system during growing season; (iv) improve dissemination technique; and (v) build capacity: adaptation and contingency & anticipatory planning.

The technical approach to expand business in the 4 proposed project countries is summarized as follows:

- To deploy a Team Tech to support the local partner team to conduct an initial field survey for the set-up of the technology infrastructure, engagement with local MNOs for the platform integrations and configurations and licensing bodies within the respective countries.
- To deploy a Call Centre Team for the set-up of the Call Center.
- To deploy a Business Development Team to support the local partner business team on the commercialization of the solutions in their respective countries and beyond.
- To deploy a Data Collection Team to be used by the deployed digital platforms to collect and design tailored climate smart advisory, weather alerts, market price information, health and financial inclusive information services
- To appropriate digital channels to communicate the generated information and warnings to target beneficiaries including smallholder farmers for their innovative farm management decision making via e-wallet market linkage.
- To deploy a Business Development Team to support the local partner business team on the commercialization of the solutions in their respective countries and beyond.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 48

## Choice of interventions: Comparisons between alternatives over potential impacts and potentials

The selection of all interventions in the SCPZ case studies where guided by each countries National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and their respective Country Strategic Plans (CSPs). The CSPs are a set of strategies developed by each country to guide its own development. The CSPs are based on the recommendations from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the World Bank.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements: What is currently applicable? 2 approaches

### Approach 1: Group Model (Group MRB)

The Access and Benefit Sharing (ABS) Agreement of the African Rice Center (African Rice Center) is the premier pan-African development institution prog economic growth and social progress across the continent. It is composed of three institutions, namely: 1). The International Institute for Tropical Agriculture (IITA); 2). The International Crops Research Institute for Semi-arid Tropics (ICRISAT); and 3). The Africa Rice Center (Africa Rice).

### Approach 2: Group Model (Group MRB)

The Bank Group focuses on five development priorities within the framework of its Ten-Year Strategy (2012) aimed at prog inclusion and green growth. Usually referred to as the “High 5”, the development priorities are: 1) Light up and Power Africa; 2) Feed the Future; 3) Invest in the Youth; 4) Make cities and human settlements sustainable; and 5) Boost Inclusive Growth.

This program is intended to support the realization of the “Feed Africa” and the “Improve the Quality of Life for the People of Africa” development priorities. The ADB has supported several transformative activities and investments in these priority areas, including: (i) improving access to electricity; (ii) increasing agricultural productivity and competitiveness through market linkages; (iii) strengthening institutions and governance systems; and (iv) promoting inclusive growth.

The ADB has financed several related projects and programs in Togo, Senegal and Guinea. For example, as of June 30, 2019, the ADB’s active portfolio in Senegal includes twenty-nine transactions for a total net commitment of $1,630 million. The largest transaction is the National Agricultural Insurance Scheme (NAIS) which provides insurance coverage to smallholder farmers against crop and livestock market risks.

The sectoral breakdown of national projects is as follows: Transport (58.7%); Water and sanitation (14.6%); Rural (9.3%); Health (8.2%); Education (4.0%); and Social services (4.0%). The remaining 1.4% is for other sectors.

In Togo, the ADB’s Ongoing portfolio includes 14 operations for a total net commitment of CFA 231.85 million. It consists of 11 national operations of UA 112.47 million accounting for 48.5% of the total budget; and three multilateral operations of UA 90.65 million, which account for 39.2%. The remaining 12.3% is for two regional projects (UA 18.73 million) with a share of 3.2% in 2016 compared to 3% in 2016.

In Guinea, as of June 30, 2018, the ADB’s portfolio comprised 16 operations amounting to a total commitment of UA 282.95 million. The portfolio is dominated by regional public projects (84.23% of commitments) comprising nine operations.

**Page**

* **Figure B-4.1:** shows the governance and implementing arrangements of the SOCP project in Togo (more details can also be found on page 20).

At the top of the Figure B-4.1 is (1), the Ministry of Agriculture (MOA) that has the oversight function over the Karsa Agropole Strategic Development Plan (KASDP). The MOA is represented by the Director General of the MOA and the Agency for the Promotion and Development Agropoles (APRODAT), which is the technical responsible party for the EE- Togo. Below this, there are two boxes representing the two SOCP projects in Togo: the Karsa Agropole Strategic Development Plan and the Agropoles Zones (AZADEs) for Togo.

* **Box 3:** shows the MOA with the advice of both agencies also serving as the JSC for the project in Togo. It is a Board of Directors, chaired by the Prime Minister, and comprised of seven active members (public and private) and one observer. The Board of Directors has the following functions: the Director General of the MOA, the Director of Administrative and Financial Affairs; the Directorate of Forecasting, Planning, Monitoring and Evaluation; the Directorate of Research and Development.

* **Box 4:** shows the Project Implementing Unit (PIU), which has as its main functions to: manage the construction of the Karsa Agropole Strategic Development Plan (KASDP) and the Agropoles Zones (AZADEs); and perform all activities related to investors, especially private sector investors among others.

APRODAT was created by virtue of Decree No. 2018-056/PE of 27 February 2018 establishing the institutional framework for the implementation of the SOCP project in Togo, which is based on the principles and rules of the public law order. The assessment of country, sector and project risks as well as of the capacity of the technical responsible party of the MOA to implement the SOCP project has been carried out by AIDB. The Decree No. 2018-056/PE also defines the terms of procurement system (Togo or AIDB used for specific activities or for all similar programmatic activities). APRODAT has been set up as a technical body in charge of implementing the SOCP project and is responsible for the following functions: Management, Monitoring and Evaluation; Rural Infrastructure; Environment; Capacity Building and Gender; Agro-industry; Legal-Industrialization; Marketing and Value Addition. The Decree No. 2018-056/PE also defines the terms of procurement system set up to boost the implementation of activities. Since then, this team of specialists has been expanded to include specialists in other areas such as finance, administration, human resources, etc., with a view to ensuring that all aspects are covered for an agricultural assist; an administrative assistant and drivers.

The Togolese Government and the Bank will agree on a list of specific activities to be implemented by APRODAT. The Decree No. 2018-056/PE also defines the terms of procurement system in relation to the actual programme implementation needs. Any revision of the plan will be subject to prior approval by AIDB.

Some of the actions recommended for Togo after the FMA are:

* **Update:** the organizational chart, including the principles of internal oversight; particularly the separation of responsibilities and the appointment of the head of service to update the directives mentioned in the Decree, especially those of the directorates involved in the implementation of the SOCP (Administrative and Financial Officer).

* **Review:** of the administrative, financial and accounting procedures manual, on which its internal oversight system will be based; covering all management cycles: expenditure management cycle (procurement procedures for goods and services).

### Figure B.4.S: Governance and Implementation Arrangement in Guinea

The FMA of the technical responsible party of the EE (ADAZZ) has recently appointed a financial coordinator as well as Head of Project Implementation Unit (PIU). The overall fiduciary risk was considered substantial because of the absence of:
- (i) A financial coordinator and personnel;
- (ii) An administrative, financial and accounting procedures manual; 
- (iii) A computerized accounting system.

Consequently, recommendations were made:

- To hire all necessary staff to the programme implementing unit (PIU);
- As soon as the programme is launched, prepare a budget plan coupled with the indicative activities schedule;
- To establish an administrative, financial and accounting procedures manual (AFAPM), and another responsible for establishing the computerized accounting and financial systems;
- Assign financial tasks as soon as the financial staff are recruited; and,
- To establish the financial management framework and instruments in accordance with ADB's requirements.

## General Implementation Arrangements for Renewable Energy Investments

### Activities 1.2.1 & 1.2.2 across the three countries which include solar irrigation schemes, solar systems for lighting and processing, as well as bio-gas based generators (Activities 1.2.3) will be implemented through a Public-Private Partnership (PPP). The PPP is the DCCM model, which stands for Design, Construct, Operate and Maintain. Under this model, the Executing Entity will be responsible for the design, construction, operation and maintenance of the solar irrigation schemes, solar systems for lighting and processing, as well as bio-gas based generators (Activities 1.2.3). The DCCM model is a type of Public-Private Partnership (PPP) in which the private sector provides services to the public sector. The Executing Entity will be responsible for the design, construction, operation and maintenance of the solar irrigation schemes, solar systems for lighting and processing, as well as bio-gas based generators (Activities 1.2.3). The DCCM model is a type of Public-Private Partnership (PPP) in which the private sector provides services to the public sector.

## Procedures and conditions of the ADB in financing sovereign operations, and in consideration of the terms and conditions of the term sheet for the SCPZ programme with the GCF.

The Ministry of Finance for each country will disburse GCF non-reimbursable funds to the Technical Responsible Party (TRP) of the executing entity of the various projects. The TRP will be responsible for project implementation, management, and coordination with the various implementing entities, including gender.

The EEs will work with the project implementation or management units (PIUs/RMUs) to procure goods and services in accordance with the GCF procurement policies. The PIUs/RMUs will submit requests for non-reimbursable funds to the TRP. The TRP will then submit these requests to the ADB for approval.
