## B

### Figure B.4.E.3: Programme Fund for Guinea

#### B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Firstly, the GCF was established to finance programs and projects for climate change adaptation and/or mitigation. With its huge scale of operations and ability to mobilize resources from a wide range of sources, the project proposed contributes directly towards reducing greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) in the three countries considered: Senegal, Guinea and Togo. The project will also contribute significantly to enhancing food security and water security; and improving access to low-carbon energy technologies, especially the switch from fossil fuels and conventional power generation to cleaner energy sources.

Secondly, for all the three pilot SDPQs countries considered in this first phase of the program, key sectors that featured most in their unconditional targets for mitigation and enhanced removals of GHG emissions are agriculture and energy. For instance, Senegal’s unconditional target is to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 20% by 2030; Togo’s unconditional target is to install 27,500 Bedougeater at the household level; and generate 160MW of the use of Solar PV; and, providing over 382 GWh (cumulative energy) from renewable energy sources (cumulative GHG reduction of 34 MTCO2e by 2030); and (ii) supporting the dissemination of best practices in agriculture and forestry to enhance carbon sequestration and reduce deforestation. Togo’s unconditional targets include: (i) the reduction of 23 MTCO2e by 2030; Similarly, for Togo, some of its unconditional actions in energy include: (i) the use of renewable energy sources at household level and capacity building for the various actors concerned. The greatest mitigation potentials from the SDPQ project are expected to be realized through the following activities: (i) the promotion of sustainable land management practices and resilient agricultural practices; and (ii) carbon sequestration from agroforestry practices. Thus, the GCF finance will make a real


---
**Page 67**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

### Contribution in line with respective NDC targets of these countries. Additionally, many of the sub-projects under the SCFF project (in particular, activities related to Deploying Low-carbon Energy Technologies and Promote CTA and Agro-biotech Innovations) are also aligned with the GCF’s Strategic Objectives.

These sub-projecs can therefore be scaled up, out-scaled, and used for knowledge and learning in many other parts of Africa.

Thirdly, and finally, one of the most important aims of the GCF is to lever additional money for the projects/programmes it funds to shift global economic development towards more sustainable pathways. To close the financing gap towards climate resilient development, the GCF has been working with governments and partners in several countries to develop a green bond market. The green business market in the region Covid-19 has further impacted investment activity in the selected countries, as institutional investors have reduced their exposure to emerging markets. However, the GCF has managed to raise $20 million from a pool of about $16.9 billion made available for the pilot SCFF program. GCF involvement in the program is paramount to take advantage of this existing on-shoring to shift global economic development in these countries towards more sustainable pathways. Also, the GCF’s support will help to ensure that the investments are aligned with the SDGs and other international commitments.

The agricultural value chain will help to create long-term investments from the private sector beyond GCF investments. This is a key justification of the GCF financial instruments consequently to make the investment viable.


### Togo

* An average score from the Country Policy and Institutional Assessment (CPIA) of the AIDB and the World Bank has never exceeded 3.2 out of 6 from 2008 to 2016. This score means that Togo is still a country facing conditions of fragility.
* From 2011 to 2015, the government had to resort to food imports due to a significant decrease in cereal production and poor transport infrastructures. As a result, the country’s debt level increased from 46% of GDP in 2011 to 79.2% in 2016.
* According to the World Food Programme (WFP), 35% of Togo’s population is food insecure and 80% of the population lives below the poverty line, with a high vulnerability to climate change. The government has been unable to meet its obligations under the WAMU/MEC framework (less than 70%) after 3 years.
* According to the ECF program, the government has committed itself to reducing its debt level by 25% of GDP in 2016 and 40% in 2018. The government is also committed to reducing its external debt from 90% of GDP in 2013 to 70% in 2018.
* Based on this analysis, the GCF financing is requested in the form of a grant ($30 million) and loan (160 million) to support the implementation of the ECF program. The total cost of this programme in Togo is estimated at US$ 18.47GCO2eq while the GCF contributes at US $6.8 for each establishment unit achieved.


### Senegal

* Despite relative political stability, Senegal still ranks 182 out of 197 countries in the 2016 Human Development Index. Some 39% of its population is food insecure.
* Though agriculture is the main growth driver of the economy, it is largely subsistence-based in nature and vulnerable to climate change. The country has been unable to meet its obligations under the WAMU/MEC framework (less than 23%) of debt in 2017 or 54% in 2018 but remains below the standard of 70% retained under the WAMU/MEC framework.
* The government has been unable to meet its obligations under the World Bank’s Economic Policy Support Instrument (2013 EFSI). Based on this, the government is committed to no longer contracting new debt and to reduce its external debt from 95.7% of GDP in 2014 to 86% by 2018.
* In these circumstances, a 7.4% grant financing and a 28% loan financing would be the best GCF instrument required to make the investment viable.
* The total cost of this programme in Senegal is estimated at US$ 18.49GCO2eq while the GCF contributes at US $5.44 for each establishment unit achieved.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 61

The economy of Guinea will still recovering from the 2014-2015 Elhite outlooks that slashed the country’s growth rate from 5.9% in 2012 to 3.8% in 2013. Though slowly recovering, the economy is still characterized by a high poverty rate of about 55%. The country has a population of approximately 10 million people and is one of the poorest countries in West Africa.

Guinea is grappling with fragile factors that discourage inclusive growth. In recognition of this, the country’s new strategy seeks to address these challenges through the promotion of sustainable economic activities, and development agriculture and agricultural product processing. However, significant infrastructure spending has been required to support these activities. The public sector debt as a share of GDP increased from 27% in 2014 to approximately 38.5% in 2017. Equally, significant public investment, financed by loans, raised the debt level from less than 27% of GDP in 2014 to over 60% in 2017. The total external debt service as a share of exports of goods and services increased from 35% in 2014 to approximately 89% in 2017. In addition, the external debt increased from 18% of GDP in 1998. According to the IMF, the public debt should remain below the threshold of 60% of GDP.

Against this background, a 74% grant financing (25% loans) would be the best GCF instrument required to accommodate the current debt situation in Guinea if the threshold should remain below the 70%. Besides, in its Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs), the country has committed itself to reduce its greenhouse gas emissions by 34% of business as usual levels. Further, the abatement cost of this programme in the Guinees is estimated at US$ 7.0MCO2e and the GCF contributes US$15M.

## B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The SCPZ initiative is such transformational that the AE has already received several requests from governments regarding Cost Sharing Mechanisms (CSMs) for the SCPZ. The AE is also exploring opportunities for similar projects in their countries of origin. In Senegal, it is scaling up the SCPZ with a new agro-industrial park to be financed together with the AE in another part of the country.

The SCPZ initiative will provide an opportunity for the Guinean government to develop and implement policies that ensure climate measures are integrated into national agriculture value chains towards low carbon and climate-resilient activities. The SCPZ will also provide a platform for the development of agro-industrial parks where shared facilities are provided to producers, processors, aggregators and distributors to operate more efficiently and effectively in order to increase their competitiveness.

The PPP arrangements for the operation and management of the facilities for users will be structured so that the private sector can benefit from the SCPZ initiative. The AE will provide guarantees on the assets financed with GCF financing. Moreover, maintenance of the facilities is part of the PPF agreements with the ECOs. The AE will also provide technical assistance to the Guinean government in order to develop a business plan and a backward linkage for efficient and integrated operation that will maximize profitability to sustain the enterprises along the value chain.

The SCPZ initiative will be structured so as to attract private sector investment, including from international investors, developers, producers and other actors who will pursue complementary investments that will facilitate the exit of public funds. The AE will also provide technical assistance to the Guinean government in order to develop a business plan and a backward linkage for efficient and integrated operation that will maximize profitability to sustain the enterprises along the value chain.

a) Improving incentives for investing in sustainable natural resources use through higher and more stable producer prices and income.

b) Avoiding market-driven resource degradation by relating market development with gender-sensitive management of the resource base.

c) Limiting exclusionary practices and structures by improving access to marketing infrastructure (information, storage, transportation, etc.) and by ensuring that all stakeholders have equal access to markets.

d) Reducing market-related risks and vulnerability through measures that help stabilize the price of foodstuffs and those that improve access to crop insurance. This will also include measures that reduce post-harvest losses and ensure better access to improved access to sowing seasons and water sources among others, including by improving infrastructure.

e) Ensuring that all stakeholders are involved in the design of the project. The targeted efforts are broadened and improved the participation of small producers/male and female in value chains serving markets for foodstuffs and high-value products such as fruits and vegetables.

The sub-programs in the SCPZ program are designed in a such way that all parties involved can make reasonable profits from their investments in comparison with the next available alternatives. This will ensure continued interest in the activities after the project is completed, and that the private sector partners can make sufficient profits from their activities.

Social strategy: Ideologies that rigidly limit opportunities, population and a balance for some groups are not sustainable because they do not take into account the benefit that power and decision-making should have with some communities. This permeates lives, relationships and policies at the city, community, national and international levels. In doing so, fundamental changes in social structures and practices are required to ensure that all stakeholders can participate fully in the SCPZ program.

The SCPZ include elements that will specifically support the marginalized and disadvantaged groups to ensure that by the end of the project, they have the capacity to continue their activities independently. This will be achieved through a series of training courses that are aimed at informing the people about the threats that the change in their environment could have on their livelihoods.

Management structures will be put in place that will include both the local communities, government organizations at different levels and private sector partners. The management structure will ensure that all stakeholders can participate fully in the project and help in breaking the barriers that exist in the relationships between the local communities, government officials and the business community. This will also ensure that the lessons learned from this project are shared with other similar projects.

A communication strategy will be put in place to create a mutually beneficial link between the social and economic capacities and responsibilities are developed given to the most appropriate institutions that are in a position to sustain the activities after the completion of the project and program.

Political strategy: In order to overcome political barriers, sensitization of political leaders, private sector involvement, and community participation will be achieved through the following approaches: The Ministry of Rural Development (MRD) and PSBC. Capacity building is also necessary across the board. In addition, this funding proposal has governments as key stakeholders in the project. This will ensure that the lessons learned from this project are shared with other similar projects.

A strong political support will ensure the continuity of the project and replication in other areas of the nation as demonstrated by the MRD and PSBC.

Monitoring and evaluation strategy: At all levels of stakeholder, the program will establish well-presented protocols and well- presented, intuitive and communicable data to strengthen decision making, progress measurement, transparency and accountability. The monitoring and evaluation system will be put in place to ensure that the lessons learned from this project are shared with other similar projects.

Institutional strategy: Social capital is critical for poverty alleviation and sustainable human and economic development. Social capital includes social networks, trust, norms, values, increased collective resource degradation and depletion, more investment in knowledge and information flows, increased collective resource degradation and depletion, more investment in knowledge and information flows, increased collective resource degradation and depletion, more investment in knowledge and information flows.

Furthermore, the program will be put in place to create a mutually beneficial link between the social and economic capacities and responsibilities are developed given to the most appropriate institutions that are in a position to sustain the activities after the completion of the project and program.

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 63 B

Firstly, the initiative involves Programme Management Unit (PMU) as well as a Programme Steering and Monitoring Committee (PSMC), involving all major stakeholders along the agricultural value chain such as the public and private sectors including civil society organizations and other relevant institutions. The program will provide avenues for knowledge sharing and learning and the continuing application of best practices.

Secondly, the program has built-in training activities that are aimed at providing: (i) capacity building and training in CRA practices, climate information and technology update; (ii) training in mixed agroforestry practices and livestock management; (iii) training on value chain development and marketing; (iv) training on gender equality and women empowerment for rural women groups and youth groups. Currently, it is envisaged that the awareness and skills of over 2.2 million people will be raised throughout the programme in order to create a conducive environment for the adoption of low carbon and climate resilient measures in agricultural value chain development both upstream and downstream.

## D1 Mitigation Impacts (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Mitigation impacts.

In terms of direct mitigation impacts, the greatest mitigation potential from the SCFCA programme under GCF financed activities is in the area of renewable energy and waste management. That is (a) Solar PV and (b) Biogas. The indirect emissions calculation is based on:

(i) avoided emission from the use of climate resilient agricultural practices; and

(ii) avoided emissions from the use of renewable energy sources.

The estimated avoided emissions for the various components of the project, including construction profiles of road construction and rehabilitation have also been estimated and are negligible in relation to the overall statement that will be achieved by the use of renewable energy, CHP and sequestration from agroforestry.

# Potential for scaling up and replication

The Scaling Up and Replication (SUR) programme is an agricultural transformation agenda of the AGRA and its RBM under its Feed Africa Initiative, aimed at boosting national productivity and integrating processing, production and marketing of crops into global markets. The SUR programme has been piloted in Burkina Faso, Côte d'Ivoire, Mali, Niger, Senegal and Guinea with the expectation of scaling up and replication in other regional member countries (RMCs). Presentations on the SUR programme have been made to various stakeholders including government officials at the request of the various Governments for the implementation of Phase II of the SCPPZ programme in these countries. This note provides an overview of the SUR programme, its objectives, activities and expected outcomes.

To this note, a number of strategies have been built into Phase I of the programme with respect to scaling up and replication. These include: (i) the establishment of a Scaling Up and Replication Unit within the SCPPZ Secretariat; (ii) the inclusion of a Training of Trainers (ToT) across all programme interventions, especially for the GFC Financed components that can provide hands-on experience during the implementation phase of the programme. The ToTs are international in nature to facilitate the replication of similar activities, especially Phase II of the programme. Third, several training workshops have been organized and will continue to be organized by the SCPPZ Secretariat with the participation of key stakeholders in the selection of programme beneficiaries; a strategy that has proven very useful in many targeted interventions. Fourth, the establishment of sub-project management committees for each activity in Phase II, such as DDIS, Bogas and Solar technologies etc. It is highly expected that the findings of the committee will generate new ideas and innovations to be shared with other countries. Fifth, the SCPPZ Secretariat has developed a project model employed in Phase I; that is, a concept of semi-autonomy of the TPIFFs, and allowing the management of the projects by the TPIFFs themselves. This model will be used as a basis for scaling up and replication with engagement of the private sector in Phase II.

Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 70

## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The program will contribute towards building in the private sector, particularly potential agribusiness ventures. This will help in creating an enabling environment for 'crowding-in' additional private sector investment into climate-resilient agriculture and low-carbon emission technologies. The program will also support the development of a pool of technical personnel that will enable the sustainability of the technology even after the completion of the project. Also, the program will contribute to building the capacity of women-led agribusiness ventures in these countries. With affordable access to finance to invest in CRA practices, women will be better positioned to lead climate change adaptation in these vulnerable communities.

## Contribution to the regulatory frameworks and policies

Some of the key challenges that are faced in mainstreaming mitigation and adaptation policies into development planning include:

- **Absence of a unified vision and approach**
- **Lack of comprehensive and localized risk and vulnerability assessments**
- **Limited availability of reliable, useful and usable climate change information:**
  - Limited strong link between indigenous communities and other local partners
  - Limited financial resources to mainstream and manage climate risks and adaptation:
    - Lack of adequate institutional capacity

The program will contribute to reducing the challenges and thereby contributing to the regulatory frameworks and policies through the following:

- **Enhancing access to climate education, training and awareness of climate change and its impacts to speed up implementation of response actions:**
  - Empowering all stakeholders to climate change response to facilitate coordinated national programs
- **Increasing the engagement of all stakeholders to achieve the objectives of energy efficiency strategies to promote a sustainable development agenda.**
- **Integrating science into decision making, improving information about risks and opportunities, and promoting collaboration among stakeholders, among others:**
  - Improving access to climate change data, infrastructure to support multi-sectoral platforms and interorganizational networks, and adequate resources

## D3. Sustainable development (max: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

As illustrated in Table D.2.1 above, the program is well aligned to national priorities and strategies for adaptation to climate change. It is therefore expected to generate important economic, environmental and social co-benefits at all levels.

### Environmental co-benefits

The environmental benefits that will accrue from the project include:

- The reduction of 16.9 Mt CO2 eq. of greenhouse gas emissions during programme lifespan;
- Increased soil organic carbon content in agricultural soils through improved crop rotation practices and intercropping;
- Improvements to soil fertility for 31,179 ha of farmland.
- Agricultural productivity increases by up to 50% on average.

### Social co-benefits including health impacts

The social benefits that will accrue from the project will include:

- Improved access to clean water and sanitation for over 2.6 million people;
- Improved access to 37MW of renewable energy which will translate to significant knock-on socio-economic and environmental benefits, such as improved air quality in urban areas and reduced greenhouse gas emissions among others.
- Renewable energy creation as labour opportunities will increase by as much as 20%;
- Improved nutrition security through the increased availability of nutrient-rich diverse foods throughout the year;
- Sustainable food production systems that are more resilient to climate change, including the use of local seed varieties and other genetic resources, while increasing the control of consumers on how they feed themselves;
- Increased access to markets for smallholder farmers in the SCP2 target areas through improved market spaces for challenging barriers, like patrilineal values, and recognizing the role of women and disadvantage people.

### Economic co-benefits

The project will provide economic activities both for the small holder farmers and processing sector in the SCP2 that will be targeted. On average, at least 50% increase in agricultural yield, which will result to a 40% income for smallholder farmers (target of 46% men; 54% women);
- Increased employment or at least 100,000 direct beneficiaries including over 48 million indirect beneficiaries from climate change work;
- Improved access to renewable energy from low-cost sources;

## Gender-sensitive development impact

### Main project activities and expected outcomes

- Over 1 million hectares created for ORA and agroforestry practices, biogas generation process targeting 46% men and 54% women;
- Financial support to over 2.3 million smallholder farmers (targeting 50% men and 50% women);
- Job creation for women that empowers them and increases their participation in household decision-making.

### Target groups

- Smallholder farmers, particularly women.
- Women who are not currently involved in agricultural activities but have the potential to participate and benefit from this project's activities.


## D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### Vulnerability of the country and/or specific vulnerable groups, including gender aspects (for adaptation only)

- The food security situation is characterized by high levels of vulnerability in terms of access to food.
- This is not only the type of main source of income but also how sensitive it is to climate change related impacts. The general population is more vulnerable than their male counterparts because of their limited access to land and other resources, which are critical for agricultural production.

### Gender aspects

- Women in rural areas in all countries shows that the majority of household members are engaged in farming and farm labor activities.
- In many cases, women have to bear the brunt of climate change shocks like droughts and flooding typically experienced in the countries. These women, who are more employed in agriculture than men, are also responsible for feeding their families and educating their children at the expense of their education.
- The impact of climate change is not only manifested in the loss of farm income as a result of reduced yields but also in the increased cost of production due to higher input prices. This leads to non-farm income that triggers short-to long-term forced migration with far-reaching implications on labour supply for smallholder farmers.

## Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population

Institutional services and access to infrastructure affect the adaptation responses of households through increased storage capacity, improved water supply systems, better transport facilities as well as finance, information and skills that are channeled to the local economy. Extension services are one of the strategies of transferring knowledge and skills to farmers and other rural populations in order to improve their adaptive capacity. The availability of credit for adaptation, use of technology is one of the most important ways of building the adaptation capacity of communities. Access to information on improved irrigation practices can be an important input for alleviating constraints related to water scarcity. In addition, access to markets and services along the value chain. The majority of households sources of credit in the countries are relatives and friends who can offer informal loans.

A variety of infrastructure can constrain the adaptive efforts of a particular community. Access to road transport, market, education and other public services including health administrative and agricultural offices are among the key infrastructural components that are essential for adaptation. In addition, access to water supply systems, sanitation, energy and integral parts of the adaptive capacity of households or communities to climate change impacts. Table D.4.2 shows the level infrastructure deficiencies in the pilot countries.

### Togo

The average score from the Country Policy and Institutional Assessment (CPIA) of the AIDB and the World Bank has never exceeded 3.2 out of 6 from 2008 to 2016. This score means that Togo is still a country facing conditions of high vulnerability, with very weak public institutions and policies. The country's fiscal situation has been deteriorating over time due to low revenue mobilization and increased expenditure especially in the area of transport infrastructure. As a result, the country’s debt level increased from less than 20% of GDP in 2015 to more than 30% in 2016. The government's ability to service its external debt is therefore very weak; indeed, the country's debt distress risk is high since 2016. To mitigate this vulnerability, the program pursued by the Government and supported by the AIDB and World Bank has focused on improving public financial management and reducing the level of indebtedness gradually from 78.5% in 2016 to 69.9% in 2019 (i.e., below the WAEMU convergence criteria). The government's objective is, however, to reduce this ratio further by 2023 to less than 40%, while contracting any new nominal external debt on non-concessional terms and by imposing substantial supervision to concessional creditors. This will be a challenging task for Togo in the coming years as it will require a fundamental role but public finance, such as climate, will contribute significantly to closing the funding gap.

### Senegal

The average annual trade deficit represented 18% of GDP over the period 2011-2015 as a result of very poor export performances which represented an annual average of -7% of GDP. At the same time, public debt in Senegal rose from less than 30% of GDP to more than 40% between 2016 and 2018 (i.e., above the WAEMU convergence criteria). The government's objective is, however, to reduce this ratio further by 2023 to below 35%, while contracting any new nominal external debt on non-concessional terms and by imposing substantial supervision to concessional creditors. This will be a challenging task for Senegal in the coming years as it will require a fundamental role but public finance, such as climate, will contribute significantly to closing the funding gap.

### Guinea

The average annual trade deficit represented 18% of GDP over the period 2011-2015 as a result of very poor export performances which represented an annual average of -7% of GDP. At the same time, public debt in Senegal rose from less than 30% of GDP to more than 40% between 2016 and 2018 (i.e., above the WAEMU convergence criteria). The government's objective is, however, to reduce this ratio further by 2023 to below 35%, while contracting any new nominal external debt on non-concessional terms and by imposing substantial supervision to concessional creditors. This will be a challenging task for Senegal in the coming years as it will require a fundamental role but public finance, such as climate, will contribute significantly to closing the funding gap.

**Source:** ADB 2016, Bank Group's Country Strategy Paper For Senegal: 2016-2020. African Development Bank, Abidjan, Côte d'Ivoire

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 75

**Guinea**

For Guinea, significant infrastructure spending has aggravated the country's budget deficit. The overall budget deficit (net of grants) deteriorated from 1.3% of GDP in 2016 to over 3.6% in 2017. Equally, significant public investment, financed by concessional loans and grants, has been made in infrastructure projects. In the same period, external debt surged from 9.3% of GDP in 2015 to over 17% in 2017. During the same period, the external debt increased from 18% of GDP in 2016 to 24% in 2017.

**Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity**

As reported in the program feasibility study, the current public financial management (PFM) system in place for the three SCFZPs is not sufficiently robust. The high level of project risk and the significant risks of the project implementation are quite substantial. There is therefore a need for institutional reforms targeted at institutional strengthening of the PFM system.

Secondly, donor funds are usually managed outside the annual budget cycles in these countries. This equally calls for improvements to the current PFMs systems in order to ensure that all projects are properly accounted for and monitored.

Third, climate finance is a relatively new area especially for many of these countries participating in the SCFZPs program. Lack of capacity at national level to manage climate funds is another challenge.

**D. Investment ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

**Existing national climate strategy**

All three pilot SCFZPs are parties to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and have ratified the Paris Agreement. As such, the pilot countries have committed to the implementation of measures to adapt to climate change. By the same token, upon ratification of their respective Paris agreements, they have also committed to reducing their greenhouse gas emissions by 2030 in order to contribute to and conditionally or unconditionally reduced GHGs by 2030 compared to the BAU emissions scenarios. SCFZPs interventions are aligned with the national climate change strategies and policies of each country and support adaptation and mitigation through NAPAs, NDCs, National Climate Change Policies and Strategies, National Communications (NC), National Adaptation Plans (NAP) and other relevant national documents.

**Existing GCF country programme**

Applying to all three pilot countries, the GCF financed projects are located in the adaptation thematic areas: Togo and Guinea are yet to have GCF financed projects that are not city-led. Besides, all of the ongoing GCF funded projects in Senegal are disaster risk-related ones.

**Alignment with national policies such as NDCs, NAMAs, and NAPs**

A summary of each country's alignment to their respective INDC consistent with the SCFZ Interventions is presented in Table D-2 under section D.2.

## Role of Accredited Entity

The role of the NDA is to provide technical input into the draft funding proposal and ensure that the approved funding promotes adaptation to climate change, mitigation and adaptation targets of the country. Also, the NDA is responsible for issuing a 'No Objection' letter to the Accredited Entities once it is satisfied with the content of the funding proposal.

Exchanges will be held with organizations and other relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, women and other vulnerable groups.

| --- | --- |
| The ADB has considerable experience in supporting African countries in developing their agricultural sector. The Bank has a long history of providing support for the agriculture sector in the continent which is intertwined with the Bank's efforts to contribute to the climate change adaptation and mitigation agenda. This is through investments in infrastructure, rural development, and other areas that are critical to the overall operations and functions of the Bank. As indicated in Figure D.5.1, the ADB has departments with complementary funding instruments for agriculture and agribusiness. |
| Between 2006 and 2014, the Bank Group carried out 198 operations in agriculture and agribusiness, amounting to 4.9 billion USD, including 181 of sovereign operations and 17 non-sovereign operations. The bulk of the sovereign projects in this period were financed by the IDA (65%), followed by the IFC (20%) and the MIGA (15%). These projects represented 80% of the total agricultural portfolio. A total of 25 percent (14% of the totals) have been allocated to AGRO- Africa, which is a regional program that supports agriculture in Sub-Saharan Africa. |
| Recent Bank interventions (under the 2010–2014) have primarily focused on building infrastructure for sustainable agricultural development, including rural roads, irrigation, storage facilities, and markets – its area of comparative advantage. The Bank has also been working with other organizations such as the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and others better positioned to intervene in different parts of the value chain. These interventions have been designed to ensure that the Bank can continue to leverage funds from partnerships and trust funds to complement its traditional funding sources in order to meet the needs of the agriculture sector. |
| The Bank has also mobilized resources from global funds such as the Global Agriculture and Food Security Program (GAFSP) and CGIAR Climate Investment Funds (CIF), including the Pilot Program for Climate Resilience (PPCR). In addition, more than 90% of the demand-based loans of the Bank's mandate in agriculture between 2010 and 2014 were allocated to projects that are climate-smart and innovative. ## D. Efficiency and effectiveness | 300 words, approximately 1 page

As shown in Table D.6.1 below, the three SCS2 countries (Togo, Senegal and Guinea are heavily indebted poor countries with public debt burden constituting a significant portion of GDP) these countries have been unable to access concessional financing from multilateral development banks (MDBs). The GCF has provided concessional terms and to impose substantive supervision of concessional borrowing. Mobilizing climate finance will contribute significantly in reducing the cost of adaptation for the three SCS2 countries. As a result, the three SCS2 countries have pledged to undertake GHG mitigation actions conditional upon receipt of concessional financing from the GCF. With GCF resources Togo, Senegal and Guinea can triple their emissions reduction targets as shown in Table D.6.1.

### Mitigation Impact

In Table D.6.1 we present the cost per ICo2e in the SCS2 program. As observed, the estimated cost per ICo2e is about $45.95. With GCF resources, the GCF only contributes $0.06 for every ICo2e achieved, which makes a respective contribution of 39% and 41% in terms of financing the mitigation potential respectively for the mobilized co-financed by ADB and the GCF.

## For the comparative analysis of electricity consumption, solar biomass to electricity, diesel power generators and electricity purchased from the grid are considered. The financial assessment was carried out using a cost of capital of 5%, which is in line with the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA) methodology for the energy sector ESG discount rate. The analysis reveals the following key findings:

Data on the production costs of biogas vary significantly across country and specific system of collection, packaging, delivery and utilization. It's therefore useful to rely on benchmark for prior expected revenue. The analysis used a cost per unit of 10.24 MW of capacity as a surrogate to calculate the expected revenue. IRENA (2017) indicate that the price of producing biogas typically ranges between $5 and $30 per kilowatt hour, with an average of around $16 per kWh. The cost of production for biogas is US$ 0.50 per cubic meter of methane for industrial waste-based biogas production. IRENA also notes that the biogas produced from agricultural residues and livestock manure can be sold at a price between $2 to $3 per cubic meter (2011). bio-slurry is an effective organic fertilizer. The emission impacts from GHGs such as nitrous oxide, are considered in the analysis.

The analysis also considered the potential cost savings from replacing kerosene and wood charcoal which are fossil- and biomass-based cooking fuels with biogas. The results show that both kerosene and wood charcoal based cookstoves and lamps are very efficient. Data on 2019 export quantity (tonnes/yr) and value for wood charcoal and kerosene were obtained from the Kenya Bureau of Statistics (KBS). The average price per tonne of wood charcoal provided in their study the average price of kerosene for rural areas of Kenya that was used for the analysis. As a result, the cost savings from replacing kerosene with biogas is estimated at $105 million over project lifespan and the cost savings from replacing wood charcoal could result in saving about $2.3million every year and over $13 million even at the high ESG discount rate

The annual grid cost savings by undertaking this on-grid solar option is over $2 million with annual emission avoided at 13,731 tCO2e. The annual avoided social costs of carbon by switching from diesel and grid to solar plants for electricity is over $2 million. The incremental costs of replacing diesel with solar PV is a cost that the developer is unlikely to recover in the short term (5 years). This is because the capital costs of solar PV are higher than those for gas generation which is financially viable only at the implicit costs of capital with GCF financing both with externalities and without.

Besides the global benefits from using this low carbon energy, investing in solar energy and biogas also reduces the risk of droughts and floods. This is because these renewable energy sources are less affected by climate change than fossil fuels which are more vulnerable to extreme weather conditions. The use of renewable energy also increases resilience of farmers through reducing the economic risks they could face in the long run as a result of climate change. Solar PV and biogas can be installed at relatively low cost, with minimal maintenance costs and predictable electricity costs in the longer run. In addition to the economic and environmental benefits described above, solar PV and biogas also provide significant opportunities for job creation during construction and operations and maintenance. Particularly for biogas energy systems, the reliance on local materials for construction of biodigesters provides significant opportunities for green jobs in the Programme Countries.

### Climate Resilient Agriculture

In order to assess the impact of GCF support for the implementation of climate resilience agriculture interventions in the SCP2 countries, we have built on previous experience and the literature, and have carried out two types of analysis: (i) a baseline scenario with no intervention implemented; and (ii) an intervention scenario with best practices compared to the current baseline. Hence:

- We have considered cereal, fruits and vegetables, legumes and nuts, roots and tubers, and cash crops extensively in our analysis.
- We have assumed, in a baseline scenario, that yield will progressively decrease year after year, reaching a reduction of 20% by 2050 compared to the current baseline.

We have analysed five climate resilience agriculture (CRA) interventions:

1. Improved nutrient management, and
2. Improved water use efficiency,
3. Improved soil health,
4. Improved pest and disease control,
5. Improved post-harvest handling

We have created a project financing model to estimate the internal rate of return (IRR), net present value (NPV) and debt coverage ratio (DCR). For each of 5 interventions analysed, Cumulating the across increases in productivity as:

The cost is calculated based on the NPVs obtained from the USAID (2017) study and the AFWA funded project.

### For the ESCO financing model, we use a discount rate of 49.75% (6.0% interest and 3.75% fee), which is the threshold cost of capital in order to incentivize ESCOs to venture into providing their services. All assumptions used for the calculation of each intervention are presented in the Excel files attached and can be easily adjusted for replication elsewhere.

Results from the CBA show that the IRR is positive for all interventions with improved nutrient management being the most profitable, followed by drip irrigation technology (DIT) and then rainwater harvesting (RWH). The payback period of each intervention is given in the table below. DIT has a very short payback period compared to RWH given the usual productivity of upscaling and production, and hence revenues. The payback time generally ranges between 1-2 years with ESCO taking profit almost immediately for most crops.

## Drip Irrigation Technology (DIT)

### Concerning DIT, in order to assess the financial and economic viability of drip supported irrigation technology within the framework of the proposed project, a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) was conducted as part of programmatic study. This can be contextualized for use in many parts of Africa to help inform irrigation water management policies.

### The CBA is based on the following assumptions:

- **Project area:** 20 ha
- **Number of farmers involved:** 150
- **Farmer-based associations and practitioners among others, with an overall guide on drip irrigation systems, particularly in terms of maintenance and operation.**

The initial investment for drip irrigation technology is based on a budget of $4,500 per hectare (covering climate resilient activities for women engaged in off-season farming including purchase of drip irrigation kits).

### The underlying assumptions used are:

- Project area: 20 ha
- Total project area: 1.27 ha including 1,463 m² of vegetable plots and 800 m² reserved for nursery.
- The suggested irrigation system used is composed of drip irrigation kits (DIT) technology adapted to the water requirements of vegetables crops in most parts of the Sahel and SSA Togo, Benin and Guinea). These include:
    - A solar powered pump with a capacity of 560 L/min
    - A solar panel with a capacity of 1.2 kWp
    - A water tower up to 5,000 liters of capacity, which must be supplied by a regular water supply such as a borehole or river.
- The following vegetable crops are irrigated during the dry season (onion, tomato, local eggplant, cabbage, pepper, carrot and cucumber).
- **Variable costs include:** drip irrigation kits, solar powered pumps with capacity of 560 L/min, water storage tank and associated piping, labor for installation and maintenance, fertilizers, pesticides and disease control, permanent labor (daily labor), seasonal labor (seasonal labor), and other variable costs.
- Fixed costs include: interest on the investment in irrigation technology, variable costs incurred (8%), fixed non-costs included a flexible maximum annual depreciation charge of 5% and total intermediate consumption by households.

Based on this, a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) was carried out to assess the profitability of investing in drip irrigation technology. The discount rate used for the calculation is 49.75%, which corresponds to the interest rate (6%) plus the fee (3.75%). The XIRR of 86% for the investment with an NPV% of $1,221 assuming a 0.75% interest rate from the GCF cost of capital and 49.75% discount rate applied to the ESCO Discount rate private section), the NPX value drops $337,820 and $248,886 respectively, with a payback period of less than one year.

If the assumptions used for the calculation are applied for the investment, the results indicate that there is strong potential for investing in drip irrigation technology in the three pilot countries. The CBA shows that the project has a very high profitability index (XIRR) and a positive NPV% for farmers especially smallholders' farmers if support were to be provided to increase access to financing, drip irrigation kits, and training on maintenance and operation of the system.

### Conclusion

The results from the CBA show that the IRR is positive for all interventions with improved nutrient management being the most profitable, followed by drip irrigation technology (DIT) and then rainwater harvesting (RWH). The payback period of each intervention is given in the table below. DIT has a very short payback period compared to RWH given the usual productivity of upscaling and production, and hence revenues. The payback time generally ranges between 1-2 years with ESCO taking profit almost immediately for most crops.

## Table D.1 Growth in Cash Flow from Agroforestry Basin Vegetation Calculations

### Agroforestry Management Practices

The economic and financial analysis for agroforestry is based on a per hectare budget of $1,800 each for Casamance, Fouta Djallon, and the Guinean Forest. The study was carried out in three agroforestry practices: cashew, mango (Keitt mango), and cocoa. All prices used in the calculations were based on field surveys conducted by the authors as well as data from the literature.

Three components typically characterized an economic budget for agroforestry practices: 1) expected revenues; 2) variable costs per hectare; and 3) fixed costs per hectare.

### Key Assumptions

* **Cashew:** Plantation lifespan is in 25 years since, after 25 years, most mango orchards are no longer very profitable.
    * Optimal for 10 to 15 years but starts at year 4.
    * Average per ha yield of 18.7 kg although yields of 20 - 30 tons per ha have been recorded in Senegal.
* **Mango (Keitt mango):** Plantation lifespan is in 25 years since, after 25 years, most mango orchards are no longer very profitable.
    * Optimal for 10 to 15 years but starts at year 4.
    * Average per ha yield of 37.8 kg although yields of 60 - 90 tons per ha have been recorded in Senegal and Togo as well as Guinea.
* **Cocoa:** Can be intercropped with okra, cocoa or onions from year 3
* **Cashew:** As for cashew, the optimal age is between 15 to 20 years depending on CRA practices for vegetable farming (if decided).
* **Mango (Keitt mango):** As for mango, the optimal age is between 15 to 20 years depending on CRA practices for vegetable farming conditions (if decided).

### Variable Costs per ha Production include:
- Site preparation costs (land clearing for liming, digging of holes and fencing the farm); costs of fertilizing hectares with MOP and FYM; cost of planting seedlings - Grazed soil, cost of preparing beds, etc.
- Maintenance costs: Fertilizer (October, June – July with nitrogen, Phosphorus & Potash, application of pesticides and herbicides) for cashew and mango (Keitt mango); irrigation water (100% in the first three years); maintenance cost for maintenance; and initial investment in drip irrigation; salary labor for harvesting of fruits.
- Fixed costs per ha: Land rent for 25 years at $3,286.00 before yields start at year 4 (Amre) (2017).
- Fixed costs per ha: Interest on loans (10%); and part of variable costs incurred (9%) and depreciation charges (5%), which is $348.42 prior to year 3 when yield starts.

The ERR is the rate at which the present value of future cash inflows equals the present value of outflows. The ERR for the base case was 12%, 12% Social Discount rate and private sector ESCA Discount rate of 0%. The CBA shows that investing in Mango orchards with drip irrigation is more profitable than other investments such as maize, rice, cotton, groundnuts, ACs and FCA. This is followed by the Kent of Kent mangoes from South Africa which have a high export value under irrigated farming conditions (30%). Please explain how the best available technologies and practices have been considered and applied. If applicable, specify the innovations/modifications/adjustments that are made based on industry best practices.

Africa Development Bank will bring long-term innovative solutions in Agri-business in a climate smart way. The facility will support the development of new business models and value chains that can be scaled up and replicated across the past 50 years on the African continent. These innovative approaches will not only be limited to:

- Country ownership that is aligned with various Governments national plans and strategies on agriculture, climate change, and sustainable development.
- Leveraging the private sector investment by "crowding in" more private sector operators.
- Optimizing the use of existing resources.
- Inclusivity sustainability and partnerships collaboration among the different stakeholders (both global and national) operating within the value chain.

## Agroecological systems: Agroecological systems conserve biodiversity and natural resources such as soil organic matter, water, crop genetic diversity and natural enemies of pests. It equally improves economic stability with reduced input costs and increased spread of market networks, which can be used to reduce dependency on commodity price swings among others.

## Mainstreaming gender: Recognizing these differences the project will seek to understand and respond to the different needs and opportunities that women and girls have in investing in ecological agriculture and clean energy adoption. The program will apply techniques that will continuously empower women and improve their socio-economic status.

## Reduction in Climate change induced migration: By reducing the production risks associated with climate change, the project will considerably reduce climate change induced migration that is very common countries like Zambia and Togo.

Private Sector Waste Management: The project will promote the involvement of the private sector in the management of waste. This approach will involve assisting the private sector to invest in businesses that are based on recycling waste and production of energy from waste materials in different forms.

### Resilience

This programme will strengthen climate resilience and reduce GHG emissions through the following approaches: by increasing staple productivity and value addition, by improving access to markets and incomes generation by facilitating access to credit and concessional financial resources; to smallholder farmers who heavily rely on agriculture


---
**Page 94**
---

There are various initiatives to help smallholder farmers and rural communities adapt to climate change in their selected livelihood activities. However, achieving this is not easy and may not be entirely possible unless key technical, institutional, policy and regulatory obstacles that hinder access to good and effective interventions on land use and forestry, water and soil are carefully addressed. Unless these obstacles are overcome, implementing programmes will remain “business as usual” way in the country. The main stress, are clearly, climate change which only add to the numerous challenges such as increasing emissions of GHG from agriculture, deforestation and forest insecurity, malnutrition and heightened conflicts over resources use.


The Intervention is projected to deliver:
- About 2.9 million direct beneficiaries
- Over 10 million indirect beneficiaries of which 54% are women
- Over 79,000 ha of land made more resilient to climate change
- A direct emission reduction of at least 3.6 Mt CO₂e by the end of the project lifetime from avoided deforestation that combines (i) solar PV for irrigation and crop processing, (ii) replacement of fossil fuel based energy systems with solar and biogas technologies and (iii) afforestation activities.
- Contribute to achieving over 9% of the total emissions reduction of Nationally Determined Contributions in the 3 countries.
- Over 10% increase in agricultural profit for growing food crops, especially during the dry season benefiting at least 75,000 smallholder farmers. At least 2/3 will be female
- Over 40% increase in the yields of maize, soybean, rice, cassava, banana, sesame

## Replicability

Given growing population and increasing economic activity, the governments of the three countries have approved the AICB for GP in other RMOs to help them improve their capacity to develop increasing food security and nutrition programs.

If successfully implemented, farming communities in the beneficiary countries will have access to a range of technologies able to sustainability power their operations. Moreover with current government support, the price of basic commodities on the global market, the demand for modernized and climate-resilient infrastructure is bound to increase rapidly.

## E3. GCF Outcome Level: Reduced emissions and increased resilience (RMF core indicators 1-4, quantitative indicators)

The document outlines how to use appropriate RMF core and supplementary indicators to monitor project/programme progress. It specifies that more than one RMF core indicator may be selected as suitable for a given project/programme. For each of the four RMF core indicators applicable to any given project/programme outcomes, programme-specific indicators should be developed under section 8.5 (programme specific indicators).

### GCF Result Area: Total GHG emission reduction

#### RMIF: Core 1: GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered

**Mean of Verification (MV)**: Reports by an independent specialist as part of the yearly baseline verification and mid-term implementation. This can be supplemented with project reports to project developers and/or communications.

**Baseline**: Reduction of 20% in CO2 eq. emissions from baseline (CO2 eq. emissions at Year 3).

**Target**: Reduction of 4,856,398 tonne CO2 eq. emissions by the end of the mid-term period (Year 10) and reduction of 7,380,000 tonne CO2 eq. emissions by the end of project after year 10.

**Final**: Reduction of 45% in CO2 eq. emissions from baseline (CO2 eq. emissions at Year 3).

### Assumptions / Note

- Implementation targets for solar PV and wind power are met as per planned timelines.
- 90% of Renewable Energy Capacity is installed and dispatching during Project Phase 1, 50% by the end of Project Phase 2, and 100% of Renewable Energy Capacity is installed and dispatching at the end of Project Phase 3.
- 14.8 MWh of biogas per day will be produced from waste across the site.
- 16,143 tonne CO2 eq. emissions during Project Phase 3 at project activities.

## GCF Result Area: MBA1 Energy generation and access

#### RMIF: Core 1: GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered

**Mean of Verification (MV)**: Reports by an independent specialist as part of the yearly baseline verification and mid-term implementation. This can be supplemented with project reports to project developers and/or communications.

- 16,143 tonne CO2 eq. emissions during Project Phase 3 at project activities.

#### Project Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

Technical and Financial Monitoring
Each SOFZ project will have a Project Monitoring and Evaluation Officer (PMEO) appointed from within the Project Implementing Unit (PIU) and charged with the responsibility of monitoring progress by compiling information on physical achievements and financial execution. For this purpose, he/she will have a roadmap with quantitative indicators on Project progress, relating to implementation of the sub-project components. Such monitoring will make it possible to obtain, every six months, the following information for each activity: physical achievement, expected costs, actual costs, differences and explanations, for possible

| **Develop a training manual on project performance monitoring, reporting and evaluation.** |  |

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 109

deviations in information will be used to prepare the half-yearly project progress reports. Moreover, in addition to the Project Steering and Monitoring Committee (PSMC) review work, ADB will carry out two supervision missions per year, to which should be added a mid-term review mission.

Project Evaluation

There will be a two-line reporting procedure. First, project feedback to ADB from the Project Management Unit and PSMC, and the ADB will conduct a bi-annual progress report (BPR) to GCF. Second, ADB will submit to GCF with compliance with the relevant arrangements for project information management (APIM) in reporting and financial arrangements for this framework. The ADB will present an annual progress report (APR) to the GCF during the five-year implementation period. In addition, following the arrangement of the APIM and the FAA, inception report, mid-term and final evaluation reports, and financial information reports (semi-annually throughout the life of the loan) will be submitted.

Project Evaluation

As per all other GCF projects, the evaluation arrangements for this SGDF programme will comply with the related ADB and GCF policies. Both the independent midterm and final evaluation will be carried out by the ADB's independent evaluation unit (IEU). The work of the ADB's independent evaluation work is guided by internationally accepted principles for the evaluation of development assistance, in particular, the Organization for Economic Cooperation, and Development Assistance Committee (OECD DAC) Evaluation Principles. The World Bank Group, Asian Development Bank, European Investment Bank, International Finance Corporation Group (IFC), and the African Development Bank, Evaluation Cooperation Group (ECG). The ADB will comply with the GCF evaluation policy during the execution of the MAE activity.

Audit

Once a year, competent and independent firms of external auditors will verify the reliability of the consolidated financial statements prepared by the PII in each country, and will assess the operation of the internal control system of the entire project. Such firms will be recruited in accordance with the terms of reference and public tendering procedures. The audit report will be submitted to the GCF within six months after the close of the audited period. The ADB will submit an annual financial statement to the GCF each year, no later than six months after the closure of the audited period. Audits will be performed in accordance with the international best practice in audit standards and procedures as recommended by the International Organization of Supreme Audit (IOSA). Consistent with clause 17.02 of the ADB, an audited annual Financial Statement will be submitted to the GCF six months after the end of ADB Fiscal Year

# F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

## Selected Risk Factor 1

### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: Medium
#### Impact: Medium

**Description:** Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

This risk occurs when agriculture is affected by many environmental factors that are often related to the weather, biology and environment including excessive or insufficient rainfall, extreme temperatures, insects and diseases, contamination by poor sanitation, lack of access to clean water, etc. These risks can be mitigated through a combination of measures such as improved seed varieties, pest control and surveillance services.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Introduction and capacity building of small hold farmers in soil and water conservation techniques and provision of small irrigation systems.
- Provision of access to improved seed varieties, pest control and surveillance services.
- Subsidies for the purchase of seeds and fertilizers.
- Capacity building in agroecological systems that provide crop diversification and therefore reducing risks to crop diseases and pests.
- Capacity building in biogas technology and establishing construction guidelines and enforcement processes.
- Provision of credit facilities at affordable interest rates.

On consideration of the mitigation measures by the project, the residual risk is considered to be low.


## Selected Risk Factor 2

### Category: Credit
#### Probability: Medium
#### Impact: Medium

**Description:** Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

This risk occurs when the borrower fails to make payments on time. Crop production is characterized by seasonality, which may influence the specific circumstances of the settlements and the cash flow distribution in a certain period. This risk is influenced by the type of legal transaction, partner’s creditworthiness, settlement and partner financial status among others.

### Mitigation Measure(s)


---
**Page 118**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 111

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, as to what extent?

### Selected Risk Factor 3
**Category:** Governance  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Low  

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

This risk may result from changes in policies and regulations affecting agriculture like restrictions in disposal of animal manure, restrictions in conservation practices or land use, changes in income tax policy, credit policy among others. It could also be due to unforeseen circumstances such as a pandemic or other natural disasters. The risk can also arise out of disputes with other countries, among others. Limited coordination among agencies and stakeholders can lead to inefficiencies in business regulations, environmental protection, and the implementation of policies.

### Mitigation Measures
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, as to what extent?

The two main mitigation options to be implemented are: (a) early engagement (i.e., provision of information and data to disseminate necessary information on policy changes), and (b) awareness creation through well-orchestrated events to disseminate the project and its achievements.

### Selected Risk Factor 4
**Category:** Other  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

Description

It is possible that the programme will be affected by the COVID-19 contact, which could cause delays in the start-up and other activities. The ADB has prepared a comprehensive COVID-19 risk management approach to ensure that project implementation is conducted in accordance with the law and the relevant regulations.

### Mitigation Measures
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, as to what extent?

ADB has prepared a comprehensive COVID-19 risk management approach to ensure that project implementation is conducted in accordance with the law and relevant regulations. In case of any unforeseen circumstances, necessary adaptive measures will be taken (e.g., remote supervision, use of digital tools, social distancing when required etc.).

# Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

Political conflict and insecurity strongly affect some countries in this proposal like in Guinea. Guinea is currently experiencing political unrest. The implementation of the project will therefore be affected.


## Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what extent?

Guinea is currently experiencing conflicts. The regions selected for the programme in Guinea are secured. However, the government of the country have pledged their support to ensure that the objectives of the SCFZ are achieved in the country (refer to the letter dated 20th June 2017). This pledge will be used as a mitigating measure to reduce the probability of risk occurring and increase the impact of the political instability in both countries, the governments remain keen on leveraging the support for the development of the targeted zones.

### Senegal

The project was classified as Category I due to its nature and potential impacts related to the proximity of sites with ecosystems, environmentally sensitive areas, or cultural heritage sites. The project will not have any significant adverse environmental impact on the environment or the people affected by it.

Resettlement action plans have been prepared for the Agro-park and rural infrastructure. Based on the land of land acquisition, a resettlement master plan has been established to compensate affected persons. A resettlement master plan will also be prepared as part of the project. Although it is not a requirement under the Bank's policies, the project team will work with the environment and social strategic measures if CAFI 480 million, which has already been taken into account.

### Guinea

The PDCA has been classified in Category I and all of its formulation stages are subject to a Strategic Environmental Assessment (SEA). The SEA is available on the ADB website (<https://www.adb.org/documents/programme-de-developpement-zones-de-transformation-agro-industrielle-petits-et-moyens-2045-report>).

The project will not have any significant adverse environmental impact on the environment or the people affected by it. The PDCA is expected to provide benefits for the local population, particularly in terms of employment and income generation.

The coastal areas of Guinea are located along the Atlantic coast. Lower Guinea is a major fishing area with important fish species such as the tuna, Patate, Konkouré and others. The natural region of Lower Guinea is also home to the country's main mining centers: the Guinnea Bauxite Company (GBC), SIRICO, TOSCO and the International Copper-Bauxite Company (ICBC).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 114

With regard to potential sites mainly for the PGZA-BK's anchor programmes, in particular the irrigation schemes in Bokito and Kankan Districts covering an area of almost 600 ha located in Kololi rural municipality and the Kapatche plains covering about 9,200 ha located in Kamara rural community provide significant development opportunities for the project. The proposed project site is also located within the boundaries of the 150,000 hectare Monchon Plain and the 6,000 hectare Koba Plain.

Kankan Department, the programme's target area, is located in Upper Guinea. This natural region covers the entire territory of the department and includes savannahs and savannahs, located between 200 and 400 meters above sea level. The Niger River and its tributaries have cut most terraced flatlands into it.

In relation to the programme, large sedimentary plains in Upper Guinea border the main water courses and cover vast areas of land. These are the Bokito plain, the Kankan plain, the Mano plain, the Fouta Djallon plain, and the Burin Tinkpase plains. In Kankan prefecture, the following sites have significant irrigation potential: the Batte plain with about 14,000 ha of irrigable land located in Montserrah rural municipality and the Sonkale plain with 850 ha located in Tiemnoum rural municipality.

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Key Gender Aspects

The project is classified in Category C according to the Gender Marker System. The detailed gender analysis of the proposed project has been carried out including recommended measures to address the main constraints faced by women in agriculture in Togo (See annex B). Some of these constraints include access to affordable financing to invest in agricultural activities, lack of land tenure security, poor access to small water infrastructure for market gardening and horticulture, lack of training on post harvest handling equipment to reduce losses, and poor outlets for the marketing of agricultural products.

At the agricultural value chain, the project will address:

- Situation analysis of Gender Equality in the AIPs. That is mapping of stakeholders and needs assessment (demographic data) of women and men involved in the agro-processing value chain and building capacity through targeted training, mentoring and coaching including involving leaders and men's gender champions).
- Access to land for food production (100 ha): This will be achieved by providing access to land for women in drip irrigation technology powered by solar pumps. This will support horticulture and market gardening of vegetables and fruits.
- Access to solar energy for simple food processing.
- Access to water for livestock keeping and animal husbandry practices (10,000 ha).
- Training and capacity strengthening of women to adopt low-carbon energy technologies in agriculture such as solar cookers and biogas digesters.
- Women empowerment on agricultural value chain development through awareness raising, training courses and coaching.
- Women empowerment to take up managerial responsibilities at the Agro-industrial Apo-Guinea Parks (AIPs).

The budget for the implementation of the gender promotion and women's empowerment component stands at SDR 23.4 million (10%), SDR 93.27 million (75% of OCB Budget) and AIDC co-financing for gender promotion in Togo is SDR 32.84 million.

### Senegal

#### Gender Aspects

The project runs in a social environment where gender inequalities are still alive and accompanied by the migration of men, which reinforces the triple role of women with its confrontations of work overload and poverty. Thus, the project will take into account specific needs of women and girls in the context of their participation in agriculture and other income-generating activities. Women and men of all social categories to the opportunity offered by the AgroPole. The project will be implemented through a gender approach that takes into consideration the following principles: (i) taking into account the specific needs of women and girls in the choice of infrastructures to be financed, (ii) support to women and youth groups, (iii) recruitment of a gender expert in the PGU. Thus, the project would be classified in Category C according to the Bank's gender marker system (Annex A.C).

---

Specifically, at the agricultural value chain, the project will also finance:

- Situation analysis of Gender Equality at the IAPs. That is mapping of stakeholders and needs assessment (identification of target women-owned companies in the ag-processing value chain and building capacity-building exercises).
- Access to finance for smallholder women farmers (SWWFs), WABES, ACCS, and FIBA to invest in agri-processing technologies in such as Biogas digesters to improve staple food crops processing in project areas.
- Access to finance to invest in agro-forestry management practices (10,000 ha).
- Training of women farmers on the use of agri-processing technologies in agriculture such as Biogas digesters to improve staple food crops processing in project area.
- Women's empowerment through value chain development through awareness raising, training courses and capacity building exercises.

The budget for the implementation of the gender promotion and women’s empowerment component in Senegal stands at $852.0 million. GCF financing for that is $32.96 (7.5% of GCF Budget support) and $32.24 million co-financing for gender-related aspects.

**Gender Aspects**

According to the Gender Classification System, the programme is classified in Category 2. The detailed gender analysis and gender action plan are presented as Annexes B & D on average. Gumien agriculture contributes an average of 17.5% to total agricultural production in Senegal. Women represent about 60% of the agricultural labour force but their contribution remains under-recognized, especially regarding capital building, entrepreneurship management. The literacy level of some women is also a handicap.

In order to alleviate these difficulties, the Special Agro-Industrial Processing Zones Development Programme proposes the following actions: (i) job creation for women and young people and access to markets; (ii) agricultural production capacity building; (iii) skill building and training in agri-processing technologies; (iv) awareness raising on gender equality and other issues (education, health etc.); and (v) community mobilization and sensitisation campaigns on behavioural changes regarding birth control and reproductive health, balanced nutrition, family planning, HIV/AIDS, child protection, violence, the prevention of early marriages, HIV/AIDS, literacy management and environmental sanitation.

Specifically, the project will also finance:

- Situation analysis of Gender Equality at the IAPs. That is mapping of stakeholders and needs assessment (identification of target women-owned companies in the ag-processing value chain and building capacity-building exercises).
- Access to finance for smallholder women farmers (SWWFs), WABES, ACCS, and FIBA to invest in agri-processing technologies in such as Biogas digesters to improve staple food crops processing in project areas.
- Access to finance to invest in agro-forestry management practices (10,000 ha).
- Training of women farmers on the use of agri-processing technologies in agriculture such as Biogas digesters to improve staple food crops processing in project area.
- Women's empowerment through value chain development through awareness raising, training courses and capacity building exercises.

# Financial Management and Procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## G.3. Financial management and procurement

The Borrower-Essex's financial management systems are sufficiently sound to ensure that funds provided will be used for their intended purposes in accordance with the terms of the loan agreement. The SCFZ has been focused on evaluation of the financial management capabilities of the CRPs related to budgeting, accounting, internal controls and risk management. These capabilities have been assessed as being adequate to ensure transparent, accountable and efficient control of funds in its place. These measures were incorporated into the SCFZ Financial Management Assessment (FMA) for each country programme.

The Borrower-Essex. As the ADB is accredited to use its internal processes, policies, systems and procedures, the SCFZ has not conducted an FMA as part of the financial management assessment. The EEs have been requested to reduce noted fiduciary risks. A detailed information on the Financial Management Assessment (FMA) of the EE's is available in Section 4.2 of the Operating Manual.

The Bank's Readiness Review Process also ensures that the Borrower-Essex has taken into account results of the FMA and has been stated as presented in Section B.10 of the funding proposal for each country programme.
