# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 117

## Procurement and Disbursement Arrangements Programme

The procurement of goods (including services other than consulting services), works, and the acquisition of consulting services financed under the project will be carried out in accordance with the Procurement Frameworks for Bank Group-funded projects. The Bank’s Procurement Methods and Procedures (BPP) will be used as a reference document to determine which BPP are considered to be the most appropriate. Indeed, following an analysis of the procurement systems currently in place at the SC2 pilot countries, it was determined that the procedures outlined within the framework of this programme, the Bank’s Procurement Methods and Procedures will be used, since a capacity building action plan has been developed for the SC2 pilot countries with the aim to rapidly ease into the national procurement systems following the reforms identified as necessary.

### Procurement Risks and Capacity Assessment (PRCA): The assessment of procurement risks at country, sector and programme level is an integral part of the PRCA process. The findings and the results were used to influence the decision to choose the BPP for all activities planned under the project.

#### Organization of Procurement Implementation: Project procurement will be carried out by the Project Implementing Unit (PIU) in accordance with the Bank’s Procurement Methods and Procedures. In addition, a procurement expert will be recruited to implement the procurement process but also to build the capacities of the PIU staff involved in this process.

#### Disbursement: The disbursement methods to be used to mobilize the project financing are:
- (i) the direct payment method.
- (ii) the special funding/revolving fund method and/or the reimbursement method. Direct payments will be made into the accounts of the SC2 pilot countries, which will in turn pay all funds received from the Asian Development Bank (ADB) to the implementing agencies. The disbursement will be made through a Special Account at the Central Banks of the three SC2 pilot countries, which will, in turn, pay all the funds received from the ADB to the implementing agencies. This account will be opened only after the project has been approved and as a condition precedent to the first disbursement. The reimbursement method will be used when eligible expenditure on all procurement activities is incurred by the SC2 pilot countries’ implementing agencies.

#### Accounting Management: Legal Agreements require the pilot SC2 countries to:
- (i) maintain accounts and books, as well as records of all transactions related to the project.
- (ii) submit monthly financial statements for review by the Bank’s auditors.
- (iii) ensure that all funds disbursed to the programme or project are accounted for in accordance with the administered resources; and
- (iv) implement accounting, administrative and financial procedures documents in manuals.

#### Programme Financial Statements: The programme or sub-projects’ financial statements prepared under the SCP2s programme management shall include:
- For all programmes: i. Statement of Receipts (funds received from the Bank, counterpart funding where applicable, co-financiers’ funding) and Expenditures (expenditures incurred for both the current year and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 118

## Financial Statements and Reporting Requirements

### Statement of Accountable Funds (SAF)
The SAF is a statement that shows separately ADB's funding, those of counterparties and co-financiers, and cash balances.

### Statement of Special Accounts
This statement provides details on the financial statements describing the applicable accounting principles used and a detailed analysis of the main accounts.

#### Revenue-Earning Projects under the Programme
For Revenue-Earning Projects under the programme (in addition to the requirements stated above): i) Balance Sheet: A reconciliation between the amount shown as "received from the Bank" and that shown in the project's balance sheet; ii) Statement of Cash Flows: A reconciliation for all other assets and liabilities of the project at the close of each fiscal year; iii) Income Statement (or Operating, or Income and Expenditure, or Profit and Loss); and iv) Cash Flow Statement.

#### Financial Statements
For Annexes to the financial statements, the following should be included as an annex to the financial statements of all projects under the programme: i) A reconciliation between the amount shown as "received from the Bank" and that shown in the project's balance sheet; ii) A statement of cash flows for all other assets and liabilities at the close of each fiscal year, disbursement, i.e., Special Account, direct payment or reimbursement guarantees, reimbursement methods with those information included in the ADB approval report and the disbursement letter; and iii) a comprehensive list of all fixed assets purchased with project funds.

### Financial Reporting
The SCFDP countries are required to regularly and exhaustively communicate accounting and financial information on the programme and sub-projects under the programme. The information should detail out data for each sub-project, including the amount received from ADB, disbursements made by ADB, and other payments according to the nature of the sub-project, the lending instrument and the country. ADB may not require a standard format for reporting financial information.

### Keeping and Filing of Documents
Each participating country in the SCFDP programme is required to keep up-to-date and preserve programme/sub-project documents and supporting documents, and to make them available to ADB upon request.

### Retention of Documentation
ADB's General Conditions require that each participant country in the programme retain all documentation related to the loan proceeds to enable external auditors or ADB's representative to examine the same at any time during the life of the project.

### Audit
The General Conditions applicable to loan and grant agreements require that all programme and sub-project accounts are audited each year in accordance with the relevant generally accepted standards. The auditor, in accordance with the terms of reference agreed upon by ADB and the country, will be responsible for ensuring that an audit is required, among other things, to examine pertinent documents; review internal financial control mechanisms to identify deficiencies or weaknesses; verify the accuracy of the information provided on the programme/project financial statements and confirm that the funds granted to the project have been used for their intended purpose. Each country must ensure that the auditor's report is submitted to ADB within six months after the end of the fiscal year in accordance with ADB's requirements. In countries where auditing is entrusted to the Auditor General's Office (AGO) under the supervision and management of ADB, the AGO will be responsible for ensuring that an audit is required at programme/project appraisal and during the loan negotiations and the audit conducted in accordance with ADB's standards. The auditor's report and management letter must be received by ADB no later than six months after the end of the financial year. In countries where auditing is entrusted to an independent external auditor, the auditor's report together with the requirements for the audit will be submitted to ADB within six (6) months from the date of submission of the audited financial statements under reappointments to the Special Account.

### Independent External Auditor
In Togo, an internal audit mechanism will be established with the appointment of an Internal Audit Officer who will ensure that all projects are in compliance with the programme and project documents. Also, an independent external auditor will be hired on a competitive basis and in accordance with the terms of reference agreed with ADB. In line with its requirement that the report must be submitted to ADB within six (6) months from the date of submission of the audited financial statements under reappointment of an independent Audit firm, to be selected competitively, the audit will be covered by project resources. External audit is required for all projects in Togo and the auditor's report together with the requirements for the audit must be submitted to ADB not later than six (6) months after the close of the fiscal year. With the case of Senegal, the programme/project financial statements prepared by the Programme/Project Unit (PUJ) will be subject to an external audit by a regional or local auditor of ADB member countries. The audit firm will be recruited for three years based on terms of reference and bidding procedures recommended by ADB. The auditors appointed under this arrangement will be responsible for verifying the reliability of the consolidated financial statements prepared by the PUJ and will assess the adequacy of internal control systems in place to ensure compliance with programme/project documents. The audit reports of external auditors will verify the reliability of the consolidated financial statements prepared by the PUJ and will assess the adequacy of internal control systems in place to ensure compliance with programme/project documents. The audit reports of external auditors will be financed by ADB. The audit reports


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 119

**F**

must be forwarded to ADB each year, no later than six months after the closure of the audited period. Audits will be performed in accordance with SYDACOM international audit standards and the AGB's comprehensive terms of reference which are available on the website.

## Programme Monitoring and Evaluation (MAE)

Simply refers to the process of procurement related MAE throughout the programme (and procurement) cycle. Information to be monitored for the programme and sub-project under the programme will be identified in the Project Implementation Document (PID). The PID will also identify the specific monitoring and evaluation criteria, including the Programme Specific Assessment Report (SPAR), Capacity development as set out in the Procurement Development Action Plan (PDAP), the results of the project implementation review (PIR) and other relevant information such as post-project reviews (PPRs), procurement audits (PA), independent procurement reviews (IPR) and other information that may be required.

In Togo, the programme is also scheduled for implementation over a 60-month period (Table 1.4). The MAE Officer to be appointed from within the PID will be responsible for collecting, analysing and compiling information on physical achievements and financial execution. This information will be used by the Steering Committee to assess progress in programme progress, relating to implementation of the sub-components. Such monitoring will make it possible to obtain, every six months, a report on the status of the project, including an assessment of the reasons for delays, actual costs, differences and explanations for possible deviations. This information will be used to prepare the half-yearly reports and annual progress reports.

Similarly for Senegal, programme monitoring and evaluation will be carried out by the PLU. The PLU will collect, analyse and compile information on physical achievements and financial execution. To this end, it will have a scorecard with technical indicators that will be updated every six months to assess the status of each activity. The scorecard will serve to obtain the following information for each activity and with a half-yearly periodicity: physical target, level of achievement, reasons for delays, actual costs, differences and explanations for possible deviations. This information is used by preparing the half-yearly progress reports and APPs. Furthermore, in addition to the review work of the Steering Committee, the PLU will also prepare an annual report on the status of each activity and the results obtained after having produced IER.

Finally, for Guinea, internal monitoring and evaluation will be carried out by the Programme Monitoring and Evaluation Unit (PMEU), while external monitoring/evaluation will be the responsibility of the Ministry of Planning and the Ministry in charge of Finance. The objective of external monitoring and evaluation is to assess the efficacy and efficiency of the programme implementation. The results obtained from this assessment will serve as a basis for preparing the annual progress reports and APPs. It will be carried out with the involvement of other stakeholders in addition to the Ministry of Planning, the Ministry in charge of Finance and the AGB. This monitoring/evaluation will be conducted on an annual basis.

Following the programme's closure, ADB and Government will produce a completion report within the required time limit.

See pages 53-54 of the Programme Operational Manual (POM) for more details on programme financial management arrangements.

## Information disclosure policy

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information contained in funding proposals. However, certain types of information may be excluded from the programme funding proposal will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board. Such information is considered sensitive and confidential by the GCF. The following types of information are considered confidential: any information that could compromise the confidentiality of the project; any information that could prejudice the confidence one is a exception of this, but the exception should not be applied broadly to one document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

## Summary Table of Countries—Specific Risks and Risks Mitigation Measures

| Togo | Risk 1: Land security measures insufficient to attract the private sector (PS) | Moderate |
|---|---|---|
| | Risk 2: Lack of access to credit and harmful practices of the private sector could limit benefits expected from the project, especially if monopolized by a minority | Moderate |
| | Risk 3: Delay in project implementation due to poor knowledge of AGRO-ECOSYSTEMS (Ecosystem) | High |
| | Risk 4: Lack of Private Sector (PS) | High |
| | Risk 2: Climate change CO2 and harmful population pressure on the environment | Moderate |
| | Risk 3: The expected benefits from the project could be monopolized by a minority (including for local actors) | Moderate |
| | Risk 4: Delay in project implementation due to insufficient control of Bank policies | High |

### Mitigation Measures

- **Mitigation 1:** The SEISA has identified mitigation measures in the project, including the establishment of the new Land Code and the establishment of one window for land tenure in Kara.
- **Mitigation 2:** The SEISA will provide technical assistance to ensure that agroforestry livelihoods should substantially enhance the project effects.
- **Mitigation 3:** Meetings were held with the private sector (including Kaljon Group, key investor), and their needs taken into account: (i) the Project will be implemented in a phased manner; (ii) support for the implementation of NP volumes as agreed by the parties involved; (iii) technical assistance to improve project management.
- **Mitigation 4:** Institutional support and regular training will be provided to the Ministry of Agriculture, AGROECO, and the Borrower (Ministries in charge of Finance/Economy).
- **Mitigation 5:** Substantial institutional support (see action points for AWARDS) will be provided by the project.
- **Mitigation 6:** The SEISA will provide technical assistance to ensure that agroforestry livelihoods should substantially enhance the project effects.

### Risk 1 - Lack of commitment of oversight authorities and/or coordination team and strategic programme implementation

**Moderate**

The programme is one of central government's priorities and was designed on the basis of systematic consultation with stakeholders including representatives of the administration and municipalities concerned.

### Role of private sector mobilisation

**Moderate**

### Slow acceptance by certain stakeholders may delay the programme’s implementation

**Moderate**

The programme was designed on the basis of systematic sensitisation and consultation to ensure fit gradual ownership by all stakeholders.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP219

## Proposal name: Staple Crops Processing Zone (SCPZ): Promoting Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains

### Accredited entity: African Development Bank

### Country(ies): Guinea, Senegal and Togo

### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Significant impact potential with large amount of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reduction and direct benefit to 2,000 beneficiaries in the three target countries, in the context of increasing food insecurity. | Financial management capacity assessments (FMCA) for each Executing Entity (EEs) will be required to ensure that the EE is ready to deliver and recommendations from the FMCA will be taken into account by the Board. The accredited entity (AE) will be obliged to directly support the capacities of EEs and sustainability of financial and financial management capacity. |
| Proposed end-to-end value chain approach that blends support for sustainable production with market access, value addition and income diversification. | Full concessionality to the end beneficiaries should be ensured. The financial exit strategy should be strengthened further through diversified solutions to allow continuation and scaling up of programme activities. |
| Biometric agricultural transition as part of the Food Africa initiative, with great potential for scale up and replication in other African countries with similar technology driven paradigm shift potential. | Significant sustainable development potential: 100,000 ha of farmland and increasing soil fertility on 31,000 ha; expected 50% increase in soybeans and 40% increase in rice yields, and enhanced food and water security. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in the annexure sheet and addendum XA titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendation.”

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment


---
**Page 145**
---

2.1 Project background

In Guinea, Senegal and Togo, the agricultural land use, land use change and forestry sectors are major sources of GHG emissions. The two latter account for more than 80 per cent of the GHG emissions in the three countries. These three countries have also been suffering from the impacts of climate change, including increasing temperature and rainfall variability, which has led to reduced agricultural productivity.

The program, Staple Crops Processing Zone (SCPZ): Promoting Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains, aims at improving food security and resilience within the agricultural value chains in these countries in Africa. Staple crops processing zones are a new type of agriculture-based spatial development initiative to concentrate agro-processing activities and boost agricultural productivity.

5. The project will contribute to a direct reduction in carbon emissions of 678,155 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2e) per year - resulting in a reduction of 40,935,582 tCO2e over the life of the project. This is achieved through: (i) adoption of climate-smart agricultural schemes; biogas technologies; climate-resilient agriculture (CRA); and agroforestry systems. The project will also contribute to a reduction in vulnerability and increase in adaptive capacity by about 1,094,728 direct beneficiaries (including 536,864 women and 557,864 men, of which 50 per cent are women), including the most vulnerable farmers in the countries.

The project area will cover agro-industrial parks in Guinea, Senegal and Togo:
(a) In Guinea, the programme targets two agro-industrial parks located in the Bolke and Kankan Regions covering 110,600 ha.
(b) In Senegal, the programme targets the Gassamane natural region, comprising the administrative areas of Bafoulabe, Diourbel, Fatick, Louga, Matam, Mbour, Thiès and Tivaouane, covering about 251,863 ha.
(c) In Togo, the programme targets the Kara Region, which consists of seven prefectures, namely Assoli, Bassar, Biansi, Danapang, Douélangue, Kérén, and Kouta, covering 165,000 ha.

The total project financing cost is USD 273,918,525, with a request for GCF grant financing of USD 179,791,156 and loans from ADB $94,999,612. The African Development Bank (AfDB) is providing USD 100 million in concessional loan; the World Bank (IBRD) is contributing USD 38 million as a grant; and the Korean Fund is contributing USD 5,000,000 as grant. Additionally, the Korea Fund is contributing USD 5,000,000 as grant; the West African Development Bank (BOAD) is providing USD 126,398,740 in concessional loan; and the Asian Development Bank (AsDB) is providing USD 31,063,900 in senior loans; and the Governments of the three countries will be contributing a total of USD 19,406,059.

The project is submitted under environmental and social safeguards (ESS) category A. 2.


2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Component 1: Strengthening the resilience of critical SAGS value chain infrastructure (Total cost: USD 143,870,000; GCF grant financing: USD 95,600,000; ADB loan: USD 21,000,000)

9. Component 1 will invest in improving the climate resilience of critical infrastructure in the agricultural value chains of the three target countries. GCF funding will be used twofold: (i) to improve access to and adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA); and CRA practices; and (ii) to increase access to renewable energy technology for the processing of staple crops, including agro-industrial parks. The project will be implemented in three phases, financed by AfDB and the co-financers, that will create agroprocessing hubs and agricultural


---
**Page 146**
---

### Component 1: Transformation centres that will in turn establish various infrastructure and facilities to boost agricultural production in the target countries.

#### 10. The component presents clear justifications for GCF additionality in bringing adaptation and mitigation results. The solar-powered irrigation systems are estimated to reduce emissions even at a relatively low level of adoption (25 per cent) by increasing water productivity through improving access to water. The off-grid solar-powered agricultural equipment for processing and storage is expected to increase the income from agricultural activities, especially for women, through diversified livelihoods. In addition, the promotion of biogas digesters will reduce the overdependence on firewood as a source of energy in rural areas.

#### 12. Component 1 will contribute a combination of loans and grants from GCF in the ratio of 25 per cent and 75 per cent, respectively. Low concessional loans will be absorbed by the three sovereign banks (SGBs) to provide credit for smallholder farmers, particularly women and agro-enterprise smallholder farmers. While the justification for higher grants is acknowledged, it is advisable to develop business models for service providers, especially with energy service companies (ESCOs) and farmers, to ensure financial profitability in the long term as an exit strategy.

### Component 2: Promotion of climate resilient agricultural practices and technologies adoption among farmer groups (total cost USD 94.7 million; GCF contribution USD 53.2 million or 56 per cent)

#### 12. Component 2 will focus on promoting CRA practices and support their widescale adoption. In this component, the GCF funding will be focused on responding to the identified climate-related challenges in the three countries, particularly focusing on water stress and heat stress. The broad range of proposed CRA practices and technologies will respond to specific local conditions and include a mix of conservation agriculture techniques, agroforestry, mixed cropping, agroecology, and integrated nutrient management, which are all well-known measures for climate change adaptation in the agricultural sector.

#### 13. The uptake of CRA by local farmers would require appropriate training and incentives to support their adoption. In this component, the GCF funding will be used to finance these activities with awareness raising, capacity-building and institution-building activities for value chain actors. In addition, as a component 1, the programme will support the development of an enabling environment for CRA by providing access to credit on long term and solidify the adoption of CRA criteria. Finally, the programme will also support the availability of early warning systems and climate information services to bridge existing gaps in the climate information and early warning systems in the three countries. These interventions are expected to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers through better informed advisory and risk management services, as contained in the agriculture and food security sector.

#### 14. Similar to component 1, the component receives a contribution of loans and grants from GCF in the ratio of 25 per cent and 75 per cent, respectively. It is estimated that over 70 per cent of the grant will be used for capacity-building activities, awareness raising, and institution-building activities, especially the proposed CRA approaches. The expected impacts are considered significant as they will contribute to increasing agricultural productivity and improving livelihoods. The proposed activities are assessed to be adequate and reasonable to support local communities while contributing to reducing emissions from agricultural and forestry activities.


---
**Page 147**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The project has significant impact potential as a mitigation project, with -0.7 Mt CO2 eq in annual emission reduction and 16.9 Mg CO2 eq over the 25-year project lifespan. These reductions will be achieved through the adoption of soil protectionists and biogas in the agricultural sector, changing land use practices, and improving water management and climate change adaptation practices.

The programme's baseline investment involves road construction and rehabilitation, which may have some negative environmental impacts; however, these are expected to be minor. The programme should carefully monitor and report all possible GHG emissions to be generated during project implementation and take appropriate measures to minimize negative environmental externalities.

16. The project’s avoided emissions and carbon sequestration were calculated using the clear-cutting of 200 ha of forest, the installation of solar installations as well as a combination of existing literature and country-specific data from the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) and the World Bank.

17. The project will have substantial adaptation and livelihood improvement benefits for 1,183,729 people in rural areas, including 540,600 smallholder farmers (54 per cent of them women), who will be supported through the end-to-end farm to market approach that includes access to climate services and capacity development, better climate-responsive practices and technologies, improved energy and water access, as well as diversification.

The AER asserts that, compared to similar projects, conservative estimates were used to calculate the avoided emissions. The project is expected to provide direct benefits to 15 per cent of each country’s total population, depending on the size and number of agro-industrial parks in each country. As access to markets is key to achieving the objectives of the programme, it will be important that the project provides support for smallholder farmers, agricultural cooperative societies, farm-based associations, and agribusiness enterprises.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

The programme was designed as an integrated initiative to build the resilience of staple crop value chains from the ground up. Through this approach, the programme will not only build the resilience of the production part of these food systems but also promote market access, value addition and diversification, and institutional mechanisms to generate a long-term, sustainable transition of agriculture in the targeted areas.

Potential for scaling up and replication: The programme was designed to boost agricultural productivity through improved technologies and practices that are scalable for scale-up and replication in other African countries. The first phase of the Initiative is currently being piloted in six countries (Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Kenya, Nigeria, Senegal, and Tanzania) and other AfDB regional member countries (RMCs). To date, preliminary field visits have been conducted in Côte d'Ivoire, Nigeria, Senegal, the United Republic of Tanzania, and Zambia, at the request of the respective Governments, for the implementation of phase II of the SCFE.

programme in these countries. Hence, the success of phase 1 is highly crucial for the scaling up and replication of the programme in other RMGs.

21. A number of strategies were built into phase I of the programme with respect to scaling up and replication in other RMGs of ABRI. Firstly, the programme was designed to train a "Central Team" that will be responsible for the implementation of the programme in other regions, especially for the GCF-financed components that can easily be scaled up and replicated in other regions. The Central Team will also have the opportunity to benefit from lessons learned on experience as international consultants during the implementation of phase I. Furthermore, for each activity, a detailed work plan has been prepared with specific milestones and deliverables prepared and developed to facilitate the replication of similar activities especially in phase II of the programme. Finally, several training workshops have been factored into the programme to facilitate knowledge sharing.

22. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment: The programme will bring knowledge and experience gained from the implementation of CRA practices by smallholder agricultural businesses investors. This will help in creating an enabling environment for crowding in additional private financing into agriculture. Equally, the project will strengthen the technical capacity of key stakeholders such as government agencies, financial institutions, civil society organizations and other key stakeholders on climate-resilient practices and low carbon emission technologies. The programme will also provide training to local NGOs. This will provide the necessary critical mass of technical personnel that will enable the sustainability of the technology transfer and replication process.

23. Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment: Lack of access to finance to invest in CRA practices has been identified as a major impediment to the growth of smallholder agricultural businesses investors. The programme aims at providing accessible affordable access to finance to invest in CRA practices, women will be empowered to act as key change agents for climate change adaptation in these vulnerable communities.

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The overall assessment of this programme’s country ownership is high.

29 The proposal demonstrates that the project contributes to the countries’ nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and aligns with their climate change priorities and strategies. The proposal highlighted in a high-level manner how and to what extent the programme will contribute to the target countries' resilience building in the agriculture sector and in line with the national development plans of these countries. 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

The overall assessment of this programme’s efficiency and effectiveness is medium-high.

31 The funding proposal package presents a financial analysis for two streams of project activities: (i) low carbon energy-related activities (solar-powered irrigation and electricity from biogas) and (ii) the private sector. The analysis provides an indicative estimate of potential financial values from GCF-financed activities. However, it does not provide a complete economic analysis or a detailed breakdown of the revenue streams presented in the funding proposal, particularly on activities financed by co-finance.

The analysis includes a sensitivity analysis, which calculates financial results using three different discount rates: (i) 10 per cent social discount rate; (ii) 8 per cent private sector ESROI discount rate; and (iii) 12 per cent social discount rate. The results show that the project is financially viable regardless of the discount rates used for agroforestry, and mixed results for energy components. The AE explained that the 10 per cent social discount rate was the standard rate it used for its renewable energy projects in Africa, while the private sector ESROI rate was used to reflect the scarce countries in the African region.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

**Programme background:** The programme is a multi-country programme that aims to reduce the environmental vulnerability of GCF investments within the agricultural value chains in the target countries. It aims to strengthen the value chains infrastructure and governance systems, while also promoting the adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) as the advanced energy technologies such as biogas digesters and solar photovoltaic systems. The expected environmental impacts are a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions through reduced farmland degradation from tree planting activities; improved soil fertility as a result of implementation of agroforestry practices; increased water availability and groundwater quality and quantity as a result of improved percolation and reduced use of agricultural chemicals; and the promotion of renewable energy sources, thereby improving the health conditions of beneficiaries, and increasing food security and nutrition as a result of the availability of staple crops year-round.

**Programme classification:** The programme is classified under the GCF environmental safeguards. The TSC has classified the programme as category I (which is equivalent to GCF category A) due to its magnitude and scale as well as proximity of some of the sites to environmentally and socially


---
**Page 151**

### ESS 1: Assessment and Management of Environmental Risks and Impacts

The ESMSs presented the planned interventions under the programme and their general locations, and identified environmental risks and impacts associated with them. The key environmental and social management measures have also been identified. Key country policies, laws, regulations, and standards relevant to the project are described. The safeguards of ADB and the ESS standards of GCF were also presented. The ESMS indicated that each of the interventions would be subject to a detailed environmental and social assessment as part of the required environmental and social assessments based on the environmental impact assessment system of the host country. They also outlined the detailed steps involved from screening to evaluation, including the identification of potential adverse impacts; the formulation of mitigation measures for significant impacts; risk; the process for assessing and managing subprojects/activities with significant risks; the process for developing safeguard measures and capacity-building for the implementation of safeguard measures. The component activities of this programme are subject to the environmental impact assessment system requirements.

### ESS 2: Labour and Working Conditions

The programme will involve significant construction activities in the selected agro processing zones, including the support infrastructure (i.e., power, roads, etc.). The operations of the processing plants and associated facilities will also be labour-intensive. Although the health and safety aspects covered under Operational Safeguard OS3 (Labour conditions, health and safety) of ADB; there is a need to ensure that the environmental impact assessment system requirements for this subject is addressed. The environmental guidelines for contractors and environmental clauses in contracts should include provisions for compliance not only on occupational health and safety but also on environmental protection. The potential risks associated with the implementation of subjects/component activities will cover risks of violation of workers’ rights in the facilities, including child labour, forced labour, bonded labour, etc., as well as other violations such as others. Considering the potential for labour issues (e.g., child labour/fostered labour) in the solar photovoltaic power generation projects and the need to ensure that these are addressed in the implementation of the programme; and that measures are incorporated in the management plan to address them, the ESMSs indicated that the following will be included: the development and implementation of a workforce management plan; the inclusion of environmental clauses in contracts with contractors; the inclusion of a work code of good conduct to be signed by all workers; in the contracts with companies in charge of the works; and ensuring that there are no child/fostered labour issues throughout programme implementation.

### ESS 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

The requirements under ESS 3 are covered under Operational Safeguard OS4 (Pollution from Hazardous Materials and Resources) of ADB. The ESMS provided brief background information on the pollution control laws and regulations and the prevailing pollution and resource efficiency issues in the agro processing zones. The environmental risks and impacts covered by the programme include salinization of groundwater due to saltwater intrusion (Senegal and Guinea); soil erosion due to deforestation, poor land use practices, inappropriate cultivation, erosive farming practices (slash and burn) and from mining; water pollution from industrial activities and mining (Guinea); inadequate waste management (Guinea) and lack of sanitation


---
**Page 152**
---

facilities, air pollution in urban areas due to vehicular and industrial emissions and burning of wastes, and threat to soil and water from use of fertilizers and pesticides. The potential negative impacts relating to ESS are mainly from the construction and operation of infrastructure such as roads, bridges, and buildings, which may lead to increased traffic accidents. Other potential handling/processing facilities as well as the establishment and maintenance of agricultural farms, agroforestry and silvicultural farms, waste management systems, and community forests.

It is important that the screening and assessment of subjects under the programme should ensure that such negative impacts are adequately mitigated. The operations of processing facilities will likely generate wastes (e.g. rejected seeds, plant residues, and other agricultural products and emissions coming from biogas digesters). If not properly handled and/or treated, these wastes may contaminate surface and groundwater and could generate objectionable odour nuisance to nearby communities. It is also important that the use of pesticides will increase under the programme given the prevalence of pests and diseases in the target areas. The EMSPs should ensure that an integrated pest management approach will be implemented in the control of pests and diseases and should train farmers on proper handling and storage of pesticides.

The EMSPs provided measures on public health issues, however, it is understood that such measures are likely to be insufficient and will have to be screened and assessed for these issues. It is therefore important that the screening and assessment of subjects under the programme should ensure that such negative impacts are adequately mitigated.

### ESS & Community Health, Safety and Security
Construction related safety issues, including increased risk of traffic accidents, potential gender-based violence (GBV) and spread of sexually transmitted infections (STIs), were also identified as a risk to the programme's EMSP. The risk of outbreak of vector-borne diseases was also identified as a risk in the programme's EMSP. The EMSPs provided measures on public health issues, however, it is understood that such measures are likely to be insufficient and will have to be screened and assessed for these issues. It is therefore important that the screening and assessment of subjects under the programme should ensure that such negative impacts are adequately mitigated.

The measures envisioned include combating waterborne diseases through intensive information, education and communication campaigns, sensitization of populations on disease prevention measures (i.e. treated mosquito nets), and other health activities. The EMSPs also identified the possibility of social conflicts as one of the potential risks in the target countries. The AR has provided a list of possible conflict situations that may arise during the implementation stage - will be implemented. These will be conducted as part of the environmental and social assessment of the subprojects.

### ES: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

The construction of various infrastructure, such as processing facility complexes as well as the support infrastructure, would likely require land acquisition. The ESA assessment identified "appropriation and involuntary resettlement" as a potential adverse environmental impact associated with the implementation of the programme.

The impacts of land acquisitions for the programme were identified in the ESMSs and may include: (i) loss of access to land resources such as forests and grazing areas, leading to loss of traditional means of livelihood. Physical displacements [i.e., loss of dwellings] are deemed less likely especially in Guinea where most households live in villages or small towns. However, some households active to land-based resources such as forests and grazing areas, leading to loss of traditional means of livelihood. Physical displacements [i.e., loss of dwellings] are deemed less likely especially in Guinea where most households live in villages or small towns. However, some households active to land-based resources such as forests and grazing areas, leading to loss of traditional means of livelihood.

### ES: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Use of Living Natural Resources

Resource managers and safeguard documents noted the significance of forest regions (e.g., the Casamance region) in terms of forest and natural habitat for wildlife. They also noted that the fragility of these ecosystems is due to a combination of natural factors, such as fire and anthropogenic factors, including deforestation due to clearing for crops and orchards, logging and livestock grazing. The ESA assessment identified several potential adverse environmental impacts associated with the programme implementation in the Casamance region: (i) loss of forest cover; (ii) degradation of remaining forests and natural habitats; (iii) development of community forests could also result in a loss of natural vegetation cover.

In the long term, these impacts may lead to increased vulnerability of communities living in or near protected areas, which may put further pressure on the remaining forests and natural habitats. Some of the mitigating measures identified include reforestation to compensate for lost trees; promotion of innovative land use practices such as agroforestry systems; and improved forest management through construction and rehabilitation of temporary quarters. The ESMSs have identified the following as mitigation measures: (i) compensation for loss of trees; (ii) promotion of alternative livelihood activities, including agroforestry systems; (iii) reforestation to compensate for lost trees; (iv) compensatory reforestation and reclamation; (v) restoration and protection of the natural habitat; (vi) improved forest management through construction and rehabilitation of temporary quarters.

A further detailed environmental and social assessment should be conducted and appropriate mitigation measures identified in order to address potential risks on land degradation and habitat loss that may result from project implementation.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES: 7: Indigenous Peoples

Indigenous Peoples may be affected by the programme through direct or indirect impacts. The GCF has developed a set of Indigenous Peoples Planning Frameworks (IPPF) or its equivalent in the ESMS, while the addenda to the GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (ESSP) and the Indigenous Peoples Policy guide the preparation of Indigenous Peoples Plans (IPP). For the purposes of this policy and guides the preparation of Indigenous Peoples Plans (IPP), the following recommendations are provided to cover remaining gaps. As per the Indigenous Peoples Policy, an IPP should be prepared for each project or programme that has a significant impact on the management of land from indigenous populations/focal communities. In annex 23 of the funding proposal, titled “Consultations with Indigenous People,” the ESMS provides guidance and examples.

The analysis reveals several flags and reinforces the ESMS conclusion that Indigenous and vulnerable groups have been inadequately considered in the programme’s design and implementation at environmental and social management plans and IPPs. With no analysis at the ESMS and IPP levels of the potential impacts on Indigenous Peoples, there is a need for further sensitivity analyses, particularly between pastoralists and farmers, with accompanying mitigation measures and recommendations. The AS is recommended to ensure that consultations are undertaken in accordance with the GCF’s Indigenous Peoples Policy and guidance provided by the countries, to ensure their inclusion in the locations where the programme will be implemented.

In this regard, the GCF Secretariat has established a GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AS and the EEs.

### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) 2021/FR, requires safeguarding from SEAH in GCF-financed activities. The AI included SEAH safeguards in the three ESFs for Guinea: South Agropole Project, West Africa Rice Development Project (WARDP), and the environmental measures in the gender assessment and gender action plan (GAP) respectively. The Guinea ESF requires that the AI ensure that all persons working on GCF-financed activities are free from SEAH, which may lead to risks of SEAH in relation to the beneficiary communities. Considerations will also be required for project documents and operational effectiveness plans, which include SEAH, and ensure the protection and confidentiality of the same to all personnel that will be impacted by the programme. In Togo, the programme will facilitate the development and delivery of a comprehensive SEAH policy and action plan. The AI will establish a system in the South Agropole Project will set up a system for reporting, receiving, recording and processing complaints related to sexual exploitation, abuse or harassment (SEAH) by project staff and/or gender experts. The Secretariat recommends that the AE ensure that SEAH complaints are handled with confidentiality and sensitivity. The monitoring of the implementation of the provisions should be as gender responsive and survivor centred as possible, and the monitoring and reporting on the effectiveness of the policy and action plan will be conducted in collaboration with members of the AI aligns with the GCF Revitalized Environmental and Social Policy SEAH provisions and requirements.

# Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and GAP, and therefore the proposal complies with the requirements of the GEF Gender Policy.

### Programme assumptions based on a literature review of available information and consultations on the programme in the beneficiary countries on ESS matters from as early as 2017. It is important to note that the gender analysis undertaken during the preparation of the programme was used to identify the needs and priorities of both men and women.

The programme will be implemented within Guinea, Senegal and Togo, where the programme will be implemented. The legal and policy regulatory framework for promoting gender equality in agriculture varies across countries. In Senegal, there are established institutional mechanisms, including national ministries for women's affairs in the case of Guinea and Togo, with mandates for supporting the implementation of policy frameworks and strategies related to gender equality.

The impacts of climate change have detrimental effects on agriculture, including limiting the capacity to produce food from staple crops. Traditions and cultural barriers negatively affect women's access to resources and their ability to participate effectively in agricultural decision-making. For instance, in Togo, female-headed households have smaller plots than those headed by men, which limits their ability to grow enough food for themselves or to sell it to provide food for their family, thereby increasing their vulnerability to climate change impacts.

The programme will be implemented through a gender-sensitive approach that addresses the impact of climate change by providing agricultural inputs and improving access to markets and modern processing technologies. In Senegal, women's groups are often responsible for managing water resources; in Guinea, women are often responsible for gathering water and fuel in traditional agrarian societies, and these tasks are becoming more laborious, challenging and time-consuming because of distance they have to travel due to climate change. Most of the smallholder beneficiary countries depend on rain-fed agriculture for daily subsistence.

The programme will be implemented through the following components, including indicators with baseline figures and targets, and responsibilities. Sex-disaggregated targets for smallerholder women farmers and female-headed households are included in all components. The programme includes a gender action plan at project implementation level and includes activities that will provide access to programme benefits such as improved access to inputs, credit, training, markets, and other services. These include improving agricultural production, and training in CRA - both for women and men, including female-headed smallholder farmers.

Implementation arrangements include having a gender expert in the project implementation unit of each target country. The gender experts are expected to monitor, evaluate and report on progress towards achieving the programme's gender targets. Financial resources from the overall programme budget have been allocated for the implementation of the GAP.

The AE defines women-led enterprises as businesses that are majority owned by women, led by women, or have a significant portion of women on their board. The AE is recommended to be used in conjunction with other criteria when identifying an enterprise as an enterprise, and this will be used as the criteria to identify that an enterprise is women-led.

The GAP could be improved by including additional activities that can be implemented by the programme, such as training for women's groups on business management, improvement in incomes of smallholder farmers, agricultural cooperative societies/farmer

### Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The programme will provide concessional financing to agriculture activities that will contribute to reducing emissions and improving resilience of ecosystems and beneficiaries in Guinea, Liberia, and Sierra Leone. The AIF will finance this project through a loan from the Governments of the target countries and a grant of USD 78.8 million. The AIF shall co-finance a senior loan of USD 150 million and a grant of about USD 103 million. Other co-financers – including the Korea Forest Service, the World Bank, and the European Union – will provide senior loans and grants. Total co-financing, including the AIF contribution, is estimated at USD 482.6 million.

All countries in the programme are least developed countries. The ratio of GCF grant (≤75 per cent) and loan (≥25 per cent) to be provided will be similar in all target countries and uses proposed for financing. It should be noted that the AIF has not received any cash transfers from USA (African Development Bank Units of Account) 233 million to USD 1.3 billion [USD 526 million to USD 1.8 billion]. The AIF is rated as “Aaa” by Moody’s.

#### Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current programme (medium-high risk)

The ADB, the AIF has a track record of financing feasible projects in different sectors of the three countries. However, it should be noted that the AIF has not received any cash transfers from USA (African Development Bank Units of Account) 233 million to USD 1.3 billion [USD 526 million to USD 1.8 billion]. The AIF is rated as “Aaa” by Moody’s.

As part of the project preparation, a feasibility study was undertaken for industrial development in an EE in each country. A detailed FMCA was carried out by the AE. Apart from the EE in Liberia, the other two EEs were found to be feasible (i.e., full compliance with FMDP) and absent of personnel and processes (Guinea). The assessment of the EE recommended filling these gaps within specified deadlines as condition precedents to the first disbursement from AIF. The AE has not yet provided a detailed plan for this disbursement in the term sheet.

#### Risk assessment

The AE assessment of the EEs reveals that two of the three EEs have capacity gaps in terms of their ability to carry out the first disbursement. The selection and implementation of ESCOs will be carried out by the EEs, and any delays in this process may delay the process depending on the programme's ability to fill the capacity gaps of the respective EE.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

#### AE fiduciary duties

The AE is the AE for this programme. The EEs of the programme are the Government of Togo, acting through the Agency for Promotion and Development of Agropoles in Togo, for Togo; the Ministry of Agriculture, Water Resources, Forests, Environment and Small and Medium Industries, for Senegal; and the Government of Guinea, acting through the Authority for the Development and Administration of Special Economic and Industrial Zones, for Guinea.

During implementation, the AE will perform periodic review of the adequacy of the procurement process and the financial management system. The AE may also conduct on-site implementation missions to assess, among others, the integrity of the accounting system, the efficiency of internal controls, the application of controls, and the smooth delivery of planned financial transactions.

The AE has conducted a capacity assessment of each EE. The financial management systems in place are adequate for the EEs. However, the overall level of fiduciary risks is substantial because of the absence of critical staff, proper manuals, and relevant software. Recommendations for each programme country based on the results of the FMGA were distributed to the AE.

In relation to accounting management, legal agreements will require the pilot SCPC countries to establish a financial management system that ensures (i) compliance with the programmes and projects financed by the resources and administered resources of the AE; and (ii) implement accounting, administrative and financial procedures documents based on the policies and guidelines established by the AE. The EEs are required to submit all funds disbursed to the programme and be prepared in accordance with applicable accounting standards.

As part of the fiduciary duties of the AE, it should review the performance of external auditors once a year upon receipt of the annual audit report to ensure that the work completed complies with the relevant international standards for financial reporting and auditing standards.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting project, the intervention is foreseen to result in an overall reduction in GHG emissions of approximately 130 Mt CO2e over a period of 25 years, while benefiting 1,394,728 direct beneficiaries (indicator 5.6.12.41 indirect beneficiaries). Senegal and Togo are also on the GCF core indicators.

The project’s logical framework explains how changes have been understood to come about. If "then," because logic is appropriately formalized in the diagram provided in the funding proposal. Moreover, the results, outcomes, and interests defined are in a manner that is achievable within the timeframe of meeting the project goal. The social and economic co-benefit identified by the project and will be monitored in its logical framework; however it is not captured as an indicator.

Overall, the logical framework was found to meet the GCF results management framework and performance measurement framework requirements. The final version of the logic framework was approved on 16 November 2017. It includes all appropriate impact and outcome indicators for mitigation, well as the social and economic co-benefit indicators. The project’s logical framework is expected to be a good basis for monitoring.

The logical framework thus expected to provide a sufficient basis for measuring the desired results.

The implementation timetable was provided in a format that would enable progress assessment during the implementation period, with the milestones and deliverables properly integrated into the overall project schedule.

The monitoring and evaluation plan in annex 11 was revised as per the Secretariat guidance. Logical framework indicators were included, and a monitoring budget was allocated. The total amount of resources required for monitoring (including internal and external evaluations) is USD 3.5 million. However, it is not clear how this amount is incorporated in annex 4 [project budget] of the funding proposal.

### 4.6 Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 8 November 2017 and it became effective on 7 November 2018.

The AIF has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme. In particular, the GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in the Republic of Senegal and the Republic of Guinea, countries in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in these countries, which are risks that need to be further assessed.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP219

## Proposal name:
Staple Crops Processing Zone (SCPZ): Promoting Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains

## Accredited entity:
African Development Bank

## Country(ies):
Guinea, Senegal and Togo

## Project/programme size:
Large

### Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1. Impact potential: Medium to high

##### 1.1 Scale: Medium to high

- **Guinea, Senegal and Togo**, which are situated in the Horn of Africa and are part of the Sahel and Sudanese agroecological zones of Africa, are among the countries that are already being impacted by the effects of climate change as a result of historical warming and variable precipitation patterns. These countries have a high level of vulnerability to climate change due to their dependence on rain-fed agriculture which is largely dominated by smallholder farmers who lack access to irrigation infrastructure.
- The proposed project aims to support the development pathway for the climate-sensitive agricultural value chain, and for vulnerable smallholder farmers in these countries through strategic investments and interventions in the entire agricultural value chain from the production of staple crops to their processing and marketing. This will be achieved by component 1 on strengthening of critical SCPZ value chain infrastructure and management governance**, and component 2 on Promotion of climate resilient agricultural practices and technologies adoption among smallholder farmers**.

##### 5. The accredited entity (AE) submitted the original proposal for R29 in 2021, and the current funding proposal is a revised version of that submission. While the overall goal in the current funding proposal remains the same, there are some key changes in the present submission compared with the previous submission, as follows:

- Only three countries—Guinea, Senegal and Togo—are covered. Ethiopia is no longer included.

## Adaptation impacts

5. Under the previous submission for B29, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) assessed that the funding proposal was not ready for endorsement, particularly because the overall climate rationale of the project was found to be weak. The issues raised were as follows:

(a) The individual feasibility studies per country (annexes 2 and 4 of the B29 submission) did not contain a clear explanation or discussion on how the CSA systems would be implemented. These were only presented in the funding proposal (section 5.1.3) and the consolidated feasibility study.

(b) No climate risk and vulnerability assessment (CRVA) had been done to establish the vulnerability context of the targeted beneficiaries and agropreserving actors, critical resources (i.e., water resources, forests, land, soil), agriculture-related built


---
**Page 165**
---

# GCF.08/172/R/A/0010

Page 5/34

## Infrastructure (e.g., irrigation, roads, power, water facilities) and investments (e.g. in crops, livestock equipment, agroprocessing and market facilities):

No water resource assessments had been done, and the funding proposal relied on the availability of water to irrigate staple crops for its success. Moreover, the accredited entity report did not clearly address the impact of the changing climate on water resources;

The funding proposal had a weak basis for selecting climate adaptation measures because it did not provide sufficient information about the project area. It is noted that the method for choosing appropriate climate adaptation and resilience measures to address the identified risks was not clear, and there were no specific criteria or methods used to assess the potential risk of maladaptation (e.g., water over-extraction, forest clearing due to agricultural expansion). The funding proposal also did not provide sufficient information on documentation showing that the selected climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) and agroforestry management practices would be appropriate for the project area. Furthermore, there were no specific CRA and AFM activities to be conducted per project area per season. Specific CRA and AFM activities to be conducted per project area per season were not clearly presented, especially in the budget and procurement plans;

There was a lack of information on capacity building on CRA and AFM, especially for the smallerholder farmers;

Overall, the funding proposal had a weak consolidated feasibility study and country facility.

### Mitigation impacts

The funding proposal seeks to achieve greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation via (a) integration of agroforestry techniques in the agricultural value chains from crop production to agroprocessing; and (b) implementation of carbon sequestration via agroforestry interventions.

The TAP estimates that, if implemented successfully beginning in 2024, the proposed project could achieve a total reduction of 1.5 million tonnes of CO₂ equivalent (CO₂ eq.) by 2029 (year 6), 14,586,726 t CO₂ eq. by 2030 (year 7) and 21,265,777 t CO₂ eq. by 2031 (year 8). This would represent a reduction of 9 per cent of GHG abatement from agroforestry (93 per cent) and the energy and waste sectors (7 per cent). On this basis, the project could contribute to achieving 45 per cent of the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) targets of the country by 2030.

The project’s basic approach to delivering renewable energy services through private sector providers (i.e., energy service corporations [ESCs]) has great potential in improving the


---
**Page 166**
---

# Economic performance of agricultural supply chains in sub-Saharan Africa

The proposed low-carbon technologies for crop production (via solar-powered drip irrigation distribution systems [DIDS]) and agroprocessing facilities (via solar-powered lights, cold-chains and biogas digesters) are expected to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. These technological advances that will, if introduced successfully, reduce GHG emissions and contribute to lower processing costs by reducing reliance on diesel.

13. However, the IAP assessment noted that the road had not demonstrated, other than perhaps for the solar elements, that the proposed renewable energy solutions were realistic and implementable in the context of the targeted areas. The assessment also highlighted concerns: the willingness of farmers to pay for their services and there was no assessment of the chosen delivery channel (i.e., ESCOs) in terms of viability, barriers and resolution of contractual challenges.

14. The previous IAP assessment noted that the proposed GCF investments in CROs and AFM were not yet fully defined and therefore it was not possible to make a judgment regarding whether the targeted farmers were on board, incentivized and willing to participate in and deliver the key outputs under component 2 that would result in high carbon sequestration potential. The new IAP assessment noted that the CROs and AFM activities were still at an early stage of development, and the targeted farmer beneficiaries had yet to be identified and perhaps also yet to be consulted.

15. Moreover, the original funding proposal (i.e., project narrative, monitoring and evaluation, project implementation plans) mentioned capacity building activities, but the budget for these activities was not included in the overall budget. The revised proposal provided a budget for each project component, the capacity-building, training and workshops per country. In addition, the key outputs under component 2 (i.e., CROs and AFM) were not yet defined and the land-use/land-cover changes among the targeted farmer beneficiaries had not been properly presented.

16. In the revised funding proposal, the revised annex 2 (Stakeholder study section 6, approved by the GCF Board on 30 June 2015) noted that the “willingness to pay” issue has been addressed: Stakeholder consultations show that the target farmer beneficiaries are willing to pay for the services provided and are interested in working with the ESCOs service. The new annex 4 (CROs for SCFZ), annex 7 (Stakeholder consultation and engagement plan) and annex 8 (capacity building, training and workshops for CROs and agroforestry) clarified and demonstrated the processes of how the CROs and AFM activities were selected and implemented in the targeted areas.

17. On the basis of the above revisions, the IAP assesses the mitigation impact as medium to high.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: High

The holistic and integrative approach of the funding proposal, if properly implemented, shows a clear paradigm shift potential; a shift that will transform the targeted agricultural areas into modern agroforestry systems with integrated climate-smart agriculture practices. The interventions on the production, post-production, processing and market access stages of staple crops such as rice, maize, cassava, banana, sesame, mango, cashew, coffee, vegetables and, as well as livestock.

### 1.2.3 Transformation of traditional agriculture into climate-smart agriculture

The project focuses on improving farmers’ skills for the production stages through the introduction of selected CRA and AFM practices (component 2, output 2.1). The interventions


---
**Page 167**
---

include an introduction and use of CEFW (component 2, output 1.2) and the integration of climate-smart technologies (i.e., use of OIM) (component 1 Output 1.3).

### 20.
For the post-production and processing stages, the project focuses on enhancing access to reliable and sustainable climate-resilient energy facilities (component 1 outputs 1.2 and 1.3), besides the integration of climate-smart technologies in agricultural value chains (component 1 output 1.3). These service facilities in SCFZs (component 1, outputs L3.). These interventions will be achieved through the development of a comprehensive paradigm shift that would enable agricultural hubs to serve as energy supply centers in the regions. This holistic approach will trigger the shift from implementing agriculture in the normal sense to an integrated approach where it is used as a means for energy production and reduction to processing stages via low-emission, climate-resilient development pathways across the agricultural value chains in Guinea, Senegal and Togo.

### 21.
The project activities are highly replicable and technically well considered they can be aligned to deliver a comprehensive paradigm shift that, if implemented correctly with and by communities, will enable agricultural hubs to become centers of energy supply, thereby enabling agricultural processing hubs to become centers of energy supply, for example for health or education, thereby developing climate-smart technologies in agriculture. The project activities are also designed to create entities that would have a strong motive and technical capability to expand supply to other uses as is already happening with the use of solar power in rural areas.

### 12.2
Repeatability and monitoring, reporting and verification for knowledge dissemination

#### 23.
The project activities are highly replicable, and capacity-building activities will be required if the technology and learning are achieved. The replication of this to other staple crop growing regions and/or other countries will require the robust documentation of the proposed project's implementation progress, results and impacts throughout its life cycle.

### 24.
The IFAD strongly recommends that, in order to achieve the potential replication and scalability of the project activities, a comprehensive monitoring, reporting and verification protocol should be implemented and ensured through regular checks by the Secretariat and through encouraging the dissemination of knowledge.

#### 25.
The funding proposal describes how these specific adaptation measures will lead to increased transformative capacities and not just increased absorptive and adaptive capacities.

### 26.
Risks and Mitigation:
There are substantial risks to achieving the paradigm shift. However, the revised funding proposal has addressed these issues by including appropriate market-based instruments for the development of a comprehensive framework for clean energy ESCOs, and documentation outlining the proposed handling of contracts and potential contractual issues. Although the coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic had already subsided at the time of submission of this revised funding proposal, it is still ongoing in 2021


---
**Page 168**
---

19 pandemic would impact the sustainability of the present and future agriculture business value chain, and this has been integrated into the relevant sections of the funding proposal.

25 In view of the above discussions and balancing the overall impact potential and the risks, the ITAP assesses paradigm shift potential as high.


### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

#### Economic co-benefits
- Overall, the implementation of the SCPF is expected to usher in new economic opportunities benefiting 634,472 people via (a) new and/or improved livelihoods; (b) increased access to markets for agricultural products and more resilient agriculture value chains and the introduction of CBEVs; (c) improved access to and efficient use of critical inputs such as fertilizers, seeds, and irrigation water; (d) adoption of more climate-smart agricultural and AFM skills and knowledge. For the GCF funded components, an estimated total of 291,728 people will benefit from these interventions (see annex 22).
- Improved access to markets for agricultural products and CBEVs within the SCPF will lead to improved soil and local environmental conditions due to a reduction in or avoidance of inputs from fertilizers, seeds, and irrigation water. The introduction of climate-smart agriculture and agro-biodigesters within the SCPF will reduce municipal solid waste and contribute to cleaner air, especially with the displacement of diesel.
- Scaling up renewable energy power generation from renewables in the SCPF could expand over time due to the scalability of renewable energy power solutions at a time when their cost is also reducing. This would lead to improved access to electricity infrastructure (e.g., health centres, schools and childcare) and other important community uses.


#### Gender action plans
The gender action plans (for each country participating) target women farmers and agro-biodigesters users in the SCPF through local civil society organizations, as described in annex 8 of the funding proposal.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium

#### Needs of the governments
Guinea, Senegal and Togo which are situated in the Horn of Africa and the Sahel, are among the major climate hotspots of the continent. As countries in the climate change vulnerable group (CCVG), they have been recognized as vulnerable countries that have urgent and immediate needs to stimulate productivity and add value, increase competitiveness, generate employment and increase incomes for their populations. The project will address these needs by supporting the project targets the critical bottlenecks in the agribusiness value chains affecting these countries. Components of the project include: (a) improving access to markets; (b) increasing yields and improve access to markets via the introduction of DFS, renewable energy power supplies (solar mini-burgers) and addressing/overcoming water related infrastructure needs (e.g., cold storage).


---
**Page 169**
---

warehouse (CF facilities), farm-to-market roads (and irrigation water supply). Component 2 addresses the capacity needs of “value chain agents” (i.e., farmers, but also the staff of project executing agencies, project implementing units and agro-industrial parks) via integration of CCA, AFM and climate-resilient agricultural practices.

### Needs of the farmer beneficiaries and agro-crop processing stakeholders

It is estimated that 80 per cent of the smallholder farmers in the SCPZ rely on rain-fed agriculture for daily subsistence. These farmers are particularly vulnerable to the adverse impacts of climate change, including changes in precipitation patterns, increased frequency of extreme weather events, and reduced access to water resources.

The activities under components 1 and 2 address these needs. The project is expected to improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers by increasing their access to productive inputs and access to resources (i.e., knowledge, skills and training), climate-friendly technologies, CCA and AFM inputs and services, and markets for their agricultural products.

The project document provides a detailed breakdown of the direct beneficiaries of the project amount to 6.9 per cent of the total population in the SCPZ. The number of smallholder farmers directly benefiting from the project is estimated at 12.7 per cent, 19.5 per cent and 14.4 per cent for Togo, Senegal and Guinea-Bissau respectively.

The eligibility criteria for the smallholder farmers to participate in these activities were greatly enhanced, as follows:

- **Activity 1.3.1:** Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2, and activities 2.2.1 and 2.2.2 in summary, as currently configured, the eligibility criteria target and exclude smallholder farmers of vulnerable groups or those who are not land-owning (credibility). For example, the eligibility criteria state a “Preference for agricultural cooperation societies, farmer-based organizations, cooperatives, associations, unions, federations, networks, or other user group, or farmer water user community to promote water harvesting through on-farm catchments and/or rainwater harvesting systems, such as tanks, ponds, dams, etc., or farmers that can commit to contributing to the necessary operation and maintenance (O&M) of rainwater harvesting systems.” This preference is inclusive of all smallholder farmers. Preferences for ACSCs, FIBAs, WABEs, and SHIFIs practicing any form of water-coping strategy are also included in this activity. The same applies to activities 2.1.3 and 2.1.4 (activity 1.1.1). “Capacity to contribute funds either by having own funds or taking loans” (activities 1.2.1 and 2.2.2). “Proximity to local and international markets, transport networks and telecommunications infrastructure” (activities 1.3.1 and 2.2.2) are also included in WABE.
