1. **Country ownership** Scale: High

2. **Alignment with national climate strategy and policies**
   - The funding proposal provides evidence of how the project is aligned with the 2015 nationally designated contributions (NDCs) of each country (Table B.1.3), which stress the importance of scaling up climate-smart agriculture in the energy, water, forestry, and land sectors; the countries' national adaptation programs of action (annex 6) and the Special Agro-Industrial Processing Zones Development Programmes (section 2.2 and annex 2); as well as with its contribution to the African Development Bank (AfDB) Strategy for the Industrialization of Africa (2014–2025).

3. **Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver**
   - The AfDB is the project’s AfDB and the executing entities are the Development and Administration of Senegal, the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock of Benin, the Ministry of Small and Medium Industries of Senegal and the Agency for Promotion and Management of Industrial Zones of Togo. The proposed component 2.3 will enhance project management capacities of these entities to oversee the projects.
   - AfDB has relevant experience and expertise in the sector, as well as in the countries, on the implementation of projects related to clean energy supply, CCA and food security. AfDB has been involved in several projects in Senegal, Benin and Togo, including those in the AfDB flagship programme “Feed Africa initiative 2016–2025” where SCFP takes a major role.

4. **Engagement with Relevant public and private stakeholders**
   - For agriculture-based economies such as Guinea, Senegal and Togo, there is evidence of a strong sense of ownership and serious engagement among the SCFP target stakeholders, as shown in Table B.1.2. The annexes provide further details on the interactions between the project team and the relevant local and national government entities for each country (e.g., Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Energy, Ministry of Environment), and the various civil society organizations, private sector representatives from the energy, agriculture and finance sectors, women’s groups, and centers excellence (universities).

5. **Risks to country ownership**
   - The previous submission lacked a fully documented stakeholder consultation and, from the previous submission, it was noted that there posed a considerable risk to country ownership at the implementation level! However, the revised funding proposal includes a stakeholder consultation and engagement plan (annex 7). This has enabled the current ITAP to be clearly


---
**Page 171**

understanding of relevance and alignment of the proposed interventions from the ground up. The stakeholder consultations show the need and strong support for the project interventions to address the many cross-cutting climate-related issues and challenges that are affecting the targeted beneficiary farmers and agroprocessing stakeholders and enterprises in the business value chains.

The ITAP assesses country ownership for the project as high.


### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

The total project cost of the resubmitted proposal is USD 271,703,525, with the GCF allocation of USD 102,798,000 (38 per cent of the total project costs) in the form of grants amounting to USD 64,200,000 and loans amounting to USD 38,598,000. The GEF, ADB and other co-financiers will contribute USD 149,426,527.95 (54 per cent of total costs), while government in-kind contributions will amount to USD 19,466,000 (7 per cent of total costs).

Overall GCF country allocations are made up of USD 38,998,167.38 for Togo (ratio 1:1.57), USD 26,240,576.57 for Senegal (ratio 1:1.64), made up of USD 160,883,989 for grants (grants leverage ratio 1:1.57), and USD 91,333,527 for loans (loans leverage ratio 1:1.64).

In terms of GCF country fund allocations, 37.8 per cent of total GCF funds are allocated to Togo amounting to USD 38,998,167.38 (74 per cent in grants, 26 per cent in loans); 32.4 per cent for Senegal amounting to USD 26,240,576.57 (70 per cent in grants, 30 per cent in loans); and 29.7 per cent is for Guinea, amounting to USD 30,517,648.04 (80 per cent in grants, 20 per cent in loans).

## Cost per direct beneficiary

Given the project’s target of 1.1 million direct beneficiaries, the estimated cost per beneficiary is USD 245.9%, while the GCF cost per beneficiary is USD 93.0%.

### Cost of carbon mitigated at the program level (GCF)

On the basis of the emission calculations (Annex 3A SCPZ EFA), total emission reductions on the project’s 25-year duration amount to 126,075 tCO₂e/year. The GCF cost is USD 2.16 per tonne of CO₂eq.

### Economic Analysis

The funding proposal identifies the following streams of potential benefits from the mitigation project:

- Reduced use of fuelwood
- Reduced use of diesel
- Reduced use of wood charcoal
- Replacement of inefficient cookstoves with higher-performance but cheaper ones
- Substitution of avoided carbon emissions amounting to USD 31 per tCO₂eq

The cost-benefit analysis primarily considered the fuel replacement at different discount rates: social (12 per cent) and private sector (20 per cent). The analysis demonstrated the need for a discount rate of 18.5 per cent, which is higher than the applied discount rate of 6.75 per cent, which would make the net present value become positive.

In view of the above considerations, the effectiveness and efficiency of the renewable mitigation project are medium.

Overall, the effectiveness and efficiency are medium.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The development of a project focusing on strengthened agricultural production and supply chains and the utilization of renewable energy on both the production side and in staple crop processing is welcome, given that these are both critically important areas of potential climate change mitigation. The project also has high potential for co-benefits with social impacts.

The TAP panel concluded that this funding proposal has addressed the key issues raised in the previous submission by:

- Strengthening the rationale case for targeted project areas;
- Submitting a CRVA;
- Clarifying climate adaptation and resilience measures per project area/country;
- Clarifying the potential risk of maladaptation (e.g. over-extraction of water resources);
- Updating and improving the feasibility study, due to the lack of updated assessments on the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic on the crop production and processing chain;
- Submitting the annexes on stakeholder consultation and management plans;

A. GENDER PLAN OF ACTION FOR TOGO
A1. GENDER ANALYSIS/ASSESSMENT

## Context and Background

The Togolese population is estimated at 7,250,318 inhabitants in 2015, of which 51.4% are women. Togo has made progress towards gender equality in the area of legislative reform. However, the country ranks 134th out of 149 countries in the 2018 Global Gender Gap Report. This low ranking is the result of a number of adverse factors including: (i) the low representation of women in decision-making spheres; (ii) the fact that women are paid less than men ($57 against $54 per month); and (iii) the fact that women have fewer children than men (2.91 against 3.01).

## Legal and Institutional Framework

The Constitution and General Institutions Mechanism of Togo’s laws is a top priority to all international conventions on women's rights and gender equality. At the national level, the general legal framework does not contain any particular discrimination. The Constitution of the Republic of 1992 stipulates equality before the law for men and women in all areas (art.3). It also provides that the State guarantees the right to vote (art.11), the right to development (art.12) and property (art.7). Following the adoption of the National Policy on Gender Equality 2015-2020, the government has taken several measures in this direction including: (i) mainstreaming gender in the electoral code; (ii) The revision of the Code of Persons and Family Law to include a chapter on women’s rights; (iii) the adoption of the law No. 2015-034 of 9 May 2015 of a new penal code with the prohibition of violence and discrimination against women, (iv) the adoption of the law No. 2016-078 of 2 June 2016 on the protection of women from sexual harassment at work.

However, many challenges remain. It is in this perspective that the government, through the Ministry of Social Action, Women and Children’s Rights (MASEF), has launched the National Strategy for Equality and Gender Equity 2019-2028 (SNEG) with the support of the African Development Bank (ADB).

## Promoting gender and women's empowerment

Promoting gender and women's empowerment is one of the areas of intervention of the Ministry of Social Action, Advancement of Women and Literacy. It is led by the Directorate General of Gender and the Advocacy Unit. The strategy for equality and gender equity 2019-2028 (SNEG) has been adopted in 2017 and composed of three persons of high decision-making level, as gender focal point. As for the women's organization, it is a priority to promote women’s empowerment through the implementation of the law since 2016 to work synergistically in areas such as gender-based violence, women’s economic empowerment and the fight against female genital mutilation.

## Governance

The 2012 Electoral Code revised by the 2013 Law has incorporated exceptional measures to improve the participation of women. The guarantee of female candidates for the legislative elections is reinforced with the introduction of parity in the composition of electoral lists. The law also provides in Article 220 paragraph 5 to consider the political will of the Head of State expressed in his speech to the nation in December 2012.

It is also noted a significant advance of the presence of women in decision-making spheres. The proportion of women in the National Assembly rose from out of 8 deputies in 2007 and it went up to 16 out of 91 deputies in 2013. In percentage terms, the proportion of women deputies increased from 11.1% in 2013 to 18.7% in 2014 and 20.5% in 2015. The proportion of women in the Senate rose from 9 out of 60 senators in 2007 to 15 out of 60 senators in 2013.

In 2018, women ministers represent 23.07% against 19.93% of male ministers. In small numbers at the level of the government, women are represented by 4.5% compared to 2.5% for men. According to the gender analysis report of the public administration in 2017, women civil servants make up 18-30% against 80.7 male civil servants.

## Education

The abolition of secondary and primary school fees, as well as the reduction of school fees for girls at secondary level (70% compared to 100% for boys), have greatly contributed to increase the school enrollment rate of girls and achieve parity at the primary level. For example, in 2017, the primary school attendance rate was 98.5%, against 46.3% in 2007. The secondary school attendance rate increased from 50% to 63.4% between 2015 and 2018.


---
**Page 179**
---

# Health: The maternal and infant mortality rates remain, respectively, 401 deaths per 100,000 live births and 49%, in 2014 (EDS III). According to the ESDT survey (2013-2014), nearly 29% of women have suffered previous pregnancy-related complications. The prevalence of unmet need for family planning is high, especially in the North-Western region (Tamougo and of Naopidog) and the Central Region (Cantons of Loma-Tessi and Ki-Kiri) where a prevalence of early marriage is more common. The maternal mortality ratio of pregnancy-related death is almost identical between women aged 15 to 49 years old (86% for pregnant women), whereas it is only 20% for men (PAO, IEDH, 2016: 47-48).

## Employment and entrepreneurship: 74.6% of women of working age (15–54 years) are economically active compared to 93.1% of men. Women play an important role in agriculture (51% of the agricultural population), services, and trade, where they account for 24.2%. Women are also involved in the production of food crops such as rice, sorghum, beans, yams, and cassava. In addition to their work on family farms, women have the obligation to work in the family fields. Women also play an important role in the processing of agricultural products (e.g., pounding, grinding, and drying). The majority of women are involved in the production of food crops such as rice, sorghum, beans, yams, and cassava.

## Women use traditional tools because the processing units still leave behind insufficient material and financial resources required to access research-based technologies is not easily accessible by most of them. In the implementation of PNSAAS, certain technologies have been successfully introduced, including rice straw mulching, intercropping, and the use of improved seeds.

## Women benefit from actions carried out with a view to getting them better organized in associations, groups, cooperative societies, in order to enable them to benefit from technical support. In 2012, there were 8,539 women's cooperatives and associations (ICAT, 2014). However, the lack of financial resources for these organizations receiving support at the organizational level for the marketing of cereals and several actions to make them self-sufficient in terms of production and marketing of agricultural products is a limiting factor. The lack of technical assistance at the grassroots level is a limiting factor. The Institute of Technical Support Advice (ICAT) has only 5.4% of female staff, compared to 2013. In addition, women's cooperatives are often not recognized by the state and women work in these NGOs because of their small numbers in the sector; precarious conditions and socio-cultural constraints. Due to this numerical insufficiency, the supervision of women's groups and individual women is difficult for extension agents. The lack of financial resources for these organizations excludes women, because it is the chiefs of exploitation generally make which shelter the units of distribution of fertilizers and seeds. In addition, the lack of technical assistance at the grassroots level does not get their fair share of advice from extension and other services (seeds and fertilizers) ... delivered by the state. The lack of financial resources for these organizations excludes women because it is the chiefs of exploitation generally make which shelter the units of distribution of fertilizers and seeds.

## At the National Agricultural Training Institute of Togo no specific module on the role of women in agriculture has been developed, although some training modules have been designed to show that only 14% of women work in the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Water Resources.

### Access to land ownership: Only 20% of women between the ages of 45 and 49 retain their land. The 2013 National Census of Agriculture shows that female heads of households have smaller plots than their male counterparts (0.7 hectares for men, compared to 3.6 hectares for women). Women are confined to crop production on small plots of land, which is less profitable and more household consumption (GIZ, INADES, ProDRAC, 2015: 19-20 and 84).

## The project area is one of the poorest, with a visibly important impact on women and children. Women are involved in most of the product lines: rice, soybean, sesame, maize. Land insecurity, access to affordable financing to invest in agricultural production, lack of infrastructure for storage and marketing, lack of technology adoption, poor access to small water infrastructure for market gardening and horticulture, lack of accessibility to markets, low value added products, and poor outlets for the marketing of agricultural products are some of the major problems for women in the project area.


---
**Page 180**
---

The following measures and actions will be taken to boost the role of women and maximize their contribution to the project: (i) grouping of women farmers by food product line (rice, maize, soybean and sesame); (ii) establishment of women industrial promoters in the CTA; (iii) training of women industrial promoters at least for women industrial promoters in the CTA; (iv) facilitation of women's access to modern agricultural production and processing equipment (e.g. milling machines and primary products), processing materials, management of packaging, maize husling); (v) building of the entrepreneurial capacity of women's groups in the production, storage, processing, packaging and marketing of agricultural products; (vi) provision of training on business management, financial literacy, record keeping, accounting, market access and access to financing; (vii) facilitation of access to basic services, such as water and electricity; and (viii) facilitation of access to credit. The budget to be allocated for specific gender promotion and women's empowerment activities is UA 7.2 million. The project is classified under category 2, according Gander Marken System.

B. GENDER PLAN OF ACTION FOR SENEGAL

B1. GENDER ANALYSIS/ASSESSMENT


## Context and background

Senegal's population is predominantly rural (54.8%) and has a young demographic structure (60% of the population are under 25 years old). Women represent 50.1% of the population. The country has made significant progress in gender mainstreaming. However, the World Economic Forum ranks Senegal 94th out of 137 countries on its Global Gender Gap Report 2021. This indicates a large gap between men and women in the country. Some constraints related to the patriarchal nature of society hinder the full participation of women in all spheres of life, including insufficient enforcement of laws in favor of women, the persistence of social and cultural constraints.

It is established that economic constraints and cultural norms prevent women from having optimal access to natural resources and formal jobs, which implies that their livelihoods or the improvement of family livelihoods (nutrition, health, education) are not guaranteed. Women's vulnerability is particularly high in sectors such as agriculture, forestry, water collection, energy, etc. Indeed, women have distinct vulnerabilities, especially when it comes to climate change: they walk longer distances to find food and water, which increases their exposure to violence; they spend less time on education, income-generating activities or participation in decision-making processes related to natural resources management; gender inequalities in the distribution of assets and opportunities (e.g., difficulties in access to land, credit and permits to use forest resources), make women's choices even more limited in the face of climate change.

It is recognized that economic constraints and cultural norms prevent women from having optimal access to natural resources and formal employment, which implies that improving family livelihoods (nutrition, health, education) are not guaranteed. Women's vulnerability is particularly high in sectors such as agriculture, forestry, water harvesting, energy, etc. Women are clearly vulnerable at risk, lack adaptive capacity and are excluded from decision-making processes related to natural resources management. Women's empowerment strategies are directly affected by the upheavals of climate change: they walk longer distances to find food and water, which increases their exposure to violence; they spend less time on education, income-generating activities or participation in decision-making processes related to natural resources management. Gender inequalities in the distribution of assets and opportunities (e.g., difficulties in access to land, credit and permits to use forest resources), make women's choices even more limited in the face of climate change.

Gender inequalities in the region covered by the project highlight women’s vulnerability, which is required to be better addressed through gender-sensitive approaches and the implementation of gender mainstreaming initiatives. Women are still far from the climate change and natural resource-related decision-making processes, which makes it difficult for them to participate effectively in the development of adaptation strategies and difficult goal to achieve, especially in the contexts of highly unequal gender relations. Despite significant efforts made by the government over recent years to promote women's participation in national social development strategies implemented to support or assist vulnerable households. Resilience is inconceivable without rural women.

## Legal and policy framework and Institutional mechanism

In 2001, Senegal adopted a constitution that reaffirms the equality of women and men. Senegal has set important milestones for the promotion of gender mainstreaming in public policies. These include the law on absolute parity between men and women in elective bodies; the Prime Minister's circular letter on gender budgeting; the National Action Plan for Gender Equality (2013-2017); the National Strategy for Women's Empowerment (2014-2025).


---
**Page 183**
---

Strategy for Gender Equality and Equity (SNEEG) 2016-2026 structured around four issues: (i) the enhancement of women's social position and the strengthening of their potential; (ii) the economic advancement of women through the creation of new jobs, the promotion of entrepreneurship, the reduction of duties and women of men and the strengthening of women's access and position in decision-making spheres; and (iv) the improvement of the impact of interventions in favor of gender equality and equity.

The National Gender Mechanism for the Advancement of Women is responsible for ensuring the political and operational coherence of the strategy. The Ministry of Planning, Finance and Economic Development and the advancement of women. The Directorate of Equality and Gender Equality (DEEG) was created by decree of 2016-148 of January 31, 2016. The National Gender Mechanism for the Advancement of Women has been set up at the level of sectorial ministries. The Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development has an operational unit in charge of promoting gender equality and equity in families' organisations and leadership of members of women's farmers' organizations.

Governance

Senegal is cited as one of the top 7 African countries in terms of women's representation in politics: there were about 41.7% women in local authorities and 41% in the National Assembly in January 2018. However, women are still underrepresented at all levels of political decision-making.

Poverty and migration

The incidence of poverty at the national level is on a downward trend, falling from 46.2% in 2011 to 34.6% in 2017. The rural population is more affected than the urban one: 59% for men against 40% for women in rural areas. The project area has a poverty rate higher than the national average, which particularly affects women given their lack of access to productive resources.

Internationally, Senegal's migration rate is 6.1 % in the region and remains the most important of the Casamance regions. The migration phenomenon mainly concerns the class composed of young people between 15 and 24 years of age and that of men than women (11,332 male migrants compared to 9,116 female migrants). Migration of households is also a reality in the project area: 70% of the population aged over 18 years old. The conditions of international migrants are worse-off in several domains of empowerment, including decision-making on production and access to productive income, and access to information.

Socio-economic indicators

In terms of health, life expectancy at birth in 2018 was estimated at 67.5 years, above the average for sub-Saharan Africa (60.7 years). Women live longer on average than men (68.7 years compared to 65 years, respectively).

Maternal mortality remains high overall: 434 per 100,000 live births. There are 459 deaths in rural areas compared to 398 deaths in urban areas per 100,000 live births. In 2017, the infant and child mortality rate reached 56% in 2017. The fertility rate is 4.6 children per woman. In rural areas, a woman has an average of 5.1 children compared to 3.9 for urban women.

Regarding food and nutrition security, according to the Rural Food Security and Nutrition Survey (ERASAN) 2014, rural households, which are food insecure, represent 30% of households (about 153,728 households), or 60% in the region of Ziguinchor. The proportion is even higher for female-headed households, where it is at 43% compared to 29.4% for male-headed households. The regions of Ziguinchor (63%), Sedhiou (52%) and Kolda (52%) have the highest rates of food insecure households.


7

**Page 184**

Nearly one in two Senegalese children aged ten or over (45.4%) can read and write in any language (53.7% for men and 37.7% for women). At the pre-school level, girls dominate with 51.2% while boys represent 48.8%. The rural areas are more disadvantaged than urban areas: girls represent 60.9% of children in rural polarities, 68.4% for primary education. For early childhood education, girls are more in the rural areas (38.7%) than in urban areas (45.4%). Finally, for higher education, women make up only 21.5% of the workforce compared to 39.5% for women. This difference is the result of the low representation of women (27.6%) and their lower level of education. Women are increasingly more disadvantaged than men: 80.2% of women do not have access to it compared to 48.3% for men.

### Employment / Unemployment

The National Employment Survey of Senegal (ENES), conducted in 2015, shows that the labour force participation rate is higher at the national level: it is more important in men (72.1%) than among women (52.2%). At national level, in terms of employment rates, there is a clear predominance of young men (55.2%) over young women (38.4%), and of men aged 20 to 64 years old (69.7%) compared to women (51.8%), the disadvantage of women. The unemployment rate for people aged 15 and over was estimated at 13.7% in the 4th quarter of 2017. Women are more affected (22.1%) than men (6.6%). According to the ENES 2015, the proportion of women employed is lower than that of men: 89.5% for men and 73.5% for women. The proportion of women underemployment linked to women's working time is very high, compared to that of men (40.3% and 20.1%).

In the project area :

- Sedhiou region: unemployment in the region is quite high with a rate of 31.7%, of which 16.5% for men and 12.1% for women, and represents a fairly frequent phenomenon.
- Kolda region: the unemployment rate observed at national level (13.7%) is 40.9% for women. According to residence, it is 42.5% in urban areas and 46.5% in rural areas. The unemployment rate observed is 38.8% overall in the Kolda region: it is 35.0% for men and 53.7% for women.
- Project area: the unemployment rate of the project area is estimated at 32.5% for men and 50.0% for women. The unemployment rate is 26.5%, which is 18.4% for men and 42.1% for women.

### Agriculture

Women are very active in the processing and marketing of food products, livestock and fisheries. Generally, they have a strong focus on family land and subsistence activities. In the informal sector, they are widely represented in small trade (40%) and in services. Women have little access to training and research services, and limited access to credit facilities and financial institutions, particularly in terms of access to drinking water, sanitation, health, transport and communications.

Some of them are involved in export crops, particularly horticultural products (fruit and vegetables) and handicrafts; even if they face significant difficulties related to access to promising markets, information, transportation, storage and marketing. They also have limited access to organic certifications, and promising outlets. They face unfair competition from manufacturers and exporters.

Women are scarcely present in cash crops, except as unskilled labour. The production and marketing of non-timber forest products (NTFPs) is a major activity for women. They use these products as nutritional supplements, for family pharmacopeia but also to generate income.

At the level of agricultural value chains, women are less integrated and play the least skilled roles than men due to their low levels of education and literacy, their low income and limited access factors of production (including land). Women have a strong role in the processing and marketing of NTFPs. They also dominate activities generating higher added value thanks to their strong purchasing power enabling them to buy inputs for their own use or for sale. In contrast, men are more involved than women in agricultural value chains; women are more involved in sowing and transplanting and harvesting, in the


---
**Page 185**
---

processing, conservation and marketing of agricultural products, while men are more involved in the supply of inputs and production (physio treatment, watering, soil preparation, manure spreading)

Despite their poor access to factors of production, women occupy a significant share of the rural labour force and play an important role in agriculture. According to the 2015 national agricultural survey conducted by the National
rained crop survey. 80.7% of farms in 2015 were run by men compared to 19.3% by women, while in 2014,
16.4% of farms were run by women compared to 83.6% by men. This shows an improvement in the situation
of women.

Generally, women generally have little access to land, due to their dependence on men's land management and other productive resources (seeds, fertilizers, loans). In this regards, women farmers organizations often appear as a means of accessing these resources. Women also face difficulties in obtaining credit for seeds and resources. Thus, men control 65.6% of the cultivated areas compared to 6.4% for women, with the exception of rice farming where women control 30%. This access is done directly or indirectly through family members,
indirectly or even through family farm managers, village chiefs and landowners. This access is done through
women are expected to marry and leave the family, the traditional system of land tenure means that giving
them access rights to land could lead to a dislocation of the family's assets.

Regarding access to seeds, women face difficulties in obtaining them due to both their male head of household or because they do not have enough money. According to the national seed reserve, 53.7% of women; 30.8% of men bought seeds without subsidies against 20% of women.

Because of their status as inferior, women have less access to the labour force than men due to the exploitation
of women in the family and the fact that they are not considered as productive agents. Women can mobilize family
labour. This explains why most of them cultivate small areas of about 0.4 ha, while men cultivate an average
of 1.5 ha. The reasons for this difference lie both in the lack of access to land and other resources due to a lack of domestic work and lack of sufficient manpower for tillage, but also because of their poor access to agricultural inputs such as plant protection products (herbicides, etc.) and agricultural equipment.

The project runs in a social environment where gender inequalities are still alive and accentuated by the
mechanisms of exploitation. Women have less access than men to land, credit for seeds and other forms of work overladen and poverty.
Thus, this project will take into account the specific needs of women and men as well as young people and children in order to ensure their participation in the project. The project will also consider the different
categories to the opportunities offered by the Agropolis. Thus, the actions to be carried out are: (i) equity in
the distribution of land; (ii) access to credit for seeds and other agricultural inputs; (iii) support to women's
specific needs of women and girls is the choice of infrastructures to be financed, (iv) support to women's and youth groups to ensure their participation in the project. The project will also take into account the PNU.
Thus, the project would be classified in category 2 according to the Bank's gender market system.

C. GENDER PLAN OF ACTION FOR GUINEA

### C1 GENDER ANALYSIS/ASSESSMENT

#### Context and Background

**Demographics**

With 51.7% women, Guinea's current population is estimated at 11,863,516**, including 1.9 million for the city of Conakry. The rate of urbanization is low: only 33% of the population lives in urban areas and more than 50% live in rural areas. The fertility rate has been decreasing since 2004 (RGPH 2014). This decline was started in recent years, from 3.1 in 1983-1987 to 2.2 for 1996-2004 (RGPHI). It is the result of this strong growth in urban areas and the decrease in fertility rates in rural areas. The average number of children per woman and more than three-quarters of the population is under 35 (77.4%). Life expectancy at birth is estimated in 2018 at 61.7 years for women, compared to 58.8 years for men. In 2014, more than four in ten women (41%) were of childbearing age, or 24% of the total population.

**Socio-cultural data**

The current Guinean population is the result of a deep mix between indigenous populations and migrants who have come in successive waves (RGPHI 2014). It is made up of several ethnic groups that live in harmony. The most numerous are the Mandinka (35%), followed by the Fulani (18%), Susu (9%), Peul, Kissi, Toma and Guerzé. Religiously, there is a high representation of Muslims (89.1%). Christians represent 7% of the population, while other religions account for less than 4%. The social organization customs and beliefs induces different behaviors regarding certain practices such as marriage and dowry. The dowry, sum paid by or promised given by the family (or his family) to the bride's family.

**Legal and regulatory framework for promoting gender equality in Guinea**

The country is marked by a socio-cultural context that is unfavorable to women's rights, with the persistence of traditional practices and customs that are often discriminatory. The legal framework is also weak: only 30% (96.8%, MICS 2016). Guinean customs limit women's power to the administration of the household; to the domestic aspects of family and community management. In this regard, their activities are agricultural production, food preparation, childcare, etc.

Guinea 4th out of 52 countries assessed in 2015**, with customary rules and practices dividing the distribution of wealth between men and women. The country is ranked last among the 38 countries included (DECD SIG Index). Guinea is among the 8 countries (76th out of 86) with the largest disparities between women's rights in the non-OECD area and among one of the ten African countries with the highest gender disparities.

**Legal and regulatory framework for promoting gender equality in Guinea**

In its preamble, Guinea’s Constitution proclaims its adherence to the ideals and principles, rights and duties established in the African Charter of Human and Peoples’ Rights and its additional protocols on women's rights and gender equality (African Protocol on Women’s Rights). The country is a party to all international conventions on women’s rights and gender equality, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The constitution also guarantees the principle of equality between men and women in marriage. It provides that no discriminatory provision prevails relative to the legal age of marriage (§ 280) which is 17 years for women and 18 for men, family authority (§ 324), since “the husband has the right to choose his wife’s profession” (§ 325), the choice of residence (§§ 247 and 331) and that he may object to his wife practicing the profession of his choice (§. 328). In the event of divorce, the court will take into account the wishes of both spouses as well as the age at which the marriage was entered into. The husband’s consent is considered grounds for divorce if committed by the wife. For the husband, it will only be considered a ground for divorce in case of adultery or desertion (§ 340). In addition to the juxtaposition of legal orders (religious, customary and modern), there is a lack of application of the law in rural areas where women are often unaware of their rights and obligations in particular women due to literacy and socio-cultural barriers. Article 1 of the Guinean Land Code allows any individual or legal person to hold the right of ownership on the land and the buildings he owns. But the practices are mainly based on the preponderance of customary law, which is not always consistent with the provisions of the law. The rights of women are undermined by this lack of application of the law in rural areas where women are often unaware of their rights and obligations in particular women due to literacy and socio-cultural barriers.

Policy framework for gender promotion

The Government at the end of May 2018 has only a 4% women out of 33 portfolios, compared to 7 in the previous government. In 2010, the Electoral Code provided for a quota of 30% representation for women in Article 129. The first municipal and legislative elections were held on 5 April 2015. The second round was held on 27 July 2017. The main political parties in the 2014 municipal and legislative election had 22.85% in the Union of Democratic Forces – UDF (369 candidates, out of which 10 are women candidates), 20.5% in the National Front for Democracy and Progress – FNDP (370 candidates, out of which 4 are women candidates) and 26% in the Union of Democratic Forces - UDF (364 candidates, 95 of whom are female candidates). In the 2015 presidential election, out of eight candidates, there was only one woman, a candidate of the Guinean People’s Party. The 2018 municipal and legislative elections were held on 7 April 2018. The parties had a total of a 29,669 councilors for the 342 constituencies in the 2014 municipal and legislative elections, there were only one woman candidate out of every 50 candidates. In the 2018 municipal and legislative elections, of a total of 29,669 councilors for the 342 constituencies in the 2014 municipal and legislative elections, there were only two women candidates out of every 50 candidates. The proportion of women in the National Assembly is 7%, with 8 women out of 114 seats. Women hold only junior positions in the public sphere. There are no women ministers or vice-ministers. Gender inequality, low female literacy and the lack of financial resources of women are also constraints to their political participation.

Guinea has joined the Beijing Action Programme and the 2030 Sustainable Development Programme based on the National Strategy for the Promotion of Women’s Rights and Gender Equality (2015-2024). In 2011 and revised in 2017 it is a contribution to addressing the inequalities that exist between women and men in Guinea. It proposes a systemic approach involving all key sectors and players in the country's development, including civil society organizations.

One can quote without being exhaustive: the National Policy of Social Protection; National Family Policy; National Policy for the Protection of Children; National Health Policy; Agricultural Development Policy; National Strategy for the Promotion of Women’s Rights and Gender Equality; National Action Plan Statement; The National Strategy to Combat the Fomization of HIV/AIDS; The Strategic Plan for the implementation of the African Union Agenda 2063; The National Strategy for the Implementation of the Decade to Implement the African Women’s Decade in Guinea; National Health Development Program; National Research and Innovation Strategy; National Strategy for the Promotion of Women’s Rights and Gender Equality for Youth and Women as part of the Peace Consolidation Fund; Social nets, Basic Education for All Program; Village Committee Training Program; Project Supporting The Promotion of Gender, Rehabilitation Program of Women Self-Production Support Groups.

However, this policy and policy framework would be more effective if a few other reforms were accelerated.


---
**Page 190**
---

including those related to gender-sensitive budgeting and gender cell operationalization within departments.

The Ministry of Planning and Cooperation undertook in 2010 with the support of the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) a study on the integration of population questions, health issues, and gender into Guinea’s policies. Moreover, the extension of the national gender policy as revised in 2017 remains insufficient; its effective operationalization is slow to take place, not only because of budget shortfalls, but also in the absence of a master plan to integrate gender equality with a coherent and realistic budgetary framework.

### Institutional mechanisms for gender promotion

The Ministry of Social Affairs, Women’s Promotion and Children’s Affairs is central to the development, coordination and development of the Government's policy in its areas of jurisdiction. The main mission of this ministry is to ensure that women are fully integrated into all spheres of life by eliminating discrimination from all forms of violence and discrimination, with central and decentralized structures. In addition, several MINISTERIAL DEPARTMENTS have a Gender Cell or a Gender Unit within their structure: i) National Committee for the Elimination of Violence against Women (CENEGV), ii) National Committee on the Prevention of Domestic Violence (CNPDV), iii) National Committee on the Observation of VBG, iv) National Committee for the Abandonment of Female Genital Mutilation/Cutting, v) Monitoring Committee on CEDAW Recommendations, and vi) National Steering Committee for the implementation of the 2017 revised national gender policy. The Ministry also has a Gender Cell within its Directorate General for women’s empowerment and promotion (CAMPFEMEN).

While these structures are important in terms of their existence and the challenges they face, it should be noted that the staff capacity is very inadequate, with most of the original framework positions not being filled, mainly in decentralized structures. The Ministry has a limited budget to finance its activities, which is why research and mobile funding, on the other hand, should be strengthened to enable them to better structure their work. In addition, the ministry needs to strengthen its technical capacity by providing it with adequate financial and appropriate technical and methodological tools to improve their performance. The same is true for decentralized structures that need to be provided with the necessary resources so that the skills development efforts deserve to be strengthened within the Women’s Empowerment and Promotion Branch.

In 2015, by Government Order No. 2015-1257 of the Minister in charge of the Public Service, established a Gender and Equity Service within each ministerial department with the mission of implementing and monitoring gender equality policies and strategies. The operationalization of gender services is done at variable speeds; less than half have dedicated offices, less than 30% have a gender cell or unit, and only one-third have a gender advisor.

Civil society organizations working to promote gender equality and empower women in Guinea play a role of crucial importance for the formulation and implementation of public action. In addition, they are key players in information, awareness, training and advocacy for the ownership of political and strategic instruments to promote and protect the rights of women and girls. However, these structures face challenges; the most important of which are the lack of expertise in project formulation on their part and the absence of a coherent framework that would allow them to participate effectively in decision-making processes. They need support in archiving, funding and disseminating their results. They have difficulty reaching out to women and girls through their networks and they do not have access to training courses and international workshops and forums. A study of training, structuring and logistics needs could lead to an interesting and useful project to build the capacity of CSO networks to promote its kind in Guinea.

# Gender and Cross-Cutting Issues

## Activity and employment.

The Statistical Yearbook (2018) and RGPHS (2014) show that the proportion of the employed labour force aged 15 and above is 63.8% in 2014, of which 61.5% for men compared to 44.5% for women, with large disparities according to the environment: 38.9% for urban and 60.7% for rural people. The net participation rate is 51.7%, higher among men (52%) than for women (51%). For the 15-14 age group, it is 19.7% in urban areas and 38.1% in rural areas). For the 15-64 age group, it is 63.8%, higher among men (78.4%) women (51%). For the 15-19 age group, it is 20.7% for urban and 38.1% in rural areas). It is 51.7% if people are literate compared to 67.3% for non-literate people.

## Entrepreneurship

On women's entrepreneurship: The Global Gender Gap Report 2017 cites Guinea as one of the countries where there has been substantial progress in covering gender gaps in women’s participation and economic opportunities. With a score of 0.813 in this area, Guinea ranks 15th in the world out of 144 countries. Women are more likely to be self-employed than men (27% compared to 19%), especially in the agriculture processing and small business subsectors, but they are often far from providing leadership. In the artisanal mining sector, women represent only 30%, while in the diamond sector, they represent less than 5%. It is estimated that 40% of these women are poor. The average age of these women is 29 years for those in diamond mining and 26 years in gold. 81% of women working in the diamond sector are illiterate, compared to only 37% of men. Women's income from artisanal mining activities is often insufficient: they can be used only to meet the primary needs of their families. They work in very precarious conditions: 7 days a week and 24 hours a day. The lack of access to credit, the absence of informal access to operating sites - inadequate infrastructure; promiscuity; housing offering no comfort.

In general, women continue to face difficulties, the most important of which are their lack of access and control of resources, especially financial resources. Women's entrepreneurship is often limited by their exposure to certain opportunities and markets, limited entrepreneurial, managerial and organizational skills, difficult relationships with authorities and infrastructure shortages, energy and transport deficiencies notably. Guinean women’s entrepreneurship needs a new lease of life to play a leading role in women's empowerment.

## Education

MICS 5 establishes that only just under two in five (39%) know how to read and write. Literacy of young older women is negatively correlated with age: those aged 15-19 (41.6%) are more literate than those aged 20-24 (36%). Among children of primary school age (age 6) in Guinea, only 43% are admitted in the first year and 78% in the fifth grade. The proportion of girls who complete their education has changed positively over the decade, from 75% to 10 to 2015%. In the TBS of primary school girls is still less represented than boys (49%) compared to 63% for boys and 82% for girls. Unfortunately, the number of children dropping out during the cycle increases as children progress through this cycle, as evidenced by the drop-out rate at the fifth grade, which was 27.9% in 2015.

In 2015-2016, general secondary education recorded a gender parity index of 0.74 and a Gross Enrollment

# HIV, maternal and child health

According to the PONDES, HIV/AIDS remains a public health problem with a prevalence of 1.7% in 2015 while the Government's target was less than 1.5%. This rate is almost twice as high among women (2.1%) men (1%). The main reason for this difference is that women are more likely to be infected by HIV than men, and this is mainly due to the fact that HIV testing is mandatory during antenatal consultations. The rate of access to ART in 2015 was 94% among pregnant women and 87% among children under five years old. However, there are still limitations in limiting the spread of AIDS virus from mother to child. The vast majority of women aged 15-49 say they have used condoms as the main method of contraception (71% in urban areas compared to 60% in rural areas). Nearly 43% use other methods as the main method of contraception, and only 28% do not use any method at all. Among women aged 15-49 who know that HIV can be transmitted during pregnancy is 45%, 40% during childbirth and 46% during breastfeeding. The percentage of those who know that HIV can be transmitted from mother to child and that


---
**Page 193**
---

this risk can be reduced if the mother takes special medications is 35%. These figures appear to public and private actors and call on them to intensify programmes aimed at behavioural change, prevention campaigns and sustained education for men and women.

MICS 2016 results that the level of fertility (for the most recent period (4–5 years prior to the survey) is estimated at 4 per 1000 live births. Health risk factors for women remain poverty, inaccessibility of health centers and low levels of maternal education. Despite efforts, access to health services is still marked by a large gap between urban and rural areas, as well as between rich and poor households.

Regarding reproductive health, MICS 2016 found that 31% of women aged 15 to 19 started their fertile lives (20% for those aged 18–19) with at least one live birth before the age of 15. In addition, a significant proportion of women aged 15 to 19 had a live birth before the age of 15 (6% for the whole group and 34% among those who were married or in union). The average number of children born alive is 4.8. The overall fertility rate by age indicates that procreation begins early in Guinea (MICS 2016). Early pregnancy is experienced by the percentage of women aged 20–24 who had at least one live birth before the age of 18, as 36%.

The contraceptive prevalence rate (percentage of women aged 15-49 who are currently married or in a common-law relationship who use (or whose partner uses) a contraceptive method (modern or traditional), is very low. According to MICS 2016, the percentage of women using modern methods was only 37%, while that of women aged 15–19 who are married or in union currently use a contraceptive method compared to 8% of women aged 40-49 years old and 10% for those aged 20-39. The percentage is even lower among women aged 35-39 years old. These figures call for an intensification and better targeting of family planning and reproductive health programs.

Climate change

The main climatic disturbances identified are the decline in rainfall, violent and recurrent droughts since the 1970s, early and frequent floods (Kankan–2001; Boké – 2003; Gouaigui – 2005), etc. disturbance to the rainfall regime is accompanied by a decrease of 4% in mean annual precipitation over the last decade, a decline in forest cover; the decline in agricultural production, the emergence of some diseases, especially in the northern part of the country. These changes have undermined agropastoral populations, herds have been decimated by droughts and floods, and the health of women has deteriorated.

According to Guinea's National Climate Change Adaptation Action Plan, more than 80% of the population lives and works in rural areas. Their livelihoods are increasingly degraded. Thus, all socio-economic groups, which include men and women, are affected by climate change. However, it is important to note that a plurality of actions are planned in the PANA or in the National Determined Contributed Plans (NDCP) related to adaptation to climate change. These plans are not gender-sensitive. In fact, while men and women and people are affected by climate change in Guinea, nor how they are affected by adaptation action measuresmen, even if they mention the low resilience to natural disasters to which the country is exposed (Boké – 2013). The lack of information on the effects of climate change on the livelihoods of men and women people and women especially their vulnerability and precariousness in the face of the negative effects of climate change.

Water resources are thus threatened by the activity of men and women due to deforestation, bushfires, pollution, etc. These activities have a direct impact on water availability and quality. They include: the clearing of forests, the destruction of forest gardens, the silting of the riverbed, the reduction of flow, pollution and the loss of biodiversity. Building the gender capacity of departmental executives in the development of climate change policy instruments and a national study on gender and climate change, including a review of economic activities, health problems in the scarcity and distance from biomass (firewood). The role of women is crucial for the conservation of water resources and the protection of forests. Their participation in these different areas/sectors can and will aggravate the already complex nutritional situation of the country.

# Water and sanitation

According to MICS 2016 data, only one in four households (25%) exists in a hand-washing place. Moreover, among those who have access to water at home, only half of them use it for washing hands with soap or another cleaning product) at the specific site. This proportion is higher in urban areas (21%) rural (8%). In 10% of Guinean households, there was no soap, water or other hand detergents. It shows that in Guinea, the percentage of people who have access to drinking water and sanitation facilities remains low. For example, hospital-acquired infections are common due to practices that do not meet hygiene standards such as poor personal hygiene among health workers, lack of proper washing hands before and after patient care stations. The same conditions for not respecting basic hygiene rules are available to all types of households. Therefore there is a need to educate men and women about the issues of compliance with basic hygiene rules.

MICS 2016 shows that access to an improved source of drinking water is still a challenge in Guinea. Overall, 58% of Guineans have access to this type of water supply. The proportion varies significantly by region: in urban areas compared to 72.2% in rural areas; 95% in large cities compared to 90.5% in secondary cities; the situation in the Kankan region (84.1%) and forest Guinea (83.6%) contrast with the Maroua region (60%) and the Boke region (57%). The proportion of households that have access to improved sanitation facilities is also very uneven by region: 92% in urban areas, 21% of the population does not use sanitation facilities. People who use them most often use pit latrines or shared toilets. In rural areas, only 30% of Guineans have access to an improved source of drinking water and 58% of households have access to a sanitation facility. The proportion is even lower in the Boke region (21%). The use of a sanitary facility shared or by the population is common not only among households living in rural areas but also in urban areas, especially in the Maroua region where it reaches 40%. Members of urban households (49%) are more likely than those of rural households (29%) to share their sanitary facilities with members of other households.

This situation highlights the importance of toilets and places of comfort in women's hygiene. Indeed, it is more than humans, vulnerable to the health risks associated with poor quality water. Its level of requirement on the quality of toilets is higher: cleanliness, drinking water giving the possibility of immediate use, a place for changing clothes, etc. The need for toilets is also linked to life events that can change, bringing a baby... bins where she can eventually throw out diapers or towl. Toilets and places of comfort are therefore essential in women's hygiene. They are also a means of protection against social and sexual violence and call for adequate government action. It is suggested to develop and implement a gender-sensitive approach to the problem, with an important component on menstrual hygiene management.

### Gender-Based Violence (VioGB)

Guinea's context is one of a high prevalence of gender-based violence in a variety of forms. The percentage of women who have experienced physical or sexual violence at least once during their lifetime is 70.1%. Early and/or forced marriages are a major social problem: 54.6% of women aged 20-49 married or were in a relationship before the age of 18. More seriously, 21.1% of women aged 15-49 married or were in a relationship before the age of 15. The prevalence is even higher among rural women (76%) and those with some form of FGM/C: 90% (NISMCs, 2016). The practice seems to be more common in rural areas (83%), where it affects 40% of women aged 15-49. Women living in the poorest households are more likely to have experienced this practice (50%) than their counterparts in the richest households.

The decline in the prevalence of female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM/C) among women must remain a major political objective. The Government, in collaboration with its partners, has made considerable efforts in this direction by ratifying the Protocol on the Elimination of Female Genital Mutilation/Cutting (PEGFM/C) and the implementation of various initiatives aimed at raising awareness and combating FGM/C: the National Committee for the Elimination of Female Genital Mutilation/Cutting (CNEMFGC), and the Office for Protection of Children and Mothers (OPROGEM), with a mandate to combat violations of women's and children's rights and to protect minors. However, understaffing, lack of training for existing staff and failure to take into account violence in remote areas of the country prevent the effective functioning of the office. The Gendarmerie has

In the Directorate of Judicial Investigations but it is located only in the commune of Matam (Conakry), and this service lacks sufficient and adequately trained staff.

Despite legal and institutional mobilization, the practice of FGM/C has increased in recent years. (Report on FGM/C in Guinea, 2016). The percentage of women who have undergone FGM/C is 78% for girls aged 5-9; 43% for those aged 10-14 and 27% for those aged 15-19. The percentage of women aged 15-49 who said that the practice of FGM/C should continue is 67.2%. (MICS, 2016), reflecting some approval for female genital mutilation/cutting. Support for progression is higher among older women and those with more children. ## Support for the development of agricultural and industrial value chains

Guinean agriculture accounts for an average of 17.5% to 23% of GDP over the last five years, and accounts for 52.5% of the working population, 75.4% of which is in rural areas (RGPHS, 2014). It is largely dominated by small-scale farming activities carried out by women. The main crops are rice, millet, sorghum, maize, palm oil, rubber, horticultural products. Vegetable production is the main source of income for 57% of rural people, while animal husbandry provides an average of 40%. Rice is the most important crop in Guinea, occupying 67% of the area being ended, and occupies 23% of primary GDP, 11% of imports and 8% of national GDP. The main crops are rice (59%), millet (14%), sorghum (10%) and maize (10%). Rice production is dominated by small-scale farming activities carried out in the forest zone, forestry and fishing account for 17%, 14% and 4% of primary GDP respectively. The animal sectors concern the raising of cattle, sheep, goats, pigs and poultry. In addition to rice, millet, sorghum and maize are cultivated by small-scale farming activities carried out around fish farming activities. Fish farming generates nearly 10,000 direct jobs and provides 40% of the animal protein consumed in the country.

According to the 2013 INPASA report, agricultural activities are largely carried out by women. There is an average of 144 women per 100 men in the national agricultural population, or 87% of the female labour force. In addition, women represent more than half (56%) of the total number of workers in small-scale and subsistence harvest activities. They devote 80% of their working time to agricultural activities. Despite this reality, they contribute only 23% of the value added from these activities. The reasons for this are varied: on the one hand, despite their relatively low participation in agricultural work, are masters of decisions about land- holding and management.

The Report on Women's Access to Land and Technology Resources in the Republic of Guinea (ICEA, 1998) states that across Guinea, traditional systems provide women with land-use rights, depriving them of the right to sell or mortgage their land. This is particularly true for women who are not married or head of family. For example, in the event of divorce, separation or widowhood, women may lose their rights to the land they exploit. Advocacy initiatives for gender-friendly land reform have not yet produced significant results regarding the recognition and protection of women's rights over land. The share of female farmers among rice farmers remains a reality mainly for men: less than 10% of rice acreage is managed by men compared with more than 95% of areas managed by women (ANRSA, General Report of the Agricultural Survey 2014- 2015, 2015).

Women's illiteracy limits their access to agricultural training and extension services, climate information, market information and credit. The lack of education and literacy also hinders their access to certain opportunities, especially when they take them away from their place of residence (PNG: 2014). According to the National Survey on Gender Equality in Agriculture (SAGE), conducted by the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock (MAGL) in collaboration with the World Bank (2018) indicates that almost all of the agricultural population (95.6%) aged 4 years and over has not been to school. Only 15% of the population, ranging in age from 20 to 79, report having received literacy training at some point. RGPHS (2014) establishes that 82.3% of people without education end up in agriculture.

# Conclusion and Recommendations

While the legal framework for protecting the rights of men and women in Guinea is satisfactory, there are shortcomings at the implementation level: tests to do with socio-cultural factors as well as the polygamy of local orders (religious, customary and modern), in a context where these texts and mechanisms of redress are unknown to the population and in particular to women (due to illiteracy). Thus, women's rights, although guaranteed by law, remain largely unenforced. The lack of awareness of women about their rights is also due to poverty.

The policy and political framework for gender promotion, as well as institutional mechanisms for gender promotion, are consistent and effective. However, ownership and mastery of the gender concept is not certain among all actors involved in the sector. In addition, the capacity of the public administration and institutions at the national, sectoral or local level is not sufficient to generate significant impacts on reducing gender inequalities. The lack of resources and skills gaps are factors that limit the coverage rate and penetration rate of most women's empowerment initiatives.

Women and men are affected differently by the challenges of accessing modern energy sources. In fact, the well-being of men and women is affected differently by the quality of access to energy. This is linked, for example, to the different roles played in the family and society. The same applies to the challenge of maintaining a quality technical plateau in health centers, particularly in the area of reproductive health. In this regard, the gender dimension must be taken into account at all stages of the project cycle with particular importance. The same is true of gender integration at all stages of the energy sector infrastructure project and its implementation. This applies to the selection of projects, the choice of technologies, the influx of foreign workers, the promotion of women's employment and entrepreneurship in and around projects, strategic planning for the development of human resources, the identification of gender-related value chains, the mastery of data on the roles and responsibilities of men and women, on their positioning in the sector, but also to the opportunities and constraints, on the analysis of gaps in access and control of critical resources, their specific needs and priorities and the need for step-by-step approach.

In many programs and projects, gender integration remains marginal and non-cross-cutting, with participation quotas or specific activities, but without inclusion in the outcome frameworks of indicators that can measure progress. The same is true of the lack of a gender dimension in the implementation of the "Gender Response to Regional and National Agricultural Investment Plans to Meet the Zero Hunger Challenge". In fact, there are no gender-sensitive strategies for the development of women's access to important resources, knowledge, opportunities and markets, which contribute to low agricultural productivity as well as food insecurity. The same is true of the lack of a gender dimension in the promotion of women in agricultural activities and ag-food in Guinea are non-existent. Thus, there is no reliable and up-to-date information on the number of women involved in agriculture, their access to land and other productive resources (crop land, credits, inputs, techniques, technologies and means of work, including production, storage, conservation and processing equipment, low market access, poor organization and marketing of agricultural products) as well as their participation in value chains, particularly in the juice processing, labeling and marketing segments of agricultural products.

Finally, in most development sectors, actions to establish, establish and sustain gender equality are not sufficiently developed at all levels of Guinean society so that appropriate solutions are made to the causes of gender inequalities. This requires a comprehensive approach and gender analysis as an instrument for reducing the gap between women's inequalities so that as few Guineans as possible contribute to remain indifferent to the discrimination and inequality.
